Non-Deterministic Functions as Non-Deterministic Processes
Joseph W. N. Paulus ORCID:0000-0002-1711-9361, Daniele Nantes-Sobrinho ORCID:0000-0002-1959-8730, Jorge A. Pérez ORCID:0000-0002-1452-6180

Abstract
. We study encodings of the λ \lambda -calculus into the π \pi -calculus in the unexplored case of calculi with non-determinism and failures . On the sequential side, we consider λ ⊕ ↯ \lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus} , a new non-deterministic calculus in which intersection types control resources (terms); on the concurrent side, we consider 𝗌 ​ π \mathsf{s}\pi , a π \pi -calculus in which non-determinism and failure rest upon a Curry-Howard correspondence between linear logic and session types. We present a typed encoding of λ ⊕ ↯ \lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus} into 𝗌 ​ π \mathsf{s}\pi and establish its correctness. Our encoding precisely explains the interplay of non-deterministic and fail-prone evaluation in λ ⊕ ↯ \lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus} via typed processes in 𝗌 ​ π \mathsf{s}\pi . In particular, it shows how failures in sequential evaluation (absence/excess of resources) can be neatly codified as interaction protocols.

Volume 19, Issue 4, Paper 1

Introduction

Milner’s seminal work on encodings of the
\lambda
-calculus into the
\pi
-calculus
[
Mil92
]
explains how
interaction
in
\pi
subsumes
evaluation
in
\lambda
.
It opened a research strand on formal connections between sequential and concurrent calculi, covering untyped and typed regimes (see, e.g.,
[
San99
,
BL00
,
BHY03
,
TCP12
,
HYB14
,
OY16
,
TY18
]
).
This paper extends this line of work by tackling a hitherto unexplored angle, namely encodability of calculi in which computation is
non-deterministic
and may be subject to
failures
—two relevant features in sequential and concurrent programming models.

We focus on
typed
calculi and study how non-determinism and failures interact with
resource-aware
computation.
In sequential calculi,
non-idempotent intersection types
offer one fruitful perspective at resource-awareness (see, e.g.,
[
Gar94
,
Kfo00
,
KW04
,
NM04
,
BKV17
]
).
Because non-idempotency amounts to distinguish between types
\sigma
and
\sigma\land\sigma
, this class of intersection types can “count” different resources and enforce quantitative guarantees.
In concurrent calculi, resource-awareness has been much studied using
linear types
. Linearity ensures that process actions occur exactly once, which is key to enforce protocol correctness.
In particular,
session types

[
Hon93
,
HVK98
]

specify the protocols that channels must respect;
this typing discipline exploits linearity to
ensure absence of communication errors and stuck processes.
To our knowledge, connections between calculi adopting these two distinct views of resource-awareness via types are still to be established.
We aim to develop such connections by relating models of sequential and concurrent computation.

On the sequential side, we introduce
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
: a
\lambda
-calculus with resources, non-determinism, and failures, which
distills key elements from
\lambda
-calculi studied in
[
Bou93
,
PR10
]
.
Evaluation in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
considers
bags
of resources, and determines alternative executions governed by non-determinism.
Failure results from a lack or excess of resources (terms), and is captured by the term
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
, where
\widetilde{x}
denotes a sequence of variables.
Non-determinism in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is
non-collapsing
(i.e., confluent): intuitively, given
M
and
N
with reductions
M\longrightarrow M^{\prime}
and
N\longrightarrow N^{\prime}
, the non-deterministic sum
M+N
reduces to
M^{\prime}+N^{\prime}
.
In contrast, under a
collapsing
(i.e., non-confluent) approach, as in, e.g.,
[
DdP93
]
, the non-deterministic sum
M+N
reduces to either
M
or
N
.

On the concurrent side, we consider
\mathsf{s}\pi
: a session-typed
\pi
-calculus with (non-collapsing) non-determinism and failure, proposed in
[
CP17
]
.

\mathsf{s}\pi
rests upon a Curry-Howard correspondence between session types and (classical) linear logic, extended with modalities that express
non-deterministic protocols
that may succeed or fail. Non-determinism in
\mathsf{s}\pi
is non-collapsing, which ensures confluent process reductions.

Contributions

This paper presents the first formal connection between a
\lambda
-calculus with non-idempotent intersection types and a
\pi
-calculus with session types. Specifically, the paper presents the following contributions:

(1)

The resource calculus
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, a new calculus that distills the distinctive elements from previous resource calculi
[
BL00
,
PR10
]
, while offering an explicit treatment of failures in a setting with non-collapsing non-determinism.

We develop the syntax, semantics, and essential meta-theoretical results for
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
. In particular,
using intersection types, we define
well-typed
(fail-free) expressions and
well-formed
(fail-prone) expressions in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and establish their properties.

(2)

An encoding of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
, proven correct following established criteria in the realm of relative expressiveness for concurrency
[
Gor10
,
KPY19
]
. These criteria attest to an encoding’s quality; we consider

type preservation
,
operational correspondence
(including  completeness and
soundness
),
success sensitiveness
, and
compositionality
.

Thanks to these correctness properties, our encoding precisely describes how typed interaction protocols (given by session types) can codify sequential evaluation in which absence and excess of resources leads to failures (as governed by intersection types).

These contributions entail different challenges.
The first is bridging the different mechanisms for resource-awareness involved (i.e., intersection types in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, session types in
\mathsf{s}\pi
).
A direct encoding of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
is far from obvious, as multiple occurrences of a variable in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
must be accommodated into the linear setting of
\mathsf{s}\pi
.
To overcome this challenge,
we introduce a variant of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, dubbed
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.
The distinctive feature of
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is a

sharing
construct, which we adopt following the
atomic

\lambda
-calculus presented in
[
GHP13
]
.

Our encoding of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expressions into
\mathsf{s}\pi
processes is then in two steps.
We first define a correct encoding from
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, which relies on the sharing construct to “atomize” occurrences of the same variable.
Then, we define another correct encoding, from
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to
\mathsf{s}\pi
, which extends Milner’s with constructs for non-determinism.

Another challenge is framing failures in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
(undesirable computations) as well-typed
\mathsf{s}\pi
processes.
Using intersection types, we define
well-formed

\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expressions, which can fail, in two stages.
First, we consider
\lambda_{\oplus}
, the sub-language of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
without
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
.
We give an intersection type system for
\lambda_{\oplus}
to regulate fail-free evaluation.
Well-formed expressions are then defined on top of well-typed
\lambda_{\oplus}
expressions.
We show that
\mathsf{s}\pi
can correctly encode the fail-free
\lambda_{\oplus}
but, more interestingly, also well-formed
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expressions, which are fail-prone.

Fig.
1
summarizes our approach: the encoding
from
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is denoted

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
, whereas the encoding from
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to
\mathsf{s}\pi
is denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
.

Organization

Next,
\lx@sectionsign

1
informally discusses key ideas in our work.

\lx@sectionsign

2
introduces the syntax and semantics of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, and defines its intersection type system.

\lx@sectionsign

3
introduces
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, the variant of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
with sharing. It also presents its associated intersection type system, and defines an encoding from
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.
In
\lx@sectionsign

4
we summarize the syntax, semantics, and session type system of
\mathsf{s}\pi
, following
[
CP17
]
.

\lx@sectionsign

5
establishes the correctness of the encoding of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and presents and proves correct the encoding of
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
.
\lx@sectionsign

6
presents comparisons with related works.

\lx@sectionsign

7
closes with a discussion about our approach and results.

This paper is an extended and revised version of the conference paper
[
PNP21a
]
. Here we have included full technical details, additional examples, and extended explanations. For the sake of readability, and to make the paper self-contained, we have included proof sketches in the main text; their corresponding full proofs have been collected in the appendices.

Figure 1.
Overview of our approach.

1.
Overview of Key Ideas

Before embarking into our technical developments, we discuss some key ideas in the definition of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and its correct encodability into
\mathsf{s}\pi
.

Non-determinism.

Our source language
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
has three syntactic categories: terms (
M,M^{\prime}
), bags (
B,B^{\prime}
) and expressions (
\mathbb{M},\mathbb{L}
). Terms can be variables, abstractions
\lambda x.M
,
applications
(M\ B)
, explicit substitutions
M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
, or the explicit failure term
\mathtt{fail}
(see below).
Bags are multisets of terms (the resources); this way, e.g.,
B=\Lbag M_{1},M_{1},M_{2}\Rbag
is a bag with
three resources (
M_{1}
,
M_{1}
, and
M_{2}
). Expressions are sums of terms, written
M_{1}+M_{2}
; they denote a non-deterministic choice between different ways of
fetching
resources from the bag.

In
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, reduction is lazy: first, a
\beta
-reduction evolves to an explicit substitution, which will then fetch the elements in the bag to be
substituted for the corresponding variable, when some conditions are satisfied: we interpret this as “consuming a resource”.
For instance,
given a
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term
M

with head variable
x
and two occurrences of
x
, we have the reduction:

\displaystyle\lambda x.M\Lbag M_{1},M_{2}\Rbag

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag M_{1},M_{2}\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

(1)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle M{\{\!|}M_{1}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag M_{2}\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle+M{\{\!|}M_{2}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag M_{1}\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle=M^{\prime}

The resulting expression
M^{\prime}
is a sum that gathers two alternative computations: it may reduce by either (i) first fetching
M_{1}
from the bag and linearly substituting it for
x
in the head position of
M
(this is denoted with
M{\{\!|}M_{1}/x{|\!\}}
) and then continue with the rest of the bag (
M_{2}
, wrapped in an explicit substitution), or (ii) fetching and
linearly substituting
M_{2}
in head position, leaving
M_{1}
in an explicit substitution.

Successful Reductions

We consider a computation as successful only when the number of elements in the bag
matches the number of occurrences of the variable to be substituted; otherwise the computation fails.
As an example, consider the previous example, now with
M=x\Lbag x\Lbag I\Rbag\Rbag

where
I=\lambda z.z
is the identity. The reduction in (
1
) is then

(\lambda x.x\Lbag x\Lbag I\Rbag\Rbag)\Lbag M_{1},M_{2}\Rbag\longrightarrow^{*}M_{1}\Lbag x\Lbag I\Rbag\Rbag\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag M_{2}\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle+M_{2}\Lbag x\Lbag I\Rbag\Rbag\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag M_{1}\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

Hence, when
\lambda x.M
is applied to a bag with two resources, it evolves successfully.
However, if
\lambda x.M
is applied to a bag with less (or more) than two resources, the computation evolves to the
explicit failure
term
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{z}}
, where
\widetilde{z}
is a multiset of variables, as we explain next.

Explicit Failure.

A construct for failure is present in the resource
\lambda
-calculus in
[
PR10
]
.
In this formulation, the failure term ‘
0
’ is consumed by sums and disappears at the end of the computation; as such, it gives no information about the failed computation and its origins.

Following
[
PR10
]
, a design decision in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is to have
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
in the syntax of terms.
The sequence
\widetilde{x}
denotes the variables captured by failure; this provides useful information on the origins of a failure.
As an example, consider a term
M
with free variables
\widetilde{y}
and in which the number of occurrences of
x
is different from 2.
Given a bag
B=\Lbag M_{1},M_{2}\Rbag
, reduction leads to a failure, as follows:

(\lambda x.M)B\longrightarrow M\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag M_{1},M_{2}\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow{}\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{PER}(\Lbag M_{1},M_{2}\Rbag)}\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{y}}=M^{\prime}

In this case,
M^{\prime}
is the sum
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{y}}+\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{y}}
, which has as many summands as the permutations of the elements of
B
. Intuitively, it means that it does not matter if one replaces the occurrence(s) of
x
first with
M_{1}
(or
M_{2}
), then the other occurrence (if any), with
M_{2}
(or
M_{1}
), the result will be the same, i.e.,
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{y}}
. Here again both possibilities are expressed in a sum. The precise semantics of failure will be presented in
\lx@sectionsign

2.2
.

Typability and Well-formedness

We define an intersection type system for
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
. This choice follows a well-established tradition of coupling resource
\lambda
-calculi with intersection types
[
Bou93
,
BL96
,
PR10
]
. Intersection types are also adopted in related calculi
[
ER03
]
. Intersection types are a natural typing structure for resources: they have similar mathematical properties of non-idempotency and commutativity, and can help to “count” the number of occurrences of a variable in a term, as well as the number of components in a bag.

In our type systems, each element of a bag must have the same type.
This way, e.g., a well-typed bag
B=\Lbag M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}\Rbag
has type
\sigma\wedge\sigma\wedge\sigma
, where
\sigma
is a strict type (cf. Def.
2.3.1
). Then, an application
M\ B
is well-typed, say, with type
\tau
, only if
M:\sigma\wedge\sigma\wedge\sigma\to\tau
.
We shall write
\sigma^{k}
to denote the intersection type
\sigma\wedge\ldots\wedge\sigma
, with
k\geq 0
copies of
\sigma
.
Notice that
\sigma^{0}
denotes the empty type
\omega
.
The typing rule for application is then as expected:

\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma^{k}\to\tau

\Gamma\vdash B:\sigma^{k}

\mathtt{[T:app]}

\Gamma\vdash M\ B:\tau

where
\Gamma
is a type context assigning types to variables.

We chose to express explicit failing terms and computation.
To properly account for these computations, we define a separate type system with so-called
well-formedness
rules, with notation ‘
\models
’.
Unlike rules for typability, rules for well-formedness capture computations that fail due to a mismatch of resources (lack or excess).
This entails some increased flexibility in selected rules.
This way, e.g, the following is the well-formedness rule for application:

\Gamma\models M:\sigma^{j}\to\tau

\Gamma\models B:\sigma^{k}

\mathtt{[F:app]}

\Gamma\models M\ B:\tau

Here the added flexibility is that we do not require
k=j
; hence, the rule can capture successful
and
failing computations, depending on whether
k=j
or not. As expected, the term
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{z}}
is not well-typed, but it is well-formed: the judgement
\Gamma\models\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{z}}:\tau
holds for an arbitrary type
\tau
and a
\Gamma
consisting of variable assignments
for the variables in
\widetilde{z}
.

Therefore, we consider two intersection type systems: one captures exclusively successful computations (see Fig.
3
); the other, which we call the well-formedness system (see Fig.
4
), subsumes the first one by admitting both successful and failing computations.
The weakening rule is admissible in both systems (see below).
Both systems enjoy subject reduction, whereas only well-typed terms satisfy subject expansion.

Controlling resources via sharing

In order to better control the use of resources, i.e., substituting variables for terms with a careful form of duplication, we borrow ideas from the
sharing graphs
by Guerrini et al.
[
Gue99
,
GMM03
]
and define the calculus
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.
The key idea is as follows: whenever a bound variable
x
occurs multiple times within a term, these occurrences, say
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
, are temporarily assigned new names (think aliases). This assignment is indicated with the
sharing construct

[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\leftarrow x]
, which we adopt following
[
GHP13
]
.
This way, for instance, the
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term
\lambda x.x\Lbag x\Rbag
would correspond to
\lambda x.x_{1}\Lbag x_{2}\Rbag[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]
in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

We also carefully treat the “erasing” of resources: if a term has vacuous abstractions, this is also indicated with the sharing construct, where the bound variable maps to “empty”. Hence, the
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term
\lambda x.y\Lbag z\Rbag
is expressed as
\lambda x.y\Lbag z\Rbag[\leftarrow x]
in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.
The tight control of resources in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
turns out to be very convenient to encode
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
, as we discuss next.

Encoding
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
.

The central result of our work is a correct translation of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
.
In defining our translation we use
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
as a stepping stone.
This is advantageous, because (i) the relation between
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
and
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is fairly direct and (ii) the sharing construct in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
makes it explicit the variable occurrences that should be treated as linear names in
\mathsf{s}\pi
.

The encoding of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
and given in
\lx@sectionsign

3.4
.
The encoding of
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
and presented in
\lx@sectionsign

5.3.2
, is arguably more interesting—we discuss it below.

The definition of
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
considers well-formed source terms in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
which are translated into well-typed
\mathsf{s}\pi
processes.
As usual, the translation is parametric on a channel name
u
, which is used to provide the behavior of the source term.

The calculus
\mathsf{s}\pi
includes a non-deterministic choice operator
P\oplus Q
and formalizes sessions which are
non-deterministically available
. Intuitively, this means that a given session protocol along a name can either be available and proceed as prescribed by the corresponding session type, or fail to be available. Clearly, such a failure may have repercussions on other sessions that depend on it.
To this end,
\mathsf{s}\pi
includes prefixes
x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}
and
x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
, which are used to confirm the availability of
x
and to signal its failure, respectively.
Process
x.\mathtt{some}_{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{k})};Q
declares the dependency of sessions
w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}
in
Q
on an external session along
x
.
The corresponding reduction rules are then:

\displaystyle x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x.\mathtt{some}_{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{k})};Q

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x.\mathtt{some}_{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{k})};Q

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle w_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}\cdots\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}w_{k}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}

Following Milner,
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
maps computation in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into session communication in
\mathsf{s}\pi
; non-deterministic sessions are used to codify the non-deterministic fetching of resources in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.
This way, the translation of
(\lambda x.M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x])B
will enable synchronizations between the translations of
M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]
and
B
.
More in details, the translation of a bag
B=\Lbag M_{1},M_{2}\Rbag
is as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Lbag M_{1}\Rbag\cdot\Lbag M_{2}\Rbag{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x.\mathtt{some}_{\widetilde{z_{1}},\widetilde{z_{2}}};x(y_{i}).x.\mathtt{some}_{y_{i},\widetilde{z_{1}},\widetilde{z_{2}}};x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};

\displaystyle\quad\overline{x}(x_{i}).(x_{i}.\mathtt{some}_{\widetilde{z_{1}}};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{i}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Lbag M_{2}\Rbag{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\mid y_{i}.\overline{\mathtt{none}})

where
\widetilde{z_{1}}
and
\widetilde{z_{2}}
denote the free variables of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, respectively.
Process
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Lbag M_{1}\Rbag\cdot\Lbag M_{2}\Rbag{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}
first expects confirmation of session
x
; then, the translation of each resource
M_{i}
is made available in a dedicated name
x_{i}
, which will be communicated to other processes.
Accordingly, the translation of
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
is expected to synchronize with the translation of the bag
B
: indeed, it confirms behavior along
x
, before receiving the names, one for each shared copy of
x
that should be used throughout the synchronizations:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle=x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}.\overline{x}(y_{1}).\Big(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{1}.\mathtt{close}\!\mid\!x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x.\mathtt{some}_{u,(\mathsf{fv}(M)\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2}\})};x(x_{1}).

\displaystyle.x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}.\overline{x}(y_{2}).\big(y_{2}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{2}.\mathtt{close}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x.\mathtt{some}_{u,(\mathsf{fv}(M)\setminus\{x_{2}\})};x(x_{2})

\displaystyle.x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(y).(y.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(M)};y.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})~\big)\Big)

Several confirmations take place along the channel names involved in the synchronizations; see
\lx@sectionsign

5.3.2
for details.

Non-determinism plays a key role in the translation of an application
M^{\prime}B
. In this case, we consider the permutations of the elements of
B
using non-deterministic choice in
\mathsf{s}\pi
.
When
B=\Lbag M_{1},M_{2}\Rbag
, the translation is:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M^{\prime}\,B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=

\displaystyle(\nu v)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Lbag M_{1},M_{2}\Rbag{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}))

\displaystyle\oplus

\displaystyle(\nu v)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Lbag M_{2},M_{1}\Rbag{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}))

A synchronization occurs when process
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}
can confirm its behavior along
v
. For instance, when
M^{\prime}=\lambda x.M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]
the translation is as

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\lambda x.M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}=v.\overline{\mathtt{some}};v(x).{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M[x_{2},x_{2}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}

and the synchronization may be possible; it depends on the translations of
M
,
M_{1}
, and
M_{2}
.

We close this section by observing that our translations
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
satisfy well-known correctness criteria, as formulated by Gorla
[
Gor10
]
and Kouzapas et al.
[
KPY19
]
(see
\lx@sectionsign

5.1
for details).

2.
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
: A
\lambda
-calculus with Non-Determinism and Failure

We define the syntax and reduction semantics of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, our new resource calculus with non-determinism and failure. We then equip it with non-idempotent session types, and establish the subject reduction property for well-typed and well-formed expressions (Theorems
3
and
7
, respectively).
We also consider the subject expansion property, which holds for well-typed expressions (Theorem
5
) but not for well-formed ones (Theorem
8
).

2.1.
Syntax

The syntax of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
combines elements from calculi introduced and studied by Boudol and Laneve
[
BL00
]
and by Pagani and Ronchi della Rocca
[
PR10
]
.
We use
x,y,\ldots
to range over the set of
variables
.
We write
\widetilde{x}
to denote the sequence of pairwise distinct variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
, for some
k\geq 0
.
We write
|\widetilde{x}|
to denote the length of
\widetilde{x}
.

{defi}

[Syntax of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
]

The
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
calculus is defined by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{10mm}}lll}\mbox{(Terms)}\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&M,N,L&::=&x\ |\ \lambda x.M\ |\ (M\ B)\ |\ M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\ |\ \mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}\\
\mbox{(Bags)}\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&A,B&::=&\mathtt{1}\ |\ \Lbag M\Rbag\ |\ A\cdot B\\
\mbox{(Expressions)}\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&\mathbb{M},\mathbb{N},\mathbb{L}&::=&M\ |\ \mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N}\\
\end{array}

We have three syntactic categories:
terms
(in functional position);
bags
(in argument position), which denote multisets of resources; and
expressions
, which are finite formal sums that represent possible results of a computation.
Terms are unary expressions: they can be variables, abstractions, and applications.
Following
[
Bou93
,
BL00
]
, the
explicit substitution
of a bag
B
for a variable
x
in a term
M
, written
M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
, is also a term.
The term
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
results from a reduction in which there is a lack or excess of resources to be substituted, where
\widetilde{x}
denotes a multiset of free variables that are encapsulated within failure.

The empty bag is denoted
\mathtt{1}
.
The bag enclosing the term
M
is
\Lbag M\Rbag
.
The concatenation of bags
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
is denoted as
B_{1}\cdot B_{2}
; the concatenation operator ‘
\cdot
’ is associative and commutative, with
\mathtt{1}
as its identity.
To ease readability, we rely on a shorthand notation for bags: we often write
\Lbag N_{1},N_{2}\Rbag
rather than
\Lbag N_{1}\Rbag\cdot\Lbag N_{2}\Rbag
.

We treat expressions as
sums
, and use notations such as
\sum_{i}^{n}N_{i}
for them. Sums are associative and commutative; reordering of the terms in a sum is performed silently.

{exa}

We give some examples of terms and expressions in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
:

•

M_{1}=(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag

•

M_{2}=(\lambda x.x)(\Lbag y,z\Rbag)

•

M_{3}=(\lambda x.x)\mathtt{1}

•

M_{4}=(\lambda x.y)\mathtt{1}

•

M_{5}=\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}

•

M_{6}=(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag+(\lambda x.x)\Lbag z\Rbag

Terms
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
, and
M_{3}
illustrate the application of the identity function
I=\lambda x.x
to bags with different formats: a bag with one component, two components, and the empty bag, respectively. Special attention should be given to the fact that the
x
has only one occurrence in
I
, whereas the bags contain zero or more components (resources). This way:

•

M_{1}
represents a term with a
correct
number of resources;

•

M_{2}
denotes a term with an
excess
of resources; and

•

M_{3}
denotes a term with a
lack
of resources

This resource interpretation will become clearer once the reduction semantics is introduced in the next subsection (cf. Example
2.2
).

Term
M_{4}
denotes the application of a vacuous abstraction on
x
to the empty bag
\mathtt{1}
.
Term
M_{5}
denotes a failure term with no associated variables.
Expression
M_{6}
denotes the non-deterministic sum between two terms, each of which denotes an application of
I
to a bag containing one element.

{nota}

[Expressions]
Notation
N\in\mathbb{M}
denotes that

N
is part of the sum denoted by
\mathbb{M}
.
Similarly, we write
N_{i}\in B
to denote that
N_{i}
occurs in the bag
B
, and
B\setminus\!\!\setminus N_{i}
to denote the bag that is obtained by removing one occurrence of the term
N_{i}
from
B
.

2.2.
Reduction Semantics

Reduction in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is defined in terms of the relation
\longrightarrow
, defined in Fig.
2
; it operates lazily on expressions, and will be described after introducing some auxiliary notions.

{nota}

We write
\mathsf{PER}(B)
to denote the set of all permutations of bag
B
.
Also,
B_{i}(n)
denotes the
n
-th term in the (permuted)
B_{i}
.
We define
\mathsf{size}(B)
to denote the number of terms in bag
B
.
That is,
\mathsf{size}(\mathtt{1})=0

and

\mathsf{size}(\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B)=1+\mathsf{size}(B)
.

{defi}

[Set and Multiset of Free Variables]

The set of free variables of a term, bag, and expression, is defined as

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{1cm}}l}\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{-.01mm}}l}\mathsf{fv}(x)\hskip-0.02843pt&=\{x\}\\
\mathsf{fv}(\lambda x.M)\hskip-0.02843pt&=\mathsf{fv}(M)\!\setminus\!\{x\}\\
\mathsf{fv}(M\ B)\hskip-0.02843pt&=\mathsf{fv}(M)\cup\mathsf{fv}(B)\\
\mathsf{fv}(M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)\hskip-0.02843pt&=(\mathsf{fv}(M)\setminus\{x\})\cup\mathsf{fv}(B)\end{array}\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{-.01mm}}l}\mathsf{fv}(\mathtt{1})\hskip-0.02843pt&=\emptyset\\
\mathsf{fv}(\Lbag M\Rbag)\hskip-0.02843pt&=\mathsf{fv}(M)\\
\mathsf{fv}(B_{1}\cdot B_{2})\hskip-0.02843pt&=\mathsf{fv}(B_{1})\cup\mathsf{fv}(B_{2})\\
\mathsf{fv}(\mathtt{fail}^{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}})\hskip-0.02843pt&=\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\}\\
\mathsf{fv}(\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N})\hskip-0.02843pt&=\mathsf{fv}(\mathbb{M})\cup\mathsf{fv}(\mathbb{N})\end{array}\end{array}

We use
\mathsf{mfv}(M)
or
\mathsf{mfv}(B)
to denote a multiset of free variables, defined similarly.
We sometimes treat the sequence
\widetilde{x}
as a (multi)set.
We write
\widetilde{x}\uplus\widetilde{y}
to denote the multiset union of
\widetilde{x}
and
\widetilde{y}
and
\widetilde{x}\setminus y
to express that every occurrence of
y
is removed from
\widetilde{x}
.
A term
M
is
closed
if
\mathsf{fv}(M)=\emptyset
(and similarly for expressions). As usual, we shall consider
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-terms modulo
\alpha
-equivalence.

{nota}

\#(x,M)
denotes the number of (free) occurrences of
x
in
M
.
Similarly, we write
\#(x,\widetilde{y})
to denote the number of occurrences of
x
in the multiset
\widetilde{y}
.

{defi}

[Head]

Given a term
M
, we define
\mathsf{head}(M)
inductively as:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{1cm}}l}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{head}(x)=x\\
\mathsf{head}(\lambda x.M)=\lambda x.M\\
\mathsf{head}(M\ B)=\mathsf{head}(M)\end{array}\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{head}(\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}})=\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}\\
\mathsf{head}(M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)=\begin{cases}\mathsf{head}(M)&\text{if $\#(x,M)=\mathsf{size}(B)$}\\
\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\end{array}\end{array}

{defi}

[Linear Head Substitution]

Let
M
be a term such that
\mathsf{head}(M)=x
.
The
linear head substitution
of a term
N
for
x
in
M
, denoted
M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}
, is defined as:

\displaystyle x{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}

\displaystyle=N

\displaystyle(M\ B){\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}

\displaystyle=(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})\ B

\displaystyle(M\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle){\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}

\displaystyle=(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\qquad\text{where }x\not=y

Finally, we define contexts for terms and expressions and convenient notations:

{defi}

[Term and Expression Contexts]
Contexts for terms (CTerm) and expressions (CExpr) are defined by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\hspace{1cm}}c}\text{(CTerm)}\quad C[\cdot],C^{\prime}[\cdot]::=([\cdot])B\mid([\cdot])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&\text{(CExpr)}\quad D[\cdot],D^{\prime}[\cdot]::=M+[\cdot]\mid[\cdot]+M\end{array}

\mathtt{[R:Beta]}

(\lambda x.M)B\longrightarrow M\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\mathsf{head}(M)=x

B=\Lbag N_{1},\dots,N_{k}\Rbag\ ,\ k\geq 1

\#(x,M)=k

\mathtt{[R:Fetch]}

M\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow M{\{\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$(B\setminus\!\!\setminus N_{1})$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle+\cdots+M{\{\!|}N_{k}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$(B\setminus\!\!\setminus N_{k})$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\#(x,M)\neq\mathsf{size}(B)\qquad\widetilde{y}=(\mathsf{mfv}(M)\setminus x)\uplus\mathsf{mfv}(B)

\mathtt{[R:Fail]}

M\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow{}\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{PER}(B)}\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{y}}

\widetilde{y}=\mathsf{mfv}(B)

\mathtt{[R:Cons_{1}]}

\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}\ B\longrightarrow{}\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{PER}(B)}\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}\uplus\widetilde{y}}

\mathsf{size}(B)=k\qquad\#(z,\widetilde{x})+k\not=0\qquad\widetilde{y}=\mathsf{mfv}(B)

\mathtt{[R:Cons_{2}]}

\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$z$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow{}\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{PER}(B)}\mathtt{fail}^{(\widetilde{x}\setminus z)\uplus\widetilde{y}}

M\longrightarrow M^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+M^{\prime}_{k}

\mathtt{[R:TCont]}

C[M]\longrightarrow C[M^{\prime}_{1}]+\cdots+C[M^{\prime}_{k}]

\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}^{\prime}

\mathtt{[R:ECont]}

D[\mathbb{M}]\longrightarrow D[\mathbb{M}^{\prime}]

Figure 2.
Reduction Rules for
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}

The reduction relation on
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is defined by the rules in
Fig.
2
. Intuitively, reductions in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
work as follows: A
\beta
-reduction induces an explicit substitution of a bag
B
for a variable
x
in a term
M
, denoted
M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
. In the case the head of the term
M
is
x
and the size of the bag
B
coincides with the number of occurrences of
x
in
M
, this explicit substitution is expanded into a sum of terms, each of which features a
linear head substitution

M{\{\!|}N_{i}/x{|\!\}}
, where
N_{i}
is a term in
B
, which will replace the variable
x
occurring in the head of
M
; the rest of the bag (
B\setminus\!\!\setminus N_{i}
) is kept in an explicit substitution. However, if there is a mismatch between the number of occurrences of the variable to be substituted and the number of resources available, then the reduction leads to the failure term. Formally,

•

Rule
\mathtt{[R:Beta]}
is standard and admits a bag (possibly empty) as parameter.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[R:Fetch]}
transforms a term into an expression: it opens up an explicit substitution into a sum of terms with linear head substitutions, each denoting the partial evaluation of an element from the bag, considering all the possible choices for substituting an element
N_{i}
of the bag for
x
.
Hence, the size of the bag will determine the number of summands in the resulting expression.

There are three rules reduce to the failure term: their objective is to accumulate all (free) variables involved in failed reductions.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[R:Fail]}
formalizes failure in the evaluation of an explicit substitution
M\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
, which occurs if there is a mismatch between the resources (terms) present in
B
and the number of occurrences of
x
to be substituted.
The resulting failure preserves all free variables in
M
and
B
within its attached multiset
\widetilde{y}
, and all possible computations that could have failed, via permutation of the bags, are captured in a non-deterministic sum.

•

Rules
\mathtt{[R:Cons_{1}]}
and
\mathtt{[R:Cons_{2}]}
describe reductions that lazily consume the failure term, when a term has
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
at its head position.
The former rule consumes bags attached to it whilst preserving all its free variables.
The latter rule is similar but for the case of explicit substitutions; its second premise ensures that either (i) the bag in the substitution is not empty or (ii) the number of occurrences of
z
in the current multiset of accumulated variables is not zero.

Notice that our Rule
\mathtt{[R:Fail]}
rule evolves to a sum of failure terms, where each summand accounts for a permutation of the elements of the bag. As our reduction strategy fails eagerly this may not be evident at first; however, there is still a non-deterministic choice of elements in
B
that are waiting to be substituted at the point of failure (see Example
2.2
).

Finally, we describe the contextual rules:

•

Rule
\mathtt{[R:TCont]}
describes the reduction of sub-terms within an expression; in this rule, summations are expanded outside of term contexts.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[R:ECont]}
says that reduction of expressions is closed by expression contexts.

{nota}

As standard,

\longrightarrow
denotes one step reduction;

\longrightarrow^{+}
and
\longrightarrow^{*}
denote the transitive and the reflexive-transitive closure of
\longrightarrow
, respectively.
We write
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R]}}\mathbb{M}
to denote that
\mathtt{[R]}
is the last (non-contextual) rule used in inferring the step from
\mathbb{N}
to
\mathbb{M}
.

{exa}

[Cont. Example
2.1
]

We show how the terms in Example
2.1
can
reduce:

•

Reduction of the term
M_{1}
with an adequate number of resources:

\displaystyle(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R:Beta]}}x\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag y\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R:Fetch]}}y\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\text{ since }\#(x,x)=\mathsf{size}(\Lbag y\Rbag)=1

•

Reduction of term
M_{2}
with excess of resources:

\displaystyle(\lambda x.x)(\Lbag y,z\Rbag)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R:Beta]}}x\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$(\Lbag y,z\Rbag)$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R:Fail]}}\mathtt{fail}^{y,z}+\mathtt{fail}^{y,z},\ \text{ since }\#(x,x)=1\neq\mathsf{size}(\Lbag y,z\Rbag)=2

•

Reduction of term
M_{3}
with lack of resources:

\displaystyle(\lambda x.x)\mathtt{1}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R:Beta]}}x\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R:Fail]}}\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset},\ \text{ since }\#(x,x)=1\neq\mathsf{size}(\mathtt{1})=0

•

Reduction of term
M_{4}
which is a vacuous abstraction applied to an empty bag:

\displaystyle(\lambda x.y)\mathtt{1}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R:Beta]}}y\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

•

M_{5}=\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}
is unable to perform any reductions, i.e., it is irreducible.

•

Reductions of the expression
M_{6}=(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag+(\lambda x.x)\Lbag z\Rbag
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag y\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle+(\lambda x.x)\Lbag z\Rbag}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag+(\lambda x.x)\Lbag z\Rbag}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag y\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle+x\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag z\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag+x\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag z\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle}

The following example illustrates the use of
\mathsf{PER}(B)
in Rule
\mathtt{[R:Fail]}
: independently of the order in which the resources in the bag are used, the computation fails.

{exa}

Let
M=(\lambda x.x\Lbag x\Lbag y\Rbag\Rbag)\ B
, with

B=\Lbag z_{1},z_{2},z_{1}\Rbag
.
We have:

\displaystyle M\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R:Beta]}}

\displaystyle x\Lbag x\Lbag y\Rbag\Rbag\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag z_{1},z_{2},z_{1}\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R:Fail]}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{PER}(B)}\mathtt{fail}^{y,z_{1},z_{2},z_{1}}

The number of occurrences of
x
in the term
obtained after
\beta
-reduction (2) does not match the size of the bag (3). Therefore, the reduction leads to failure.
Notice that

\sum_{\mathsf{PER}(B)}\mathtt{fail}^{y,z_{1},z_{2},z_{1}}

expands to a sum between six instances of

\mathtt{fail}^{y,z_{1},z_{2},z_{1}}
,
corresponding to permutation of 3 elements of the bag
B
.

Notice that the left-hand sides of the reduction rules in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
do not interfere with each other.
Therefore, reduction in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
satisfies a
diamond property
:

Proposition
\thethm

(Diamond Property for
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)
.

For all
\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{N}_{1}
,
\mathbb{N}_{2}
in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
s.t.
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{1}
,
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{2}
with
\mathbb{N}_{1}\neq\mathbb{N}_{2}
then there exists
\mathbb{M}
such that
\mathbb{N}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}
,
\mathbb{N}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}
.

Proof (Sketch).

By inspecting the rules of Fig.
2
one can check that the left-hand sides only clash in a non-variable position with Rules
\mathtt{[R:Fail]}
and
\mathtt{[R:Cons2]}
. The clash does not generate a critical pair: in fact, when applied to the
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term
\mathtt{fail}^{z,\widetilde{x}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$z$}\rangle\!\rangle
both rules reduce to
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
.
For all the other rules, whenever they have the same shape, the side conditions of the rules determine which rule can be applied. Therefore,
an expression can only perform a choice of reduction steps when it is a sum of terms in which multiple summands can perform independent reductions. Without loss of generality, consider an expression
\mathbb{N}=N+M
such that
N\longrightarrow N^{\prime}
and
M\longrightarrow M^{\prime}
. Then we let
\mathbb{N}_{1}=N^{\prime}+M
and
\mathbb{N}_{2}=N+M^{\prime}
by Rule
\mathtt{[R:ECont]}
. The result follows for
\mathbb{M}=N^{\prime}+M^{\prime}
, since
\mathbb{N}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{N}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}
.
∎

Remark 1

(A Sub-calculus without Failure (
\lambda_{\oplus}
))
.

We find it convenient to define
\lambda_{\oplus}
, the sub-calculus of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
without explicit failure.
The syntax of
\lambda_{\oplus}
is obtained from Definition
2.1
by excluding
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
from the syntax of terms.
Accordingly, the reduction relation for
\lambda_{\oplus}
is given by Rules
\mathtt{[R:Beta]}
,
\mathtt{[R:Fetch]}
,
\mathtt{[R:ECont]}
, and
\mathtt{[R:TCont]}
in Fig.
2
.
Finally, Definition
2.2
is kept unchanged with the provision that
\mathsf{head}(M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)
is undefined when
\#(x,M)\not=\mathsf{size}(B)
.

2.3.
Well-formed
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-Expressions

As mentioned in
\lx@sectionsign

1
, we define a notion of
well-formed expressions
for
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
by relying on a non-idempotent intersection type system, similar to the one given by Pagani and Della Rocca in
[
PR10
]
. Our system for well-formed expressions will be defined in two stages:

(1)

First we define a intersection type system for the sub-language
\lambda_{\oplus}
(cf. Rem.
1
), given in Fig.
3
.
Unlike the system in
[
PR10
]
, our type system includes a weakening rule and a rule for typing explicit substitutions.

(2)

Second, we define well-formed expressions for the full language
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, via Def.
2.3.2
.

We say that we check for “well-formedness” (of terms, bags, and expressions) to stress that, unlike standard type systems, our system is able to account for terms that may reduce to the failure term.

2.3.1.
Intersection Types

Intersection types allow us to reason about types of resources in bags but also about every occurrence of a variable.
That is, non-idempotent intersection types enable us to distinguish expressions not only by measuring the size of a bag but also by counting the number of times a variable occurs within a term.

{defi}
[Types for
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
]

We define
strict
and
multiset types
by the grammar:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\hspace{1.2cm}}c}\text{(Strict)}\quad\sigma,\tau,\delta::=\mathbf{unit}\ |\ \pi\rightarrow\sigma\hfil\hskip 34.14322pt&\text{(Multiset)}\quad\pi::=\sigma^{k}\ |\ \omega\end{array}

where
\sigma^{k}
stands
for
\sigma\wedge\cdots\wedge\sigma

(
k
times, for some
k>0
).
A strict type can be the unit type
\mathbf{unit}
or a functional type
\pi\rightarrow\sigma
, where

\pi
is a multiset type and
\sigma
is a strict type.
Multiset types can be either
an intersection of strict types

\sigma^{k}

(if
k>0
)
or the empty type
\omega
, which would correspond to
\sigma^{k}
with
k=0
. Hence,
\sigma^{k}
denotes an intersection;
the operator
\wedge
is commutative, associative, and non-idempotent, that is,
\sigma\wedge\sigma\neq\sigma
. The empty type is the type of the empty bag; it acts as the identity element to
\wedge
.

{defi}

Type contexts

\Gamma,\Delta,\ldots
are sets of type assignments
x:\pi
, as defined by the grammar:

\displaystyle\Gamma,\Delta

\displaystyle=\text{-}\ |\ \Gamma,x:\pi

The set of variables in
\Gamma
is denoted as
\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)
.
In writing
\Gamma,x:\pi
we assume that
x\not\in\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)
.

We generalize the operator
\wedge
from types to contexts, and define
\Gamma\wedge\Delta
as follows:

(\Gamma_{1}\wedge\Gamma_{2})(x)=\begin{cases}x:\pi_{1}\wedge\pi_{2}&x:\pi_{i}\in\Gamma_{i},\ \pi_{i}\not=\omega,\ i\in\{1,2\}\\
x:\pi_{i}&x:\pi_{i}\in\Gamma_{i},x\not\in\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma_{j}),\ i\not=j,\ i,j\in\{1,2\}\\
\text{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Type judgements
are of the form
\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}:\sigma
, where
\Gamma
is a type context. We write
\vdash\mathbb{M}:\sigma
to denote
\text{-}\vdash\mathbb{M}:\sigma
.

{defi}

(Well-typed Expressions)
An expression
\mathbb{M}\in\lambda_{\oplus}
is
well-typed
(or typable) if there exist
\Gamma
and
\tau
such that
\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}:\tau
is entailed via the rules in Fig.
3
.

\mathtt{[T:var]}

x:\sigma\vdash x:\sigma

\mathtt{[T:\mathtt{1}]}

\vdash\mathtt{1}:\omega

\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma

\mathtt{[T:weak]}

\Gamma,x:\omega\vdash M:\sigma

\Gamma,{x}:\sigma^{k}\vdash M:\tau

\mathtt{[T:abs]}

\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.M:\sigma^{k}\rightarrow\tau

\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma

\Delta\vdash B:\sigma^{k}

\mathtt{[T:bag]}

\Gamma\wedge\Delta\vdash\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B:\sigma^{k+1}

\Gamma\vdash M:\pi\rightarrow\tau

\Delta\vdash B:\pi

\mathtt{[T:app]}

\Gamma\wedge\Delta\vdash M\ B:\tau

\Gamma,{x}:\sigma^{k}\vdash M:\tau

\Delta\vdash B:\sigma^{k}

\mathtt{[T:ex\text{-}sub]}

\Gamma\wedge\Delta\vdash M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}:\sigma

\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{N}:\sigma

\mathtt{[T:sum]}

\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N}:\sigma

Figure 3.
Typing Rules for
\lambda_{\oplus}

The rules are standard. We only consider intersections of the same strict type, say
\sigma
, since the current objective is to count the number of occurrences of a variable in a term, and measure the size of a bag. We now give a brief description of the rules in Fig.
3
:

•

Rules
\mathtt{[T{:}var]}
,
\mathtt{[T{:}\mathtt{1}]}
and
\mathtt{[T{:}weak]}
are as expected: the first assigns a type to a variable, the second
assigns the empty bag
\mathtt{1}
the empty type
\omega
, and the third introduces a useful weakening principle.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[T:abs]}
types an abstraction
\lambda x.M
with
\sigma^{k}\to\tau
, as long as the variable assignment
x:\sigma^{k}
has an intersection type with
\sigma
occurring exactly
k
times.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[T:bag]}
types a bag
B
with a type
\sigma^{k+1}
as long as every component of
B
is typed with same type
\sigma
, a defined amount of times.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[T{:}app]}
types an application
M\ B
with
\tau
as long as
M
and
B
match on the multiset type
\pi
, i.e.,
M:\pi\to\tau
and
B:\pi
. Intuitively, this means that
M
expects a fixed amount of resources, and
B
has exactly this number of resources.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[T{:}ex\text{-}sub]}
types an explicit substitution
M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
with
\tau
as long as the bag
B
consists of elements of the same type as
x
and the size of
B
matches the number of times
x
occurs in
M
, i.e.,
B:\sigma^{k}
and
x:\sigma^{k}
types the assignment of
M:\tau
.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[T:sum]}
types an expression (a sum) with a type
\sigma
, if each summand has type
\sigma
.

Notice that with the typing rules for
\lambda_{\oplus}
the failure term
\mathtt{fail}
cannot be typed. We could consider this set of rules as a type system for
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, i.e. the extension of
\lambda_{\oplus}
with failure, in which failure can be expressed but not typed.

{exa}

[Cont. Example
2.2
]

We explore the typability of some of the terms given in previous examples:

(1)

Term
M_{1}=(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag
is typable, as we have:

\mathtt{[T:var]}

x:\sigma\vdash x:\sigma

\mathtt{[T:abs]}

\vdash\lambda x.x:\sigma\rightarrow\sigma

\mathtt{[T:var]}

y:\sigma\vdash y:\sigma

\mathtt{[T:\mathtt{1}]}

\vdash\mathtt{1}:\omega

\mathtt{[T:bag]}

y:\sigma\vdash\Lbag y\Rbag\cdot\mathtt{1}:\sigma

\mathtt{[T:app]}

y:\sigma\vdash(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag:\sigma

(2)

Term
M_{2}=(\lambda x.x)(\Lbag y,z\Rbag)
is not typable.

•

The function
\lambda x.x
has a functional type
\sigma\to\sigma
;

•

The bag has an intersection type of size two:
y:\sigma,z:\sigma\vdash(\Lbag y,z\Rbag):\sigma^{2}
;

•

Rule
\mathtt{[T:app]}
requires a match between the type of the bag and the left of the arrow: it can only consume a bag of type
\sigma
.

(3)

Similarly,
M_{3}=(\lambda x.x)\mathtt{1}
is not typable: since
\lambda x.x
has type
\sigma\to\sigma
, to apply the Rule
\mathtt{[T:app]}
the bag must have a type
\sigma
, but the empty bag
\mathtt{1}
can only be typed with
\omega
.

(4)

Term
M_{4}=(\lambda x.y)\mathtt{1}
is typable, as follows:

\mathtt{[T:var]}

y:\sigma\vdash y:\sigma

\mathtt{[T:weak]}

y:\sigma,x:\omega\vdash y:\sigma

\mathtt{[T:abs]}

y:\sigma\vdash\lambda x.y:\omega\rightarrow\sigma

\mathtt{[T:\mathtt{1}]}

\vdash\mathtt{1}:\omega

\mathtt{[T:app]}

y:\sigma\vdash(\lambda x.y)\mathtt{1}:\sigma

Our typing system for
\lambda_{\oplus}
satisfies standard properties, such as subject reduction, which follows from the
Linear
Substitution Lemma. We stress ‘linearity’ because the lemma is stated in terms of the head linear substitution
{\{\!|}\cdot{|\!\}}
.

Lemma 2

(Linear Substitution Lemma for
\lambda_{\oplus}
)
.

If
\Gamma,x:\sigma^{k}\vdash M:\tau
(with
k\geq 1
),
\mathsf{head}(M)=x
, and
\Delta\vdash N:\sigma

then

\Gamma\wedge\Delta,x:\sigma^{k-1}\vdash M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}:\tau
.

Proof 2.1
.

Standard, by induction on the rule applied in
\Gamma,x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau
.

Theorem 3

(Subject Reduction for
\lambda_{\oplus}
)
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}:\tau
and
\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
then
\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}^{\prime}:\tau
.

Proof 2.2
.

By induction on the reduction rule (Fig.
2
) applied in
\mathbb{M}
.

Lemma 4

(Linear Anti-substitution Lemma for
\lambda_{\oplus}
)
.

Let
M
and
N
be
\lambda_{\oplus}
-terms such that
\mathsf{head}(M)=x
, then we have:

•

\Gamma,x:\sigma^{k-1}\vdash M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}:\tau
, with
k>1
, then there exist
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
such that
\Gamma_{1},x:\sigma^{k}\vdash M:\tau
, and
\Gamma_{2}\vdash N:\sigma
, where
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\wedge\Gamma_{2}
.

•

\Gamma\vdash M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}:\tau
, with
x\not\in\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)
, then there exist
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
such that
\Gamma_{1},x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau
, and
\Gamma_{2}\vdash N:\sigma
, where
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\wedge\Gamma_{2}
.

Proof 2.3
.

Standard, by structural induction.

Theorem 5

(Subject Expansion for
\lambda_{\oplus}
)
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}^{\prime}:\tau
and
\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
then
\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}:\tau
.

Proof 2.4
.

Standard, by structural induction. See App.
B
for details.

2.3.2.
Well-formed Expressions (in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)

Building upon the type system for
\lambda_{\oplus}
, we now define a type system for checking
well-formed

\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-expressions. This approach enables us to admit expressions with a failing computational behavior, may it be due to the mismatch in the number of resources required and available, or be due to consumption of a failing behavior by another expression.
Such definition relies on the
core context
which is the key to the well-formedness of failure terms: free variables that are result of weakening will disregarded in the typing of the failure term.

{defi}

[Core Context]
Given a context
\Gamma
, the associated
core context
is defined as

\Gamma^{\dagger}=\{x:\pi\in\Gamma\,|\,\pi\not=\omega\}
.

{defi}

[Well-formed
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expressions]

An expression
\mathbb{M}
is
well-formed
if there exist
\Gamma
and
\tau
such that
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\tau
is entailed via the rules in Fig.
4
.

\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}:\tau

\mathtt{[F:wf\text{-}expr]}

\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\tau

\Gamma\vdash B:\pi

\mathtt{[F:wf\text{-}bag]}

\Gamma\models B:\pi

\Delta\models M:\tau

\mathtt{[F:weak]}

\Delta,x:\omega\models M:\tau

\Gamma,{x}:\sigma^{n}\models M:\tau\quad x\notin\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)

\mathtt{[F:abs]}

\Gamma\models\lambda x.M:\sigma^{n}\rightarrow\tau

\Gamma\models M:\sigma

\Delta\models B:\sigma^{k}

\mathtt{[F:bag]}

\Gamma\wedge\Delta\models\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B:\sigma^{k+1}

\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\sigma

\Gamma\models\mathbb{N}:\sigma

\mathtt{[F:sum]}

\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N}:\sigma

\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma^{\dagger})=\widetilde{x}

\mathtt{[F:fail]}

{\Gamma}\models\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}:\tau

\Gamma,{x}:\sigma^{k}\models M:\tau

\Delta\models B:\sigma^{j}

k,j\geq 0

\mathtt{[F:ex\text{-}sub]}

\Gamma\wedge\Delta\models M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

\Gamma\models M:\sigma^{j}\rightarrow\tau

\Delta\models B:\sigma^{k}

k,j\geq 0

\mathtt{[F:app]}

\Gamma\wedge\Delta\models M\ B:\tau

Figure 4.
Well-Formed Rules for
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}

Below we give a brief description of the rules in Fig.
4
. Essentially, they differ from the ones in Fig.
3
, by allowing mismatches between the number of copies of a variable in a functional position and the number of components in a bag.

•

Rules
\mathtt{[F{:}wf\text{-}expr]}
and
\mathtt{[F{:}wf\text{-}bag]}
derive that well-typed expressions and bags in
\lambda_{\oplus}
are well-formed.

•

Rules
\mathtt{[F{:}abs]}
,
\mathtt{[F{:}bag]}
, and
\mathtt{[F{:}sum]}
are as in the type system for
\lambda_{\oplus}
, but extended to the system of well-formed expressions.

•

Rules
\mathtt{[F{:}ex\text{-}sub]}
and
\mathtt{[F{:}app]}
differ from the similar typing rules as the size of the bags (as declared in their types) is no longer required to match the number of occurrences of the variable assignment in the typing context (
\mathtt{[F:ex\text{-}sub]}
), or the type of the term in the functional position (
\mathtt{[F:app]}
).

•

Rule
\mathtt{[F{:}fail]}
has no analogue in the type system: we allow
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
to be well-formed with any strict type, provided that the core context contains the types of the variables in
\widetilde{x}
(i.e., none of the variables in
\widetilde{x}
is typed with
\omega
).

Clearly, the set of well-typed expressions is strictly included in the set of well-formed expressions.
Take, e.g.,
M=x\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag N_{1},N_{2}\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
, where both
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are well-typed.
It is easy to see that
M
is well-formed. However,
M
is not well-typed.

{exa}

[Cont. Example
2.3.1
]

We explore the well-formedness of some of the terms motivated in previous examples:

(1)

Term
M_{1}=(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag
is well-typed and also well-formed, as we have:

y:\sigma\vdash(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag:\sigma

\mathtt{[F:wf\text{-}expr]}

y:\sigma\models(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag:\sigma

(2)

We saw that term
M_{2}=(\lambda x.x)(\Lbag y,z\Rbag)
is not typable; however, it is well-formed:

\vdash\lambda x.x:\sigma^{1}\rightarrow\sigma

\mathtt{[F:wf\text{-}expr]}

\models\lambda x.x:\sigma^{1}\rightarrow\sigma

y:\sigma,z:\sigma\vdash\Lbag y,z\Rbag:\sigma^{2}

\mathtt{[F:wf\text{-}bag]}

y:\sigma,z:\sigma\models\Lbag y,z\Rbag:\sigma^{2}

1,2\geq 0

\mathtt{[F:app]}

y:\sigma,z:\sigma\models(\lambda x.x)(\Lbag y,z\Rbag):\sigma

Notice that both
\vdash\lambda x.x:\sigma^{1}\rightarrow\sigma
and
\Gamma\vdash\Lbag y,z\Rbag:\sigma^{2}
are well-typed.

(3)

Similarly, the term
M_{3}=(\lambda x.x)\mathtt{1}
is also well-formed. The corresponding derivation is as above, but uses an empty context as well as the well-formedness rule for bags:

\ \vdash\mathtt{1}:\sigma^{0}

\mathtt{[F:wf\text{-}bag]}

\models\mathtt{1}:\sigma^{0}

Notice how
\sigma^{0}=\omega
and that
\models\mathtt{1}:\omega
.

(4)

Term
M_{4}=(\lambda x.y)\mathtt{1}
is well-typed and also well-formed.

(5)

Interestingly, term
M_{5}=\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}
is well-formed as:

\mathtt{[F:fail]}

\models\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}:\tau

{exa}

Let us consider an expression that is not well-formed:

\lambda x.x\Lbag\lambda y.y,~\lambda z.z_{1}\Lbag z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag\Rbag\Rbag.

Notice that
\lambda x.x
is applied to bags of two different types:

•

The first bag containing
\lambda y.y
is well-typed, thus well-formed. Consider the derivation
\Pi_{1}
:

\mathtt{[T:var]}

y:\sigma\vdash y:\sigma

\mathtt{[T:abs]}

\vdash\lambda y.y:\sigma\to\sigma

\mathtt{[T:\mathtt{1}]}

\vdash\mathtt{1}:\omega

\mathtt{[T:bag]}

\vdash\Lbag\lambda y.y\Rbag\cdot\mathtt{1}:\sigma\to\sigma

\mathtt{[F:wf\text{-}bag]}

\models\Lbag\lambda y.y\Rbag\cdot\mathtt{1}:\sigma\to\sigma

In the rest of the example we will omit the labels of rule applications, and concatenations with the empty bag
\mathtt{1}
(i.e.,
\Lbag\lambda y.y\Rbag\cdot\mathtt{1}
will be written simply as
\Lbag\lambda y.y\Rbag
) and corresponding sub-derivations consisting of applications of Rule
\mathtt{[T:\mathtt{1}]}
.

•

The second bag contains
\lambda z.z_{1}\Lbag z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag\Rbag
contains an abstraction that acts as a weakening as
z
does not appear within
z_{1}\Lbag z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag\Rbag
. Consider the derivation
\Pi_{2}
:

z_{1}:\sigma\to\sigma\vdash z_{1}:\sigma\to\sigma

z_{1}:\sigma\to\sigma\vdash z_{1}:\sigma\to\sigma

z_{2}:\sigma\vdash z_{2}:\sigma

z_{2}:\sigma\vdash\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag:\sigma

z_{1}:\sigma\to\sigma,z_{2}:\sigma\vdash z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag:\sigma

z_{1}:\sigma\to\sigma,z_{2}:\sigma\vdash\Lbag z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag\Rbag:\sigma

z_{1}:\sigma\to\sigma\wedge\sigma\to\sigma,z_{2}:\sigma\vdash z_{1}\Lbag z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag\Rbag:\sigma

z_{1}:\sigma\to\sigma\wedge\sigma\to\sigma,z_{2}:\sigma,z:\omega\vdash z_{1}\Lbag z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag\Rbag:\sigma

z_{1}:\sigma\to\sigma\wedge\sigma\to\sigma,z_{2}:\sigma\vdash\lambda z.z_{1}\Lbag z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag\Rbag:\omega\to\sigma

z_{1}:\sigma\to\sigma\wedge\sigma\to\sigma,z_{2}:\sigma\models\lambda z.z_{1}\Lbag z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag\Rbag:\omega\to\sigma

\underbrace{z_{1}:\sigma\to\sigma\wedge\sigma\to\sigma,z_{2}:\sigma}_{\Gamma}\models\Lbag\lambda z.z_{1}\Lbag z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag\Rbag\Rbag:\omega\to\sigma

•

The concatenation of these two bags is not well-formed since each component has a different type:
\sigma\to\sigma
and
\omega\to\sigma
. Therefore,
\lambda x.x\Lbag~\lambda y.y~,~\lambda z.z_{1}\Lbag z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag\Rbag~\Rbag
is not well-formed.

Notice that if we change
\lambda y.y
to
\lambda y.y_{1}
in the first bag, we would have a derivation
\Pi_{1}^{\prime}
for
y_{1}:\sigma\models\lambda y.y_{1}:\omega\to\sigma
. This would allow us to concatenate the bags with derivation
\Pi_{3}
:

\Pi_{1}^{\prime}

y_{1}:\sigma\models\lambda y.y_{1}:\omega\to\sigma

\Pi_{2}

\Gamma\models\lambda z.z_{1}\Lbag z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag\Rbag:\omega\to\sigma

\mathtt{1}:\omega

\Gamma\models\Lbag\lambda z.z_{1}\Lbag z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag\Rbag\Rbag\cdot\mathtt{1}:\omega\to\sigma

\Gamma,y_{1}:\sigma\models\Lbag\lambda y.y_{1}\Rbag\cdot\Lbag\lambda z.z_{1}\Lbag z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag\Rbag\Rbag\cdot\mathtt{1}:(\omega\to\sigma)^{2}

Thus, the whole term becomes well-formed:

x:\omega\to\sigma\vdash x:\omega\to\sigma

\vdash\lambda x.x:(\omega\to\sigma)\to\omega\to\sigma

\models\lambda x.x:(\omega\to\sigma)\to\omega\to\sigma

\Pi_{3}

\Gamma,y_{1}:\sigma\models\Lbag~\lambda y.y_{1}~,~\lambda z.z_{1}\Lbag z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag\Rbag~\Rbag:(\omega\to\sigma)^{2}

\Gamma,y_{1}:\sigma\models\lambda x.x\Lbag~\lambda y.y_{1}~,~\lambda z.z_{1}\Lbag z_{1}\Lbag z_{2}\Rbag\Rbag~\Rbag:\omega\to\sigma

Well-formedness rules satisfy subject reduction with respect to the rules in Fig.
2
and relies on the linear substitution lemma for
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
:

Lemma 6

(Substitution Lemma for
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)
.

If
\Gamma,x:\sigma^{k}\models M:\tau
(with
k\geq 1
),
\mathsf{head}(M)=x
, and
\Delta\models N:\sigma

then

\Gamma\wedge\Delta,x:\sigma^{k-1}\models M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}
.

We now show subject reduction on well formed expressions in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
. We use our results of subject reduction for well-typed
\lambda_{\oplus}
(Theorem
3
) and extend them to
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

Theorem 7

(Subject Reduction in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)
.

If
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\tau
and
\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
then
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}^{\prime}:\tau
.

Proof 2.5

(Proof (Sketch))
.

By structural induction on the reduction rules. See App.
A
for details.

Differently from
\lambda_{\oplus}
, subject expansion fails for
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
. This is due to the possibility of failure in the use of resources. In
\lambda_{\oplus}
, if a resource is substituted within a term it is always done once, hence the term substituted must always be well-typed; however, in reductions that lead to the failure term, resources within a bag may be discarded before ever being substituted and hence, there is no requirement to be well-formed. Formally, we have:

Theorem 8

(Failure of Subject Expansion in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)
.

If
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}^{\prime}:\tau
and
\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
then it is not necessarily the case that
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\tau
.

Proof 2.6
.

A counter-example suffices here. Consider the term
\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}
, which is well-formed but not well-typed, and let
\Omega^{l}
be the term
(\lambda x.x\Lbag x\Rbag)\Lbag\lambda x.x\Lbag x\Rbag\Rbag
.
Notice that
\text{-}\models\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}:\tau
and

\mathtt{fail}^{x}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag\Omega^{l}\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}
, but

\mathtt{fail}^{x}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag\Omega^{l}\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
is not well-formed (nor well-typed)
.

3.
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
: A Resource Calculus With Sharing

We define
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, a variant of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
with a sharing construct, which we adopt following the atomic
\lambda
-calculus in
[
GHP13
]
.
In
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, a variable is only allowed to appear once in a term:
multiple occurrences of the same variable are atomized, i.e., they are given new different variable names.
The “atomization” of variable occurrences realized in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
via sharing will turn out to be very convenient to define our encoding into
\mathsf{s}\pi
.

Our language
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, defined in
\lx@sectionsign

3.1
, includes also a form of explicit substitution, called
explicit linear substitution
, which enables a refined analysis of the consumption of linear resources. Later, in
\lx@sectionsign

3.2
, we introduce the reduction semantics that implements a lazy evaluation.
In
\lx@sectionsign

3.3
, we present a non-idempotent intersection type system to control the use of resources.
Finally, in
\lx@sectionsign

3.4
we give an encoding from
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, denoted

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
, whose correctness is established in
\lx@sectionsign

5
.

3.1.
Syntax

The syntax of
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
only modifies the syntax of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-terms, which is defined by the grammar below; the syntax of bags
B
and expressions
\mathbb{M}
is as in Def.
2.1
.

\displaystyle\mbox{(Terms)}\quad M,N,L::=

\displaystyle~~x\ |\ \lambda x.(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\ |\ (M\ B)\ |\ M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}\ |\ \mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}

\displaystyle\ |\ M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\ |\ (M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

Distinctive aspects are the
sharing construct

M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]

and the
explicit linear substitution

M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}
.
The term
M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
defines the sharing of variables
\widetilde{x}
occurring in
M
using
x
.
We shall refer to
x
as
sharing variable
and to
\widetilde{x}
as
shared variables
.
Notice that
\widetilde{x}
can be empty:
M[\leftarrow x]
expresses that
x
does not share any variables in
M
. The sharing construct
M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
binds the variables in
\widetilde{x}
; the occurrence of
x_{i}
can appear within the fail term
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{y}}
, if
x_{i}\in\widetilde{y}
.
In the explicit linear substitution
M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}
binds
x
in
M
.
As in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, the term
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
explicitly accounts for failed attempts at substituting the variables
\widetilde{x}
, due to an excess or lack of resources. A variable that is not explicitly sharing/shared is called
independent
.

{exa}

The following are examples of
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-terms.

•

(Shared identity)
\hat{{\bf I}}=\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x]

•

(Independent variables) An independent variable
x
applied to a 1-component bag (another independent variable):
x\Lbag x_{1}\Rbag

•

\hat{{\bf I}}
applied to a 1-component bag:
\hat{{\bf I}}\Lbag y_{1}\Rbag[y_{1}\leftarrow y]

•

\hat{{\bf I}}
applied to a 2-component bag:
\hat{{\bf I}}(\Lbag y_{1},y_{2}\Rbag)[y_{1},y_{2}\leftarrow y]

•

Shared vacuous abstraction:
(\lambda y.x_{1}\Lbag x_{2}\Rbag[\leftarrow y])[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]

•

\hat{{\bf I}}
applied to a bag containing an explicit substitution of a failure term that does not share the variable
y
:
\hat{{\bf I}}\Lbag\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag N\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\Rbag

•

An abstraction on
x
of two shared occurrences of
x
:
\hat{D}=\lambda x.x_{1}\Lbag x_{2}\Rbag[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]

The syntax of terms is subject to some natural conditions on variable occurrences and on the structure of the sharing construct and the explicit linear substitution. We formalize these conditions as
consistency
, defined as follows:

{defi}

[Consistent Terms, Bags, and Expressions]

We say that the expression
\mathbb{M}
is

consistent
if each subterm
M_{0}
of
\mathbb{M}
satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

If
M_{0}=M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
then: (i)

\widetilde{x}
contains pairwise distinct variables;
(ii) every
x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}
must occur exactly once in
M
; (iii)
x_{i}
is not a sharing variable;
(iv)
M
is consistent.

(2)

If
M_{0}=M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}
then: (i) the variable
x
must occur exactly once in
M
;
(ii)
x
cannot be a sharing variable; (iii)
M
and
N
are consistent; (iv)
\mathsf{fv}(M)\cap\mathsf{fv}(N)=\emptyset
.

(3)

Otherwise, for other forms of
M_{0}
, variables must occur exactly once, i.e.,:

•

If
M_{0}=\lambda x.(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])
then:
x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(M)
;
\widetilde{x}
contains pairwise distinct variables; every
x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}
must occur exactly once in
M
and is not a sharing variable;
M
is consistent.

•

If
M_{0}=(M\ B)
then
\mathsf{fv}(M)\cap\mathsf{fv}(B)=\emptyset
and
M
and
B
are consistent.

•

If
M_{0}=\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
then
\widetilde{x}
contains pairwise distinct variables.

•

If
M_{0}=(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
then:
x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(M)
;
\widetilde{x}
contains pairwise distinct variables; every
x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}
must occur exactly once in
M
and is not a sharing variable;
\mathsf{fv}(M)\cap\mathsf{fv}(B)=\emptyset
; and
M
and
B
are consistent.

Consistency extends to bags as follows.
The bag
\mathtt{1}
is always consistent.
The bag
\Lbag M\Rbag
is consistent if
M
is consistent.
The bag
A\cdot B
is consistent if (i)
A
and
B
are consistent and (ii)
\mathsf{fv}(A)\cap\mathsf{fv}(B)=\emptyset
.

We now discuss the consistency conditions for the sharing construct
M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
. Condition 1(ii) enforces that variables cannot have more than one linear occurrence in the subject of a sharing construct: this condition rules out terms such as

x_{1}\Lbag x_{1}\Lbag y\Rbag\Rbag[x_{1}\leftarrow x]
. Condition 1(iii), which rules out terms of the form
x_{1}\Lbag x_{2}\Lbag x_{3}\Lbag y\Rbag\Rbag\Rbag[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x^{\prime}][x^{\prime},x_{3}\leftarrow x]
, is for convenience: by requiring that sharing occurrences appear at the top level in bindings, we can easily deduce the number of occurrences of a variable by measuring the size of
\widetilde{x}
in
[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
, rather than inductively having to measure the occurrences of each
x^{\prime}\in\widetilde{x}
in multiple sharing constructs.

Conditions on the explicit linear substitution
M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}
formalize our design choice:
an explicit linear substitution is defined when the number of
variables to be substituted coincides with the number of available resources.
In particular, Condition 2(i) rules out
terms of the form
y{\langle\!|}M/x{|\!\rangle}
, where an explicit linear substitution has no variable to perform a substitution. Condition 2(ii) rules out terms such as
M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]{\langle\!|}M/x{|\!\rangle}
, in which a term is to be linearly substituted for a single variable
x
; however, as the variable is shared twice within
M
, there are less available terms to be substituted than it is necessary.

Finally, Condition 3 enforces that each variable occurs only once in a consistent term, and also that in

\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
, the
\widetilde{x}
denotes a set of variables (rather than a multiset), as variables can appear at most once within consistent terms. Thus, consistent terms also excludes terms such as
\mathtt{fail}^{x,x}
.

In what follows, we shall be working with consistent terms only, which we will call simply terms in
our definitions and results. As we will see, consistency will be preserved by
reduction (Theorem
11
)
and ensured by typing (Theorem
18
) and a structural congruence on terms (Theorem
32
).

3.2.
Reduction Semantics

Similarly to
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, the reduction semantics of
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is given by a relation
\longrightarrow
, defined by the rules in Fig.
5
; it consists of an extension of reductions in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
that deals with the sharing construct
[\cdot\leftarrow\cdot]
and with the explicit linear substitution
\cdot{\langle\!|}\cdot/\cdot{|\!\rangle}
. In order to define the reduction rules formally, we require some auxiliary notions:
the free variables of an expression/term,
the head of a term, linear head substitution, and contexts.

{defi}

[Free Variables]

The set of free variables of a term, bag and expressions in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, is defined inductively as

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{2cm}}l}\mathsf{fv}(x)=\{x\}\hfil\hskip 56.9055pt&\mathsf{fv}(\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}})=\{\widetilde{x}\}\\
\mathsf{fv}(\Lbag M\Rbag)=\mathsf{fv}(M)\hfil\hskip 56.9055pt&\mathsf{fv}(B_{1}\cdot B_{2})=\mathsf{fv}(B_{1})\cup\mathsf{fv}(B_{2})\\
\mathsf{fv}(M\ B)=\mathsf{fv}(M)\cup\mathsf{fv}(B)\hfil\hskip 56.9055pt&\mathsf{fv}(\mathtt{1})=\emptyset\\
\mathsf{fv}(M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle})=(\mathsf{fv}(M)\setminus\{x\})\cup\mathsf{fv}(N)\hfil\hskip 56.9055pt&\mathsf{fv}(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])=(\mathsf{fv}(M)\setminus\{\widetilde{x}\})\cup\{x\}\\
\mathsf{fv}(\lambda x.(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]))=\mathsf{fv}(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\setminus\{x\}\hfil\hskip 56.9055pt&\mathsf{fv}(\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N})=\mathsf{fv}(\mathbb{M})\cup\mathsf{fv}(\mathbb{N})\end{array}\\
\ \mathsf{fv}((M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)=(\mathsf{fv}(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\setminus\{x\})\cup\mathsf{fv}(B)\end{array}

As usual, a term
M
is
closed
if
\mathsf{fv}(M)=\emptyset
.

{defi}

[Head]

The head of a term
M
, denoted
\mathsf{head}(M)
, is defined inductively:

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{3cm}}l}\mathsf{head}(x)=x\hfil\hskip 85.35826pt&\mathsf{head}(\lambda x.(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]))=\lambda x.(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\\
\mathsf{head}(M\ B)=\mathsf{head}(M)\hfil\hskip 85.35826pt&\mathsf{head}(M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle})=\mathsf{head}(M)\\
\mathsf{head}(\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}})=\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}\hfil\hskip 85.35826pt&\end{array}\\
\ \mathsf{head}(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])=\begin{cases}x&\text{If $\mathsf{head}(M)=y\text{ and }y\in\widetilde{x}$}\\
\mathsf{head}(M)&\text{Otherwise}\end{cases}\\
\ \mathsf{head}((M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)=\begin{cases}\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}&\text{If $|\widetilde{x}|\not=\mathsf{size}(B)$}\\
\mathsf{head}(M[\leftarrow x])&\text{If $\widetilde{x}=\emptyset$ and $B=\mathtt{1}$}\\
(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle&\text{Otherwise}\end{cases}\end{array}

The most notable difference between
\mathsf{head}(\cdot)
in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
(cf. Definition
2.2
) and in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
concerns explicit substitution. Both definitions return
\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}
in a mismatch of resources; in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, the head term of an explicit substitution is only defined in the case of empty sharing (weakening). As we will see, this allows us to prioritize explicit substitution reductions over fetch reductions, as the head variable will block until an explicit substitution is separated into its linear component.

{defi}

[Linear Head Substitution]
Given a term
M
with
\mathsf{head}(M)=x
, the linear substitution of a term
N
for
x
in
M
, written
M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}
is inductively defined as:

\displaystyle x{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}

\displaystyle=N

\displaystyle(M\ B){\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}

\displaystyle=(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})\ B

\displaystyle(M{\langle\!|}L/y{|\!\rangle}){\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}

\displaystyle=(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})\ {\langle\!|}L/y{|\!\rangle}

\displaystyle x\not=y

\displaystyle((M[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle){\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}

\displaystyle=(M[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y]{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle x\not=y

\displaystyle(M[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y]){\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}

\displaystyle=(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y]

\displaystyle x\not=y

We now define contexts for terms and expressions in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
. Term contexts involve an explicit linear substitution, rather than an explicit substitution: this is due to the reduction strategy we have chosen to adopt (cf. Rule
