\mathtt{[RS:Ex\text{-}Sub]}
in Fig.
5
), as we always wish to evaluate explicit substitutions first. Expression contexts can be seen as sums with holes.

{defi}

[Term and Expression Contexts in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
]
Let
[\cdot]
denote a hole.
Contexts for terms and expressions are defined by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{.7cm}}rl}\text{(Term Contexts)}\hfil\hskip 19.91684pt&C[\cdot],C^{\prime}[\cdot]&::=([\cdot])B\mid([\cdot]){\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}\mid([\cdot])[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\mid([\cdot])[\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\\
\text{(Expression Contexts)}\hfil\hskip 19.91684pt&D[\cdot],D^{\prime}[\cdot]&::=M+[\cdot]\mid[\cdot]+M\end{array}

The substitution of a hole with a term
M
in a context
C[\cdot]
, denoted
C[M]
, must be a
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term.

We assume that the terms that fill in the holes respect
consistency
(i.e., variables appear in a term only once, shared variables must occur in the context).

{exa}

This example illustrates that certain contexts cannot be filled with certain terms. Consider the hole in context
C[\cdot]=([\cdot]){\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}
.

•

The hole cannot be filled with
y
, since
C[y]=y{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}
is not a consistent term.
Indeed,
M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}
requires that
x
occurs exactly once within
M
.

•

Similarly, the hole cannot be filled with
\mathtt{fail}^{z}
with
z\neq x
, since
C[\mathtt{fail}^{z}]=(\mathtt{fail}^{z}){\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}
and
x
does not occur in the
\mathtt{fail}^{z}
, thus, the result is not a consistent term.

\mathtt{[RS{:}Beta]}

(\lambda x.M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])B\longrightarrow M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

B=\Lbag M_{1}\Rbag\cdots\Lbag M_{k}\Rbag\qquad k\geq 1

M\not=\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{y}}

\mathtt{[RS{:}Ex\text{-}Sub]}

\!M[x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow\sum_{B_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}M{\langle\!|}B_{i}(1)/x_{1}{|\!\rangle}\cdots{\langle\!|}B_{i}(k)/x_{k}{|\!\rangle}

\mathsf{head}(M)=x

\mathtt{[RS{:}Lin\text{-}Fetch]}

M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}\longrightarrow M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}

k\neq\mathsf{size}(B)\quad\widetilde{y}=(\mathsf{fv}(M)\setminus\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\})\cup\mathsf{fv}(B)

\mathtt{[RS{:}Fail]}

M[x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow\sum_{\mathsf{PER}(B)}\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{y}}

\widetilde{y}=\mathsf{fv}(B)

\mathtt{[RS{:}Cons_{1}]}

\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}B\longrightarrow{}\hskip-11.38109pt\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{PER}(B)}\hskip-8.53581pt\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}\cup\widetilde{y}}

\mathsf{size}(B)=k\quad k+|\widetilde{x}|\not=0\quad\widetilde{z}=\mathsf{fv}(B)

\mathtt{[RS{:}Cons_{2}]}

(\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}\cup\widetilde{y}}[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{}\longrightarrow\hskip-8.53581pt\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{PER}(B)}\hskip-8.53581pt\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{y}\cup\widetilde{z}}

\widetilde{z}=\mathsf{fv}(N)

\mathtt{[RS{:}Cons_{3}]}

\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{y}\cup x}{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}{}\longrightarrow\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{y}\cup\widetilde{z}}

M\longrightarrow M^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+M^{\prime}_{k}

\mathtt{[RS{:}TCont]}

C[M]\longrightarrow C[M^{\prime}_{1}]+\cdots+C[M^{\prime}_{k}]

\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}^{\prime}

\mathtt{[RS{:}ECont]}

D[\mathbb{M}]\longrightarrow D[\mathbb{M}^{\prime}]

Figure 5.
Reduction Rules for
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

Now we are ready to describe the rules in Fig.
5
. Intuitively, the lazy reduction relation
\longrightarrow
on expressions works as follows:
a
\beta
-reduction in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
results into an explicit substitution
M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
, which then evolves, as an in intermediate step, to an expression consisting of explicit linear substitutions, which are the ones reducing to a linear head substitution
{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}
(with
N\in B
) when the size of
B
coincides with the number of occurrences of
x
in
M
.
The term reduces to failure when there is a mismatch between the size of
B
and the number of shared variables to be substituted.
More in details, we have:

•

Rule
\mathtt{[RS{:}Beta]}
is standard and reduces to an explicit substitution.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[RS{:}Ex\text{-}Sub]}
applies when the size
k
of the bag coincides with the length of the list
\widetilde{x}=x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
. Intuitively, this rule “distributes” an explicit substitution into a sum of terms involving explicit linear substitutions; it considers all possible permutations of the elements in the bag among all shared variables.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[RS{:}Lin\text{-}Fetch]}
specifies the evaluation of a term with an explicit linear substitution into a linear head substitution.

We have three rules that reduce to the failure term—their objective is to accumulate all (free) variables involved in failed reductions.
Accordingly:

•

Rule
\mathtt{[RS{:}Fail]}
formalizes failure in the evaluation of an explicit substitution
M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
, which occurs if there is a mismatch between the resources (terms) present in
B
and the number of occurrences of
x
to be substituted.
The resulting failure term preserves all free variables in
M
and
B
within its attached set
\widetilde{y}
.

•

Rules
\mathtt{[RS{:}Cons_{1}]}
and
\mathtt{[RS{:}Cons_{2}]}
describe reductions that lazily consume the failure term, when a term has
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
at its head position.
The former rule consumes bags attached to it whilst preserving all its free variables.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[RS{:}Cons_{3}]}
accumulates into the failure term the free variables involved in an explicit linear substitution.

The contextual Rules
\mathtt{[RS{:}TCont]}
and
\mathtt{[RS{:}Econt]}
are standard.

{exa}

We show how a term can reduce using Rule
\mathtt{[RS{:}Cons_{2}]}
.

\displaystyle(\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])\Lbag\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag N\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\Rbag

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[RS{:}Beta]}}x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$\Lbag N\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[RS{:}Ex\text{-}Sub]}}x_{1}{\langle\!|}\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag N\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle/x_{1}{|\!\rangle}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[RS{:}Lin\text{-}Fetch]}}\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag N\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[RS{:}Cons_{2}]}}\mathtt{fail}^{\mathsf{fv}(N)}

{exa}

We illustrate how Rule
\mathtt{[RS{:}Fail]}
can introduce
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
into a term. It also shows how Rule
\mathtt{[RS{:}Cons_{3}]}
consumes an explicit linear substitution:

\displaystyle x_{1}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag N\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle[x_{1}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag M\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[RS{:}Ex\text{-}Sub]}}x_{1}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag N\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle{\langle\!|}M/x_{1}{|\!\rangle}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[RS{:}Fail]}}\mathtt{fail}^{\{x_{1}\}\cup\mathsf{fv}(N)}{\langle\!|}M/x_{1}{|\!\rangle}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[RS{:}Cons_{3}]}}\mathtt{fail}^{\mathsf{fv}(M)\cup\mathsf{fv}(N)}

Similarly to
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, reduction in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
satisfies a
diamond property
. Therefore, we have the analogue of Proposition
2.2
:

Proposition 9

(Diamond Property for
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)
.

For all
\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{N}_{1}
,
\mathbb{N}_{2}
in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
s.t.
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{1}
,
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{2}
with
\mathbb{N}_{1}\neq\mathbb{N}_{2}
then there exists
\mathbb{M}
such that
\mathbb{N}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{N}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}
.

Proof 3.1
.

The thesis follows as in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
since the left-hand sides of the reduction rules in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
do not interfere with each other.

Remark 10

(A Calculus with Sharing but Without Failure (
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
))
.

As we did in Remark
1
, we define a sub-calculus of
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
in which failure is not explicit. The calculus
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
is obtained from the syntax of
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
by disallowing the term
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
. The relevant reduction rules from Fig.
5
are
\mathtt{[RS:Beta]}
,
\mathtt{[RS{:}Ex\text{-}Sub]}
,
\mathtt{[RS{:}Lin\text{-}Fetch]}
, and the two contextual rules. We keep Def.
3.2
unchanged with the provision that
\mathsf{head}(M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)
is undefined when
|\widetilde{x}|\not=\mathsf{size}(B)
.

3.3.
Non-Idempotent Intersection Types

Similarly to
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, we now define
well-formed

\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expressions and a system of rules for checking well-formedness by modifying the rules in Fig.
4
. The grammar of strict and multiset types, the notions of typing assignments,
and judgements are the same as in Section
2.3
.
We need an extension to the notion of typing context: whereas in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
variables were only assigned to multiset types, now sharing variables are assigned to multiset types, shared and independent variables are assigned to strict types.

{defi}

We extend the definition of typing contexts (Def.
2.3.1
) as follows:

\displaystyle\Gamma,\Delta

\displaystyle=\text{-}\ |\ \Gamma,x:\pi\ |\ \Gamma,x:\sigma

The definition of core contexts is extended accordingly, and also denoted as
\Gamma^{\dagger}
.

The presentation is in two phases:

(1)

We consider the intersection type system given in Fig.
6
for which we consider the sub-calculus
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
, the sharing calculus excluding failure (cf. Rem.
10
).

(2)

We define well-formed expressions for the full language
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, via Def.
3.3.2
(see below).

To avoid ambiguities, we write
x:\sigma^{1}
to make it explicit that the type assignment involves an intersection type (and a sharing variable), rather than a strict type.

3.3.1.
Well-typed Expressions (in
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
)

The typing rules in Fig.
6
are essentially the same as the ones in Fig.
3
, but now taking into account the sharing construct
M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
and the explicit linear substitution. We discuss selected rules:

•

Rules
\mathtt{[TS{:}var]}
,
\mathtt{[TS{:}\mathtt{1}]}
,
\mathtt{[TS{:}bag]}
,
\mathtt{[TS{:}app]}
, and
\mathtt{[TS{:}sum]}
are the same as in Fig.
3
, considering sharing within the terms and bags.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[TS{:}weak]}
deals with
k=0
, typing the term
M[\leftarrow x]
, when there are no occurrences of
x
in
M
, as long as
M
is typable.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[TS{:}abs\text{-}sh]}
is as expected: it requires that the sharing variable is assigned the
k
-fold intersection type
\sigma^{k}
.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[TS{:}ex\text{-}lin\text{-}sub]}
supports explicit linear substitutions and consumes one occurrence of
x:\sigma
from the context.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[TS{:}ex\text{-}sub]}
types explicit substitutions where a bag must consist of both the same type and length of the shared variable it is being substituted for.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[TS{:}share]}
requires that the shared variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
have the same type as the sharing variable
x
, for
k\neq 0
. This rule justifies the need for the extension of contexts with assignments of the form
x:\sigma
. This way, e.g., Example
3.3.1
below gives an application of Rule
\mathtt{[TS:share]}
with
k=1
).

{defi}

[Well-typed Expressions]
An expression
\mathbb{M}\in\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is
well-typed
(or typable) if there exist
\Gamma
and
\tau
such that
\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}:\tau
is entailed via the rules in Fig.
6
.

Again, the failure term
\mathtt{fail}
in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is not typable via this typing system. The following examples illustrate the typing rules.

{exa}

The term
((\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])\Lbag y_{1}\Rbag)[y_{1}\leftarrow y]
is well-typed, as follows:

\mathtt{[TS{:}var]}

x_{1}:\sigma\vdash x_{1}:\sigma

\mathtt{[TS{:}share]}

x:\sigma^{1}\vdash x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x]:\sigma

\mathtt{[TS{:}abs\text{-}sh]}

\vdash\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x]:\sigma^{1}\rightarrow\sigma

\mathtt{[TS{:}var]}

y_{1}:\sigma\vdash y_{1}:\sigma

\mathtt{[TS{:}\mathtt{1}]}

\vdash\mathtt{1}:\omega

\mathtt{[TS{:}bag]}

y_{1}:\sigma\vdash\Lbag y_{1}\Rbag\cdot\mathtt{1}:\sigma^{1}

\mathtt{[TS{:}app\text{-}sh]}

y_{1}:\sigma\vdash((\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])\Lbag y_{1}\Rbag):\sigma

\mathtt{[TS{:}share]}

y:\sigma^{1}\vdash((\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])\Lbag y_{1}\Rbag)[y_{1}\leftarrow y]:\sigma

\mathtt{[TS{:}var]}

x:\sigma\vdash x:\sigma

\mathtt{[TS{:}\mathtt{1}]}

\vdash\mathtt{1}:\omega

\Delta\vdash M:\tau

\mathtt{[TS{:}weak]}

\Delta,x:\omega\vdash M[\leftarrow x]:\tau

\Delta,x:\sigma^{k}\vdash M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]:\tau

\mathtt{[TS{:}abs\text{-}sh]}

\Delta\vdash\lambda x.(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]):\sigma^{k}\rightarrow\tau

\Gamma\vdash M:\pi\rightarrow\tau

\Delta\vdash B:\pi

\mathtt{[TS{:}app]}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M\ B:\tau

\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma

\Delta\vdash B:\sigma^{k}

\mathtt{[TS{:}bag]}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B:\sigma^{k+1}

\Delta\vdash N:\sigma\qquad\Gamma,x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau

\mathtt{[TS\!:\!ex\text{-}lin\text{-}sub]}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}:\tau

\Delta\vdash B:\sigma^{k}\qquad\Gamma,x:\sigma^{k}\vdash M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]:\tau

\mathtt{[TS\!:ex\text{-}sub]}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}:\sigma

\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{N}:\sigma

\mathtt{[TS{:}sum]}

\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N}:\sigma

\Delta,x_{1}:\sigma,\cdots,x_{k}:\sigma\vdash M:\tau\quad x\notin\mathtt{dom}(\Delta)\quad k\not=0

\mathtt{[TS{:}share]}

\Delta,x:\sigma^{k}\vdash M[x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}\leftarrow x]:\tau

Figure 6.
Typing Rules for
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
.

Theorem 11

(Consistency Stability Under
\longrightarrow
)
.

If
\mathbb{M}
is a consistent
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-expression and
\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
then
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is consistent.

Proof 3.2
.

By structural induction, and analyzing the reduction rules applied in
\mathbb{M}
. See
Appendix

B
for details.

As expected, the typing system satisfies the subject reduction property w.r.t. the reduction relation given in Fig.
5
, excluding rules for failure.

Theorem 12

(Subject Reduction in
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
)
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}:\tau
and
\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
then
\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}^{\prime}:\tau
.

Proof 3.3
.

Standard by induction on the rule applied in
\mathbb{M}
.

Lemma 13

(Linear Anti-substitution Lemma for
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
)
.

Let
M
and
N
be
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
-terms such that
\mathsf{head}(M)=x
. The following hold:

•

If
\Gamma,x:\sigma^{k-1}\vdash M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}:\tau
, with
k>1
, then there exist
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
such that
\Gamma_{1},x:\sigma^{k}\vdash M:\tau
, and
\Gamma_{2}\vdash N:\sigma
, where
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\wedge\Gamma_{2}
.

•

If
\Gamma\vdash M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}:\tau
, with
x\not\in\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)
, then there exist
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
such that
\Gamma_{1},x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau
, and
\Gamma_{2}\vdash N:\sigma
, where
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\wedge\Gamma_{2}
.

Proof 3.4
.

By structural induction on the reduction rule from Fig.
6
.
See App.
B
for details.

Theorem 14

(Subject Expansion for
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
)
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}^{\prime}:\tau
and
\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
then
\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}:\tau
.

Proof 3.5
.

Standard, by induction on the reduction rule applied. See App.
B
for details.

3.3.2.
Well-formed Expressions (in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)

On top of the intersection type system for
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
, we define well-formed expressions:
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-terms whose computation may lead to failure.

{defi}

[Well-formedness in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
]
An expression
\mathbb{M}
is well formed if there exist
\Gamma
and
\tau
such that
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\tau
is entailed via the rules in Fig.
7
.

\Gamma\vdash\mathbb{M}:\tau

\mathtt{[FS\!:\!wf\text{-}expr]}

\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\tau

\Gamma\vdash B:\pi

\mathtt{[FS\!:\!wf\text{-}bag]}

\Gamma\models B:\pi

\Gamma\models M:\tau

\mathtt{[FS\!:\!weak]}

\Gamma,x:\omega\models M[\leftarrow x]:\tau

\Gamma,x:\sigma^{k}\models M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]:\tau\quad x\notin\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)

\mathtt{[FS{:}abs\text{-}sh]}

\Gamma\models\lambda x.(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]):\sigma^{k}\rightarrow\tau

\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma^{\dagger})=\widetilde{x}

\mathtt{[FS{:}fail]}

\Gamma\models\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}:\tau

\Gamma\models M:\sigma^{j}\rightarrow\tau\quad\Delta\models B:\sigma^{k}

\mathtt{[FS{:}app]}

\Gamma,\Delta\models M\ B:\tau

\Gamma\models M:\sigma\quad\Delta\models B:\sigma^{k}

\mathtt{[FS{:}bag]}

\Gamma,\Delta\models\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B:\sigma^{k+1}

\Gamma,x:\sigma\models M:\tau\quad\Delta\models N:\sigma

\mathtt{[FS{:}ex\text{-}lin\text{-}sub]}

\Gamma,\Delta\models M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}:\tau

\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\sigma\quad\Gamma\models\mathbb{N}:\sigma

\mathtt{[FS{:}sum]}

\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N}:\sigma

\Gamma,x:\sigma^{k}\models M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]:\tau

\Delta\models B:\sigma^{j}

\mathtt{[FS{:}ex\text{-}sub]}

\Gamma,\Delta\models M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

\Gamma,x_{1}:\sigma,\cdots,x_{k}:\sigma\models M:\tau\quad x\notin\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)\quad k\not=0

\mathtt{[FS{:}share]}

\Gamma,x:\sigma^{k}\models M[x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}\leftarrow x]:\tau

Figure 7.
Well-formedness Rules for
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

Rules
\mathtt{[FS{:}wf\text{-}expr]}
and
\mathtt{[FS{:}wf\text{-}bag]}
guarantee that every well-typed expression and bag, respectively, is well-formed. Since our language is expressive enough to account for failing computations, we include rules for checking the structure of these ill-behaved terms—terms that can be well-formed, but not typable. For instance,

•

Rules
\mathtt{[FS{:}ex\text{-}sub]}
and
\mathtt{[FS{:}app]}
differ from similar typing rules in Fig.
6
: the size of the bags (as declared in their types) is no longer required to match.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[FS{:}fail]}
has no analogue in the type system: we allow the failure term
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
to be well-formed with any type, provided that the core context contains types for the variables in
\widetilde{x}
.

The other rules are similar to their corresponding ones in Fig.
4
and Fig.
6
.

The following example illustrates a
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expression that is well-formed but not well-typed.

{exa}
[Cont. Example
3.3.2
]
The
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expression consisting of an application of
\hat{I}
to a bag containing a failure term

\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])\Lbag\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\Rbag
is well-formed with type
\sigma
. The derivation, with omitted rule labels, is the following:

x_{1}:\sigma\vdash x_{1}:\sigma

x_{1}:\sigma\models x_{1}:\sigma

x:\sigma^{1}\models x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x]:\sigma

\models\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x]:\sigma\to\sigma

\models\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}:\sigma

y:\omega\models\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]:\sigma

\vdash\mathtt{1}:\omega

\models\mathtt{1}:\omega

\models\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$1$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma

\vdash\mathtt{1}:\omega

\models\mathtt{1}:\omega

\models\Lbag\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$1$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\Rbag:\sigma^{1}

\models\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x]\Lbag\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$1$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\Rbag:\sigma

Besides, we have

\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])\Lbag\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\Rbag\longrightarrow^{*}\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle
.

Well-formed
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expressions satisfy the subject reduction property; as usual, the proof relies on a linear substitution lemma for
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

Lemma 15

(Substitution Lemma for
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)
.

If
\Gamma,x:\sigma\models M:\tau
,
\mathsf{head}(M)=x
, and
\Delta\models N:\sigma

then

\Gamma,\Delta\models M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}:\tau
.

Proof 3.6
.

By structural induction on
M
. See App.
B
for details.

Theorem 16

(Subject Reduction in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)
.

If
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\tau
and
\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
then
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}^{\prime}:\tau
.

Proof 3.7
.

By structural induction on the reduction rule from Fig.
5
.
See App.
B
for details.

We close this part by stating the failure of subject expansion for well-formed expressions.

Theorem 17

(Failure of Subject Expansion in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)
.

If
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}^{\prime}:\tau
and
\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
then it is not necessarily the case that
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\tau
.

Proof 3.8
.

We adapt the counter-example from the proof of Theorem
8
.
Consider the term
\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}
, which is well-formed but not well-typed, and let
\Omega^{l}
be the term
(\lambda x.x_{1}\Lbag x_{2}\Rbag[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x])\Lbag\lambda x.x_{1}\Lbag x_{2}\Rbag[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]\Rbag
.
Notice that

\mathtt{fail}^{x_{1}}[x_{1}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag\Omega^{l}\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}

and
\text{-}\models\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}:\tau
,
but

\mathtt{fail}^{x_{1}}[x_{1}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag\Omega^{l}\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
is not well-formed (nor well-typed).

Theorem 18

(Consistency enforced by typing)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-expression. If
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}
then
{\mathbb{M}}
is consistent.

Proof 3.9
.

By induction on the type derivation. See
Appendix

B
for details.

Taking Stock

Up to here, we have presented our source language
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
—a new resource lambda calculus with failure—and its fail-free sub-calculus
\lambda_{\oplus}
. Based on them we defined well-typed and well-formed expressions. Similarly, we defined the intermediate calculus
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and its sub-calculus
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
. We now move on to define
a translation of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

3.4.
From
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}

Borrowing inspiration from translations given in
[
GHP13
]
for the atomic
\lambda
-calculus, we now define a translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
from well-formed expressions in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
. It relies on an auxiliary translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
on

\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-terms, which depends on
the notion of (simultaneous) linear substitution (Def.
3.4
) which, intuitively, forces all bound variables in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to become shared variables in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

The correctness of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
will be addressed in
\lx@sectionsign

5.2
.

{defi}

[Linear substitution]
Suppose given a
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term
M
, a variable
x
, and a sequence of variables
\widetilde{w}=y,\widetilde{z}
.
When
\#(x,M)=|\widetilde{w}|
and
\{y\}\cap\widetilde{z}=\emptyset
,
the
linear substitution

M\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y,\widetilde{z}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle
of variable
x
for variables
\widetilde{w}
in
M
is defined inductively as follows:

\displaystyle x\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle

\displaystyle=y

\displaystyle(\lambda z.M)\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle

\displaystyle=\lambda z.(M\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle)\quad\text{if }x\in\mathsf{fv}(M)

\displaystyle(M\ B)\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}((M\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle)\ B)&\text{if }x\in\mathsf{fv}(M)\\
(M\ (B\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle))&\text{if }x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(M),x\in\mathsf{fv}(B)\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{z}}\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{z}^{\prime},y}\quad\text{if }x\in\widetilde{z}\text{ and }\widetilde{z}=\widetilde{z}^{\prime},x

\displaystyle(M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$z$}\rangle\!\rangle)\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(M\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle)\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$z$}\rangle\!\rangle&\text{if }x\in\mathsf{fv}(M)\\
M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle$}\!/\mbox{\small$z$}\rangle\!\rangle&\text{if }x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(M),x\in\mathsf{fv}(B)\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathtt{1}\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle

\displaystyle=\text{undefined }

\displaystyle\Lbag M\Rbag\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle

\displaystyle=\Lbag M\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\Rbag\quad\text{if }x\in\mathsf{fv}(M)

\displaystyle(A\cdot B)\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}((A\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle)\cdot B)&\text{if }x\in\mathsf{fv}(A)\\
(A\cdot(B\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle))&\text{if }x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(A),x\in\mathsf{fv}(B)\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle M\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y,\widetilde{z}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle

\displaystyle=(M\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle)\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{z}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle

Otherwise, in all other cases, the substitution is undefined.
We write
M\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{k}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle
to stand for

(\cdots((M\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$z_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle)\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$z_{2}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle)\cdots\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$z_{k}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle)
.

Notice that for a
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term with multiple occurrences of the variable to be substituted for, this linear substitution fixes an ordering of instantiation. For example,
\lambda x.y\Lbag y,x\Rbag\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$z_{1},z_{2}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle
results in
\lambda x.z_{1}\Lbag z_{2},x\Rbag
, and a permutation of variables as in
\lambda x.z_{2}\Lbag z_{1},x\Rbag
is not accounted for. This is not restrictive; actually it is enough for our purposes since this substitution will only be used in Def.
3.4
and the variables being substituted will be bound by sharing, and therefore could be
\alpha
-renamed.

{defi}

[From
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
]

Let
M\in\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.
Suppose
\Gamma\models{M}:\tau
, with

\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)=\mathsf{fv}(M)=\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}\}
and
\#(x_{i},M)=j_{i}
.
We define
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
as

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{y_{1}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x_{1}$}\rangle\cdots\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{y_{k}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x_{k}$}\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[\widetilde{y_{1}}\leftarrow x_{1}]\cdots[\widetilde{y_{k}}\leftarrow x_{k}]

where
\widetilde{y_{i}}=y_{i_{1}},\cdots,y_{i_{j_{i}}}

and the translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}:\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}\to\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is defined in Fig.
8
.
The translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
extends homomorphically to expressions.

As already mentioned,
the translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
“atomizes” occurrences of variables, in the spirit of
[
GHP13
]
: it converts
n
occurrences of a variable
x
in a term into
n
distinct variables
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
.
The sharing construct coordinates the occurrences of these variables by constraining each to occur exactly once within a term.
We proceed in two stages:

(1)

First, we use
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
to ensure that each free variable (say,
y
) is replaced by a shared variable (say,
y_{i}\in\widetilde{y}
), which is externally bound by the
y
in
[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y]
.

(2)

Second, we apply the auxiliary translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
on the corresponding to the sharing of bound variables.

We now describe the two cases of Fig.
8
that are noteworthy.

•

In
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\lambda x.M{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
, the occurrences of
x
are replaced with fresh shared variables that only occur once in
M
.

•

The definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
considers two possibilities.
If the bag being translated is non-empty and the explicit substitution would not lead to failure (the number of occurrences of
x
and the size of the bag coincide) then we translate the explicit substitution as a sum of explicit linear substitutions.
Otherwise, the explicit substitution will lead to a failure, and the translation proceeds inductively.
As we will see, doing this will enable a tight operational correspondence result with
\mathsf{s}\pi
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}x{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}

\displaystyle=x\hskip 91.04872pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathtt{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}=\mathtt{1}\hskip 85.35826pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}=\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M\ B{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}B{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}\hskip 162.18062pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}=\Lbag{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}\Rbag\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}B{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\lambda x.M{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}

\displaystyle=\lambda x.({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M\langle\widetilde{y}/x\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow x])\quad\text{ $\#(x,M)=n$, each $y_{i}\in\widetilde{y}$ is fresh}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\hskip-10.00002pt\sum_{\hskip 8.19447ptB_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}B{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}})}\hskip-17.07182pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M\langle\widetilde{y}/x\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}{\langle\!|}B_{i}(1)/x_{1}{|\!\rangle}\cdots{\langle\!|}B_{i}(k)/x_{k}{|\!\rangle}&\hskip-5.0pt\#(x,M)=\mathsf{size}(B)=k\geq 1\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M\langle y_{1}.\cdots,y_{k}/x\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small${\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}B{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle&\hskip-5.0pt\text{otherwise, }\#(x,M)=k\geq 0\end{cases}

Figure 8.
Auxiliary Translation:
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

{exa}

[Cont. Example
2.1
]

We illustrate the translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
on previously discussed examples. In all cases, we start by ensuring that the free variables are shared. This explains the occurrence of
[y_{1}\leftarrow y]
in the translation of
M_{1}
as well as
[y_{1}\leftarrow y]
and
[z_{1}\leftarrow z]
in the translation of
M_{2}
. Then, the auxiliary translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
ensures that bound variables that are guarded by an abstraction are shared. This explains, e.g., the occurrence of
[x_{1}\leftarrow x]
in the translation of
M_{1}
.

•

The translation of a
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term with one occurrence of a bound variable and one occurrence of a free variable:
M_{1}=(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y_{1}\Rbag{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[y_{1}\leftarrow y]

\displaystyle=((\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])\Lbag y_{1}\Rbag)[y_{1}\leftarrow y]

•

The translation of a
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term with one bound and two different free variables:
M_{2}=(\lambda x.x)(\Lbag y,z\Rbag)
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}(\lambda x.x)(\Lbag y,z\Rbag){\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}(\lambda x.x)(\Lbag y_{1},z_{1}\Rbag){\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[y_{1}\leftarrow y][z_{1}\leftarrow z]

\displaystyle=((\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])(\Lbag y_{1},z_{1}\Rbag))[y_{1}\leftarrow y][z_{1}\leftarrow z]

•

The translation of a
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term with a vacuous abstraction:
M_{4}=(\lambda x.y)\mathtt{1}
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M_{4}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}(\lambda x.y)\mathtt{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}(\lambda x.y_{1})\mathtt{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[y_{1}\leftarrow y]

\displaystyle=((\lambda x.y_{1}[\leftarrow x])\mathtt{1})[y_{1}\leftarrow y]

•

The translation of a
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-expression:
M_{6}=(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag+(\lambda x.x)\Lbag z\Rbag
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M_{6}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag+(\lambda x.x)\Lbag z\Rbag{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}(\lambda x.x)\Lbag y\Rbag{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}(\lambda x.x)\Lbag z\Rbag{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}

\displaystyle=((\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])\Lbag y_{1}\Rbag)[y_{1}\leftarrow y]+((\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])\Lbag z_{1}\Rbag)[z_{1}\leftarrow y]

{exa}

The translation of a
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term with two occurrences of a bound variable and two occurrences of a free variable:
M=(\lambda x.x\Lbag x\Rbag)(\Lbag y,y\Rbag)
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}(\lambda x.x\Lbag x\Rbag)(\Lbag y,y\Rbag){\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}(\lambda x.x\Lbag x\Rbag)(\Lbag y_{1},y_{2}\Rbag){\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[y_{1},y_{2}\leftarrow y]

\displaystyle=((\lambda x.x_{1}\Lbag x_{2}\Rbag[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x])(\Lbag y_{1},y_{2}\Rbag))[y_{1},y_{2}\leftarrow y]

{exa}

Now consider the translation of
y\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
, with
\mathsf{fv}(B)=\emptyset
and
y\neq x
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}y\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}y_{0}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[y_{0}\leftarrow y]

\displaystyle=y_{0}[\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small${\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}B{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle[y_{0}\leftarrow y].

Hence, the translation induces (empty) sharing on
x
, even if
x
does not occur in the term
y
.

Proposition 19

(
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
Preserves Consistency)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-expression. Then

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
is a consistent

\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-expression.

Proof 3.10
.

By induction on the structure of
\mathbb{M}
. See App.
B
for details.

4.
\mathsf{s}\pi
: A Session-Typed
\pi
-Calculus with Non-Determinism

The
\pi
-calculus
[
MPW92
]
is a model of concurrency in which
processes
interact via
names
(or
channels
) to exchange values, which can be themselves names.
Here we overview
\mathsf{s}\pi
, introduced by Caires and Pérez in
[
CP17
]
, in which
session types

[
Hon93
,
HVK98
]
ensure that the two endpoints of a channel perform matching actions:
when one endpoint sends, the other receives; when an endpoint closes, the other closes too.
Following
[
CP10
,
Wad12
]
,

\mathsf{s}\pi
defines a Curry-Howard correspondence between session types and a linear logic with two dual modalities (
{\binampersand}A
and
\oplus A
), which define
non-deterministic
sessions.
In
\mathsf{s}\pi
, cut elimination corresponds to process communication, proofs correspond to processes, and propositions correspond to session types.

4.1.
Syntax and Semantics

We use
x,y,z,w\ldots
to denote names implementing the
(session) endpoints
of protocols specified by session types.
We consider the sub-language of
[
CP17
]

without labeled choices and replication, which is actually sufficient to encode
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

{defi}

[Processes]
The syntax of
\mathsf{s}\pi
processes is given by the grammar in Fig.
9
.

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\hspace{1.5cm}}l}P,Q&::=&{\bf 0}\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&\text{(inaction)}\\
&\ |&\overline{x}(y).P\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&\text{(output)}\\
&\ |&x(y).P\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&\text{(input)}\\
&\ |&(P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q)\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&\text{(parallel)}\\
&\ |&(\nu x)P\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&\text{(restriction)}\\
&\ |&[x\leftrightarrow y]\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&\text{(forwarder)}\\
&\ |&x.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&\text{(session close)}\\
&\ |&x.\mathtt{close};P\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&\text{(complementary close)}\\
&\ |&x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};P\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&\text{(session confirmation)}\\
&\ |&x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&\text{(session failure)}\\
&\ |&x.\mathtt{some}_{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})};P\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&\text{(session dependency)}\\
&\ |&P\oplus Q\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&\text{(non-deterministic choice)}\end{array}

Figure 9.
Syntax of
\mathsf{s}\pi
.

As standard,
{\bf 0}
is the inactive process. Session communication is performed using the pair of primitives output and input: the output process
\overline{x}(y).P
sends a fresh name
y
along session
x
and then continues as
P
; the input process
x(y).P
receives a name
z
along
x
and then continues as
P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$z$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}
, which denotes the capture-avoiding substitution of
z
for
y
in
P
.
Process
P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q
denotes the parallel execution of
P
and
Q
.
Process
(\nu x)P
denotes the process
P
in which name
x
has been restricted, i.e.,
x
is kept private to
P
. The forwarder process
[x\leftrightarrow y]
denotes a bi-directional link between sessions
x
and
y
.
Processes
x.\overline{\mathtt{close}}
and
x.\mathtt{close};P
denote complementary actions for closing session
x
.

The following constructs introduce non-deterministic sessions which, intuitively,
may
provide a session protocol
or
fail.

•

Process
x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};P
confirms that the session on
x
will execute and continues as
P
.

•

Process
x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
signals the failure of implementing the session on
x
.

•

Process
x.\mathtt{some}_{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})};P
specifies a dependency on a non-deterministic session
x
.
This process can either (i) synchronize with an action
x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}
and continue as
P
, or (ii) synchronize with an action
x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
, discard
P
, and propagate the failure on
x
to
(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})
, which are sessions implemented in
P
.
When
x
is the only session implemented in
P
, the tuple of dependencies is empty and so we write simply
x.\mathtt{some};P
.

•

P\oplus Q
denotes a
non-deterministic choice
between
P
and
Q
.
We shall often write
\bigoplus_{i\in I}P_{i}
to stand for
P_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus P_{n}
.

In
(\nu y)P
and
x(y).P
the distinguished occurrence of name
y
is binding, with scope
P
.
The set of free names of
P
is denoted by
\mathit{fn}(P)
.
We identify process up to consistent renaming of bound names, writing
\equiv_{\alpha}
for this congruence.
We omit trailing occurrences of
{\bf 0}
; this way, e.g., we write
x.\mathtt{close}
instead of
x.\mathtt{close};{\bf 0}
.

Structural congruence
, denoted
\equiv
, expresses basic identities on the structure of processes and the non-collapsing nature of non-determinism.

{defi}

[Structural Congruence]

Structural congruence
is defined as the least congruence relation on processes such that:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\begin{array}[]{rcl}P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}{\bf 0}&\equiv&\!{\bf 0}\\
P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q&\equiv&Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}P\\
(P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q)\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}R&\equiv&P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}(Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}R)\\
\left[x\leftrightarrow y\right]&\equiv&\left[y\leftrightarrow x\right]\\
((\nu x)P)\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q&\equiv&(\nu x)(P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q),x\not\in\mathit{fn}(P)\\
(\nu x)(P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}(Q\oplus R))&\equiv&(\nu x)(P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q)\oplus(\nu x)(P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}R)\\
\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\bf 0}\oplus{\bf 0}&\equiv&{\bf 0}\\
P\oplus Q&\equiv&Q\oplus P\\
(P\oplus Q)\oplus R&\equiv&P\oplus(Q\oplus R)\\
(\nu x){\bf 0}&\equiv&{\bf 0}\\
(\nu x)(\nu y)P&\equiv&(\nu y)(\nu x)P\\
P\equiv_{\alpha}Q&\Longrightarrow&P\equiv Q\end{array}\end{array}

4.2.
Operational Semantics

The operational semantics of
\mathsf{s}\pi
is given by a reduction relation, denoted
P\longrightarrow Q
, which is the smallest relation on processes generated by the rules in Fig.
10
. These rules specify the computations that a process performs on its own. We now explain each rule.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{1.5cm}}rcl}\mathtt{[Comm]}\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&\overline{x}{(y)}.Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x(y).P&\longrightarrow&(\nu y)(Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}P)\\
\mathtt{[Forw]}\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&(\nu x)([x\leftrightarrow y]\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}P)&\longrightarrow&P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\}\quad(x\neq y)\\
\mathtt{[Close]}\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&x.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x.\mathtt{close};P&\longrightarrow&P\\
\mathtt{[Some]}\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x.\mathtt{some}_{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})};Q&\longrightarrow&P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q\\
\mathtt{[None]}\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x.\mathtt{some}_{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})};Q&\longrightarrow&w_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}\cdots\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}w_{n}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\\
\mathtt{[Cong]}\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&P\equiv P^{\prime}\wedge P^{\prime}\longrightarrow Q^{\prime}\wedge Q^{\prime}\equiv Q&\Longrightarrow&P\longrightarrow Q\\
\mathtt{[Par]}\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&Q\longrightarrow Q^{\prime}&\Longrightarrow&P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q\longrightarrow P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q^{\prime}\\
\mathtt{[Res]}\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&P\longrightarrow Q&\Longrightarrow&(\nu y)P\longrightarrow(\nu y)Q\\
\mathtt{[NChoice]}\hfil\hskip 42.67912pt&Q\longrightarrow Q^{\prime}&\Longrightarrow&P\oplus Q\longrightarrow P\oplus Q^{\prime}\\[5.69054pt]
\end{array}

Figure 10.
Reduction for
\mathsf{s}\pi
.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[Comm]}
formalizes communication, which concerns bound names only (internal mobility): name
y
is bound in both
\overline{x}{(y)}.Q
and
x(y).P
.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[Forw]}
implements the forwarder process that leads to a name substitution.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[Close]}
formalizes session closure and is self-explanatory.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[Some]}
describes the synchronization of a process, that is dependent on a non-deterministic session
x
, with the complementary process
x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}
that confirms the availability of such non-deterministic session.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[None]}
applies when the non-deterministic session is not available, prefix
x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
triggers this failure to all dependent sessions
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
; this may in turn trigger further failures (i.e., on sessions that depend on
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
).

•

Rule
\mathtt{[NChoice]}
defines the closure of reduction w.r.t. non-collapsing non-deterministic choice.

•

Rules
\mathtt{[Cong]}
,
\mathtt{[Par]}
and

\mathtt{[Res]}
are standard and formalize that reduction is closed under structural congruence, and also contextual closure of parallel and restriction constructs.

{exa}

We illustrate confluent reductions starting in a non-deterministic process
R
which will fail during communication due to unavailability of a session:

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\nu x)(x.\mathtt{some}_{(y_{1},y_{2})};y_{1}(z).y_{2}(w).{\bf 0}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}(x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};P\oplus x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}))

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle(\nu x)(x.\mathtt{some}_{(y_{1},y_{2})};y_{1}(z).y_{2}(w).{\bf 0}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}\!x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};P)\oplus(\nu x)(x.\mathtt{some}_{(y_{1},y_{2})};y_{1}(z).y_{2}(w).{\bf 0}\!\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}\!x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})

Letting
Q=y_{1}(z).y_{2}(w).{\bf 0}
, we have:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\nu x)(x.\mathtt{some}_{(y_{1},y_{2})};Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};P)\oplus(y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}y_{2}.\overline{\mathtt{none}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R=(\nu x)(x.\mathtt{some}_{(y_{1},y_{2})};Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}(x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};P\oplus x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\nu x)(Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}P)\oplus(y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}y_{2}.\overline{\mathtt{none}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\nu x)(Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}P)\oplus(\nu x)(x.\mathtt{some}_{(y_{1},y_{2})};Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})}

Observe that reduction is confluent.
The resulting term

(\nu x)(Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}P)\oplus(y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}y_{2}.\overline{\mathtt{none}})

includes both alternatives for the interaction on
x
, namely the successful one (i.e.,
(\nu x)(Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}P)
) but also the failure of
x
, which is then propagated to
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
, i.e.,
y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}y_{2}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
.

4.3.
Type System

The type discipline for
\mathsf{s}\pi
is based on the type system given in
[
CP17
]
, which contains modalities
{\binampersand}A
and
\oplus A
, as dual types for non-deterministic sessions.

{defi}

[Session Types]

Session types are given by

\displaystyle A,B

\displaystyle::=\bot\ |\ \mathbf{1}\ |\ A\otimes B\ |\ A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B\ |\ {\binampersand}A\ |\ \oplus A

Types are assigned to names: an
assignment

x:A
enforces the use of name
x
according to the protocol specified by
A
.
The multiplicative units
\bot
and
\mathbf{1}
are used to type terminated (closed) endpoints.

A\otimes B
types a name that first outputs a name of type
A
before proceeding as specified by
B
.
Similarly,
A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B
types a name that first inputs a name of type
A
before proceeding as specified by
B
.
Then we have the two modalities introduced in
[
CP17
]
.
We use
{\binampersand}A
as the type of a (non-deterministic) session that
may produce
a behavior of type
A
.
Dually,
\oplus A
denotes the type of a session that
may consume
a behavior of type
A
.

The two endpoints of a session must be
dual
to ensure absence of communication errors.
The dual of a type
A
is denoted
\overline{A}
.
Duality corresponds to negation
(\cdot)^{\bot}
in linear logic:

{defi}

[Duality]

The duality relation on types is given by:

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{1}}

\displaystyle=\bot

\displaystyle\overline{\bot}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle\overline{A\otimes B}

\displaystyle=\overline{A}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}\overline{B}

\displaystyle\overline{A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B}

\displaystyle=\overline{A}\otimes\overline{B}

\displaystyle\overline{\oplus A}

\displaystyle={\binampersand}\overline{A}

\displaystyle\overline{{\binampersand}A}

\displaystyle=\oplus\overline{A}

Typing judgments are of the form
P\vdash\Delta
, where
P
is a process and
\Delta
is a context of
the form
x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}
, which defines the assignment of type
A_{i}
to name
x_{i}
(with
1\leq i\leq n
); all names
x_{i}
must be distinct. The context
\Delta
is
linear
in that it is subject to exchange (the ordering of assignments does not matter), but not to weakening and contraction. In writing ‘
\Delta,x:A
’, we assume that
x
does not occur in
\Delta
; also, in writing ‘
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
’, we assume that the names in
\Delta_{1}
are distinct from those in
\Delta_{2}
.
The empty context is denoted ‘
\cdot
’.
We write
{\binampersand}\Delta
to denote that all assignments in
\Delta
have a non-deterministic type, i.e.,
{\binampersand}\Delta=w_{1}:{\binampersand}A_{1},\ldots,w_{n}:{\binampersand}A_{n}
, for some
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
.
The typing judgment
P\vdash\Delta
corresponds to the logical sequent
\vdash\Delta
for classical linear logic, which can be recovered by erasing processes and name assignments.

\mathtt{[T\cdot]}

{\bf 0}\vdash

\mathtt{[Tid]}

[x\leftrightarrow y]\vdash x{:}A,y{:}\overline{A}

P\vdash\Delta,y:{A}\quad Q\vdash\Delta^{\prime},x:B

\mathtt{[T\otimes]}

\overline{x}(y).(P\mid Q)\vdash\Delta,\Delta^{\prime},x:A\otimes B

P\vdash\Gamma,y:C,x:D

\mathtt{[T\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}]}

x(y).P\vdash\Gamma,x:C\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}D

\mathtt{[T\mathbf{1}]}

x.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\vdash x:\mathbf{1}

P\vdash\Delta

\mathtt{[T\bot]}

x.\mathtt{close};P\vdash x{:}\bot,\Delta

P\vdash\Delta\quad Q\vdash\Delta^{\prime}

\mathtt{[T\mid]}

P\mid Q\vdash\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}

P\vdash\Delta,x:\overline{A}\quad Q\vdash\Delta^{\prime},x:A

\mathtt{[Tcut]}

(\nu x)(P\mid Q)\vdash\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}

P\vdash\Delta,x:A

\mathtt{[T{\binampersand}_{d}^{x}]}

x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};P\vdash\Delta,x:{\binampersand}A

P\;{\vdash}\widetilde{w}:{\binampersand}\Delta,x:A

\mathtt{[T\oplus^{x}_{\widetilde{w}}]}

x.\mathtt{some}_{\widetilde{w}};P\vdash\widetilde{w}{:}{\binampersand}\Delta,x{:}\oplus A

\mathtt{[T{\binampersand}^{x}]}

x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\vdash x:{\binampersand}A

P\vdash{\binampersand}\Delta\qquad Q\;{\vdash}{\binampersand}\Delta

\mathtt{[T{\binampersand}]}

P\oplus Q\vdash{\binampersand}\Delta

Figure 11.
Typing rules for
\mathsf{s}\pi
.

Typing rules for processes correspond to proof rules in the logic; see Fig.
11
.
This way, Rule
\mathtt{[T\cdot]}
allows us to introduce the inactive process
{\bf 0}
. Rule
\mathtt{[Tid]}
interprets the identity axiom using the forwarder process. Rules
\mathtt{[T\otimes]}
and
\mathtt{[T\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}]}
type output and input of a name along a session, respectively.
Rules
\mathtt{[T\mathbf{1}]}
and
\mathtt{[T\bot]}
type the process constructs for session termination.
Rules
\mathtt{[Tcut]}
and
\mathtt{[T\mid]}
define cut and mix principles in the logic, which induce typing rules for independent and dependent parallel composition, respectively.

The last four rules in Fig.
11
are used to type process constructs related to non-determinism and failure.
Rules
\mathtt{[T{\binampersand}_{d}^{x}]}
and
\mathtt{[T{\binampersand}^{x}]}
introduce a session of type
{\binampersand}A
, which may produce a behavior of type
A
: while the former rule covers the case in which
x:A
is indeed available, the latter rule formalizes the case in which
x:A
is not available (i.e., a failure).
Rule
\mathtt{[T\oplus^{x}_{\widetilde{w}}]}
, accounts for the possibility of not being able
to consume the session
x:A
by considering sessions, the sequence of names
\widetilde{w}=w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
, different from
x
as potentially not available.
Rule
\mathtt{[T{\binampersand}]}
expresses non-deterministic choice of processes
P
and
Q
that implement non-deterministic behaviors only.

The type system enjoys type preservation, a result that
follows directly from the cut elimination property in the underlying logic; it ensures that the observable interface of a system is invariant under reduction.
The type system also ensures other properties for well-typed processes (e.g. global progress and confluence); see
[
CP17
]
for details.

Theorem 20

(Type Preservation
[
CP17
]
)
.

If
P\vdash\Delta
and
P\longrightarrow Q
then
Q\vdash\Delta
.

Having defined
\mathsf{s}\pi
, we now move on to define a correct translation from
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to
\mathsf{s}\pi
.

5.
A Correct Encoding

Having introduced the typed sequential calculi
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
(as well as the translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}~\cdot~{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}:\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}\to\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
) and the typed concurrent calculus
\mathsf{s}\pi
, in this section we show how to correctly translate
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
, using
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
as a stepping stone.

Before delving into technical details, we briefly discuss the significance of our encoding. As in Milner’s seminal work, our translation explains how interaction in
\pi
provides a principled interpretation of evaluation in
\lambda
. We tackle the challenging case in which evaluation and interaction are fail-prone and non-deterministic, effectively generalizing previous translations. Because our encoding preserves types, our developments also delineate a new connection between non-idempotent intersection types and logically motivated session types—indeed, our translation of functions as processes goes hand-in-hand with a translation on types (Fig.
17
), which reveals a new protocol-oriented interpretation of the non-idempotent intersections that govern functional resources.

As already mentioned, we shall proceed in two steps. We rely on the translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
from well-formed expressions in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to well-formed expressions in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
given in
\lx@sectionsign

3.4
.
As
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
share the same syntax of types, in this case the translation of types is the identity.
Then, the translation
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
(for some name
u
) transforms well-formed expressions in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to well-typed processes in
\mathsf{s}\pi
(cf. Fig.
12
).
We first define
encodability criteria
for translations, which include type preservation; these criteria lead to the notion of
correct encoding
(
\lx@sectionsign

5.1
). Then, in
\lx@sectionsign

5.2
we establish the correctness of the translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
(Corollary
29
);
finally, in
\lx@sectionsign

5.3
, we present the translation
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
and establish its correctness (Corollary
38
).

\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
\mathsf{s}\pi
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
\lx@sectionsign

3.4
\lx@sectionsign

5.3
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

Figure 12.
Summary of our approach.

5.1.
Encodability Criteria

We follow most of the criteria defined by Gorla in
[
Gor10
]
, a widely studied abstract framework for establishing the
quality
of translations.
A
language

\mathcal{L}
is defined as a pair containing a set of terms
\mathcal{M}
and a reduction semantics
\longrightarrow
on terms (with reflexive, transitive closure denoted
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\longrightarrow}}
). A behavioral equivalence on terms, denoted
\approx
, is also assumed.
Then, a
correct encoding
, defined next, concerns a translation of terms of a source language
\mathcal{L}_{1}
into terms of a target language
\mathcal{L}_{2}
that respects certain criteria.
The criteria in
[
Gor10
]
concern
untyped
languages; because we consider
typed
languages, we follow Kouzapas et al.
[
KPY19
]
in requiring also that translations preserve typability.

{defi}

[Correct Encoding]

Let
\mathcal{L}_{1}=(\mathcal{M},\longrightarrow_{1})

and

\mathcal{L}_{2}=(\mathcal{P},\longrightarrow_{2})

be two languages and let
\approx_{1}
be a behavioral equivalence on terms in
\mathcal{M}
.
We use
M,M^{\prime},\ldots
and
P,P^{\prime},\ldots
to range over elements in
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{P}
.
We say that a translation
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{P}
is a
correct encoding
if it satisfies the following criteria:

(1)

Type preservation:
For every well-typed
M
, it holds that
\llbracket M\rrbracket
is well-typed.

(2)

Operational Completeness:
For every
{M},{M}^{\prime}
, and
{M}^{\prime\prime}
such that
{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\longrightarrow}}_{1}{M}^{\prime}\approx_{1}{M}^{\prime\prime}
, it holds that
\llbracket{M}\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\longrightarrow}}_{2}\llbracket{M}^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
.

(3)

Operational Soundness:
For every
M
and
P
such that
\llbracket M\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\longrightarrow}}_{2}P
, there exist
M^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime\prime}
such that
M\longrightarrow^{*}_{1}M^{\prime}\approx_{1}M^{\prime\prime}
and
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\longrightarrow}}_{2}\llbracket M^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
.

(4)

Success Sensitiveness:
Let
\checkmark_{1}
and
\checkmark_{2}
denote a success predicate in
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{P}
, respectively.
For every
{M}
, it holds that
M\checkmark_{1}
if and only if
\llbracket M\rrbracket\checkmark_{2}
.

We briefly describe the criteria. First, type preservation is a natural requirement and a distinguishing aspect of our work, given that we always consider source and target calculi with types.
Operational completeness formalizes how reduction steps of a source term are mimicked by its corresponding translation in the target language;
\approx_{1}
conveniently abstracts away from source terms useful in the translation but which are not meaningful in comparisons.
Operational soundness concerns the opposite direction: it formalizes the correspondence between (i) the reductions of a target term obtained via the translation and (ii) the reductions of the corresponding source term. The role of
\approx_{1}
can be explained as in completeness.
Our use of the equivalence
\approx_{1}
for
\mathcal{M}_{1}
, rather than of an equivalence on
\mathcal{M}_{2}
, is a minor difference with respect to
[
Gor10
]
.
Finally, success sensitiveness complements completeness and soundness, which concern reductions and therefore do not contain information about observable behaviors.
The so-called success predicates
\checkmark_{1}
and
\checkmark_{2}
serve as a minimal notion of
observables
; the criterion then says that observability of success of a source term implies observability of success in the corresponding target term, and vice versa.

Besides these semantic criteria, we also consider
compositionality
, a syntactic criterion that requires that a composite source term is translated as the combination of the translations of its sub-terms.

5.2.
Correctness of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}

We prove that the translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
from
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
in
\lx@sectionsign

3.4
is a correct encoding, in the sense of Def.
5.1
.
Because our translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
is defined in terms of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
, it satisfies
weak compositionality
, in the sense of Parrow
[
Par08
]
.

5.2.1.
Type Preservation

We now prove that

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}

translates well-formed
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-expressions
into
well-formed expressions
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-expressions
(Theorem
24
).
Notice that because
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
share the same type syntax, there is no translation on types/contexts involved (i.e., an identity translation applies).

Next we define well formed preservation in the translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
from
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
. We rely on the prerequisite proof of type preservation in the translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
on the sub-calculi from
\lambda_{\oplus}
to
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
, and also on syntactic properties of the translation such as: (i) the property below guarantees that the translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
commutes with the linear head substitution; (ii) preservation of typability/well-formedness w.r.t. linear substitutions in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

Proposition 21
.

Let
M,N
be
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-terms. We have:

(1)

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}{\{\!|}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}N{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}/x{|\!\}}
.

(2)

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{x}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{x}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle
, where
\widetilde{x}=x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
is sequence of pairwise distinct fresh variables.

Proof 5.1
.

By induction of the structure of
M
.

Lemma 22

(Preservation under Linear Substitutions in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)
.

Let
{M}\in\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

(1)

Typing: If
\Gamma,x:\sigma^{k}\vdash{M}:\tau

then

\Gamma,x_{i}:\sigma^{k-1}\vdash{M}\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$x_{i}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle:\tau
.

(2)

Well-formedness: If
\Gamma,x:\sigma^{k}\models{M}:\tau

then
\Gamma,x_{i}:\sigma^{k-1}\models{M}\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$x_{i}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle:\tau
.

Proof 5.2
.

Standard by induction on the rules from Fig.
6
for item (1), and Fig.
7
for item (2).

The following example illustrates that the translation of a well-formed
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-expression is a well-formed
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-expression.

{exa}
[Cont. Example
2.3.2
]
Term
M_{2}=(\lambda x.x)(\Lbag y,z\Rbag)
is well-formed with a well-formedness judgment
y:\sigma,z:\sigma\models(\lambda x.x)(\Lbag y,z\Rbag):\sigma
. In Example
3.4
we showed that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}=((\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])(\Lbag y_{1},z_{1}\Rbag))[y_{1}\leftarrow y][z_{1}\leftarrow z]
which is well-formed with translated well-formed judgment
y:\sigma^{1},z:\sigma^{1}\models{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}:\sigma
.
The derivation is given below (using rules from Fig.
7
); we omit the labels of rule applications and concatenations with the empty bag, i.e., we write
\Lbag y_{1}\Rbag
instead of
\Lbag y_{1}\Rbag\cdot\mathtt{1}
.

x_{1}:\sigma\vdash x_{1}:\sigma

x_{1}:\sigma\models x_{1}:\sigma

x:\sigma^{1}\models x[x_{1}\leftarrow x]:\sigma

\models\lambda x.(x[x_{1}\leftarrow x]):\sigma\to\sigma

y_{1}:\sigma\vdash y_{1}:\sigma

y_{1}:\sigma\models y_{1}:\sigma

y_{1}:\sigma^{1}\models\Lbag y_{1}\Rbag:\sigma^{1}

z_{1}:\sigma\vdash z_{1}:\sigma

z_{1}:\sigma\models z_{1}:\sigma

z_{1}:\sigma^{1}\models\Lbag z_{1}\Rbag:\sigma^{1}

y_{1}:\sigma^{1},z_{1}:\sigma^{1}\models\Lbag y_{1}\Rbag\cdot\Lbag z_{1}\Rbag:\sigma^{2}

y_{1}:\sigma^{1},z_{1}:\sigma^{1}\models\lambda x.(x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])\Lbag y_{1},z_{1}\Rbag:\sigma

y:\sigma^{1},z_{1}:\sigma^{1}\models\lambda x.(x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])\Lbag y_{1},z_{1}\Rbag[y_{1}\leftarrow y]:\sigma

y:\sigma^{1},z:\sigma^{1}\models\lambda x.(x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])\Lbag y_{1},z_{1}\Rbag[y_{1}\leftarrow y][z_{1}\leftarrow z]:\sigma

As the translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
for

\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-terms is defined in terms of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
,
it is natural that preservation of well-formedness under
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
(Theorem
24
) relies on the preservation of well-formedness under
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
, given next.

To state well-formedness preservation, we use
\Gamma^{\dagger}
, the core context of
\Gamma
(Def.
3.3
). In the following property, we use an additional condition on
\Gamma^{\dagger}
, which reflects the fact that intersection types get “flattened” by virtue of the translation. The condition, denoted
\widehat{\Gamma}^{\dagger}
, is defined whenever
\Gamma^{\dagger}
contains only unary multisets as follows: if

x:\sigma^{1}\in\Gamma^{\dagger}
for all
x\in\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma^{\dagger})
, then
x:\sigma\in\widehat{\Gamma}^{\dagger}
.

Lemma 23

(Well-formedness preservation for
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
)
.

Let
B
and
\mathbb{M}
be a bag and an expression in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, respectively. Also, let
\Gamma
be a context such that
\widehat{\Gamma}^{\dagger}
is defined. We have:

(1)

If
\Gamma\models B:\pi

then
\widehat{\Gamma}^{\dagger}\models{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}B{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}:\pi
.

(2)

If
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\sigma

then
\widehat{\Gamma}^{\dagger}\models{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}:\sigma
.

Proof 5.3

(Proof (Sketch))
.

By mutual induction on the typing derivations
\Gamma\models B:\sigma
and
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\sigma
. The proof of item (1) follows mostly by induction hypothesis, by analyzing the rule applied (Fig.
4
).
The proof of item (2), also follows by analyzing the rule applied, but it is more delicate, especially when treating cases involving Rules
\mathtt{[FS:app]}
or
\mathtt{[FS:ex\text{-}sub]}
, for which the size of the bag does not match the number of occurrences of variables in the expression. See App.
C.1.2
for full details.

Theorem 24

(Well-formedness Preservation for
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
)
.

Let
B
and
\mathbb{M}
be a bag and an expression in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, respectively.

(1)

If
\Gamma\models B:\pi

then
\Gamma^{\dagger}\models{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}B{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}:\pi
.

(2)

If
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\sigma

then
\Gamma^{\dagger}\models{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}:\sigma
.

Proof 5.4

(Proof (Sketch))
.

By mutual induction on the typing derivations
\Gamma\models B:\sigma
and
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\sigma
. Note that for a bag
B
, since the first part of translation consists in sharing the free variables of
B
, we will work with the translated bag
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}B{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}B\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{x_{1}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x_{1}$}\rangle\ldots\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{x_{k}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x_{k}$}\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[\widetilde{x_{1}}\leftarrow x_{1}]\ldots[\widetilde{x_{k}}\leftarrow x_{k}]
, and the rest of the proof depends on Proposition
21
that moves linear substitutions outside
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
, then Lemma
22
that guarantees preservation of typability/well-formedness under linear substitutions, and Lemma
23
for treating the closed translation. The dependency extends to the proof of item (2), for expressions. The full proof can be found in App.
C.1.2
.

5.2.2.
Operational Correspondence: Completeness and Soundness

Def.
5.1
states operational completeness and soundness over the reflexive, transitive closure of the reduction rules. However, in the case of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
, we prove completeness and soundness for a single reduction step (cf. Fig.
14
). This is sufficient: by the diamond property (Proposition
2.2
) a result stated for
\longrightarrow
can be extended easily to
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\longrightarrow}}
, by induction on the length of the reduction sequence. (The result is immediate when the length is zero.)

We rely on a
structural equivalence
over
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-expressions, denoted
\equiv_{\lambda}
, which is the least congruence satisfying
\alpha
-conversion and satisfying the identities in Fig.
13
. This congruence allows us to move explicit substitutions to the right of the term and to ignore explicit substitutions of a variable
x
for empty bags in a term that does not contain
x
.

{exa}

Consider the failure term
M=\mathtt{fail}^{y,y,z}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
.
Since
\mathsf{size}(\mathtt{1})=0
, the term
M
cannot reduce using Rule
\mathtt{[R:Cons_{2}]}
, which requires that the size of the bag is greater than 0. Instead, we use the structural equivalence identity in Fig.
13
:

\mathtt{fail}^{y,y,z}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\equiv_{\lambda}\mathtt{fail}^{y,y,z}
.

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\hspace{0.5cm}}l}M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle&\equiv_{\lambda}&M\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\text{(if $x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(M)$)}\\
MB_{1}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B_{2}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle&\equiv_{\lambda}&(M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B_{2}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)B_{1}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\text{(if $x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(B_{1})$)}\\
M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B_{2}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle&\equiv_{\lambda}&(M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B_{2}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\text{(if $x\neq y,x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(B_{1})$ and $y\not\in\mathsf{fv}(B_{2})$)}\\
M\equiv_{\lambda}M^{\prime}&\Rightarrow&C[M]\equiv_{\lambda}C[M^{\prime}]\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt\\
\mathbb{M}\equiv_{\lambda}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}&\Rightarrow&D[\mathbb{M}]\equiv_{\lambda}D[\mathbb{M}^{\prime}]\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt\end{array}

Figure 13.
