Theorem 25

(Operational Completeness)
.

Let
\mathbb{M},\mathbb{N}
be well-formed
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expressions.
Suppose
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R]}}\mathbb{M}
.

(1)

If
\mathtt{[R]}=\mathtt{[R:Beta]}
then
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}\longrightarrow^{\leq 2}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
;

(2)

If
\mathtt{[R]}=\mathtt{[R:Fetch]}
then
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}\longrightarrow^{+}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
, for some
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbb{M}\equiv_{\lambda}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
.

(3)

If
\mathtt{[R]}\neq\mathtt{[R:Beta]}
and
\mathtt{[R]}\neq\mathtt{[R:Fetch]}
then
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
.

Proof 5.5

(Proof (Sketch))
.

By induction on the rules from Figure
2
applied to infer
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R]}}\mathbb{M}
. We analyse the reduction depending on whether
\mathtt{[R]}
is either
\mathtt{[R:Beta]}
, or
\mathtt{[R:Fetch]}
, or neither. In the case the rule applied is
\mathtt{[Beta]}
, then
\mathbb{N}=(\lambda x.M^{\prime})\Lbag B\Rbag
and
\mathbb{M}=M^{\prime}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
. When applying the translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
to
\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbb{M}
we obtain:

•

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}=((\lambda x.{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\langle{\widetilde{y}/x}\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow x]){\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}B^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}})[\widetilde{x_{1}}\leftarrow x_{1}]\ldots[\widetilde{x_{k}}\leftarrow x_{k}]

•

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B^{\prime}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[\widetilde{x_{1}}\leftarrow x_{1}]\ldots[\widetilde{x_{k}}\leftarrow x_{k}]

where
B^{\prime}
and
M^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
stand for the renamings of
B
and
M^{\prime}
, respectively, after sharing the multiple occurrences of their free/bound variables (Def.
3.4
). Note that

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{}_{\mathtt{[RS:Beta]}}({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\langle{\widetilde{y}/x}\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small${\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}B^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)[\widetilde{x_{1}}\leftarrow x_{1}]\ldots[\widetilde{x_{k}}\leftarrow x_{k}]:=\mathbb{L},

and according to rules in Fig.
5
, the remaining reduction depends upon the characteristics of the bag
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}B^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
:

(1)

\mathsf{size}({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}B^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}})=\#(x,M^{{}^{\prime\prime}})=k\geq 1
.
Then,

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{}_{\mathtt{[RS:Beta]}}\mathbb{L}\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[RS:ex\text{-}sub]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
.

(2)

Otherwise,
\mathbb{L}
can be further expanded, the “otherwise case” of the translation of explicit substitutions, such that

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{}_{\mathtt{[RS:Beta]}}({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\langle{\widetilde{y}/x}\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small${\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}B^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)[\widetilde{x_{1}}\leftarrow x_{1}]\ldots[\widetilde{x_{k}}\leftarrow x_{k}]=\mathbb{L}={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}.

In the case the rule applied is
\mathtt{[R:Fetch]}
, the proof depends on the size
n
of the bag. The interesting case is when the bag
B
has only one component (i.e.,
n=1
): from
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[F:Fetch]}}\mathbb{N}
we have that
\mathbb{N}=M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag N_{1}\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
and
\mathbb{M}=M{\{\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$1$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
. We need to use the congruence
\equiv_{\lambda}
to obtain
\mathbb{M}=M{\{\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$1$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\equiv_{\lambda}M{\{\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\}}:=\mathbb{M^{\prime}}
and then conclude that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{M^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
.
The analysis for the other cases is also done by inspecting the structure of expressions and bags. The full proof can be found in App.
C.2
.

We establish soundness for a single reduction step. As we discussed for completeness, the property generalizes to multiple steps.

Theorem 26

(Operational Soundness)
.

Let
\mathbb{N}
be a well-formed
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expression.
Suppose
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}
. Then, there exists
\mathbb{N}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R]}}\mathbb{N}^{\prime}
and

(1)

If
\mathtt{[R]}=\mathtt{[R:Beta]}
then
\mathbb{L}\longrightarrow^{\leq 1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{N}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
;

(2)

If
\mathtt{[R]}\neq\mathtt{[R:Beta]}
then
\mathbb{L}\longrightarrow^{*}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{N}^{\prime\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
, for
\mathbb{N}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathbb{N}^{\prime}\equiv_{\lambda}\mathbb{N}^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof 5.6

(Proof (Sketch))
.

By induction on the structure of
\mathbb{N}
and inspecting the rules from Fig.
5
that can be applied in
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
. The interesting cases happen when
\mathbb{N}
is either an application
\mathbb{N}=(M\ B)
or an explicit substitution
\mathbb{N}=M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)
. The former is reducible when
\mathbb{N}
is an instance of
\mathtt{[R:Beta]}
or when
M=\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
and
\mathbb{N}
is an instance of
\mathtt{[R:Cons_{1}]}
. The latter, for
\mathbb{N}=M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)
, the proof is split in several subcases depending whether: (i) size of the bag
\mathsf{size}(B)=\#(x,M)\geq 1
, and three possible reductions can take place
\mathtt{[RS:lin\text{-}fetch]}
,
\mathtt{[RS:Cons_{3}]}
and
\mathtt{[RS:Cont]}
, depending if
M
is a failing term or not; (ii)
\mathsf{size}(B)\neq\#(x,M)
or
\mathsf{size}(B)=0
, and the proof follows either applying Rule
\mathtt{[RS:Fail]}
or by induction hypothesis. The full proof can be found in App.
C.2
.

5.2.3.
Success Sensitiveness

We now consider success sensitiveness, a property that complements (and relies on) operational completeness and soundness. For the purposes of the proof, we consider the extension of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
with dedicated constructs and predicates that specify success.

{defi}

We extend the syntax of terms for
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
with the same
\checkmark
construct.
In both cases, we assume
\checkmark
is well formed.
Also, we define
\mathsf{head}(\checkmark)=\checkmark
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\checkmark{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}=\checkmark

An expression
\mathbb{M}
has success, denoted
\mathbb{M}\Downarrow_{\checkmark}
, when there is a sequence of reductions from
\mathbb{M}
that leads to an expression that includes a summand that contains an occurrence of
\checkmark
in head position.

{defi}

[Success in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
]

In
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, we define

\mathbb{M}\Downarrow_{\checkmark}\iff\exists M_{1},\cdots,M_{k}.~\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow^{*}M_{1}+\cdots+M_{k}\text{ and }\mathsf{head}(M_{j})=\checkmark,

for some
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
.

{defi}

[Head of an expression]
We extend Def.
3.2
from terms to expressions as follows:

\mathsf{head}_{\sum}(\mathbb{M})=\begin{cases}\mathsf{head}(M_{i})&\text{if }\mathsf{head}(M_{i})=\mathsf{head}(M_{j})\text{ for all }M_{i},M_{j}\in\mathbb{M}\\
\text{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Proposition 27

(Preservation of head term)
.

The head of a term is preserved when applying the translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
, i.e.,

\forall M\in\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}.~~\mathsf{head}(M)=\checkmark\iff\mathsf{head}_{\sum}({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}M{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}})=\checkmark.

Proof 5.7

(Proof (Sketch))
.

By induction on the structure of
M
considering the extension of the language established in Def.
5.2.3
. See App.
C.3
for details.

Theorem 28

(Success Sensitivity)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a well-formed
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-expression.
Then,

\mathbb{M}\Downarrow_{\checkmark}\iff{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}\Downarrow_{\checkmark}.

Proof 5.8

(Proof (Sketch))
.

By induction on the structure of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expressions. The if-case follows from operational soundness (Thm.
26
) by analyzing a reductions starting from
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
. Reciprocally, the only-if-case follows by operational completeness (Thm.
25
), analyzing reductions starting from
\mathbb{M}
. See App.
C.3
for details.

We have the corollary below, which follows from Theorems
24
,

25
,

26
, and

28
:

Corollary 29
.

Our translation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}

is a correct encoding, in the sense of Def.
5.1
.

5.3.
From
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to
\mathsf{s}\pi

We now define our translation of
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
, and establish its correctness.
As usual in translations of
\lambda
into
\pi
, we use a name
u
to provide the behavior of the translated expression.
In our case,
u
is a non-deterministic session: the translated expression can be available or not; this is signalled by prefixes ‘
u.\overline{\mathtt{some}}
’
and
‘
u.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
’, respectively.
Notice that every (free) variable
x
in a
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term
M
becomes a name
x
in its corresponding process
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
and is assigned an appropriate session type.

5.3.1.
An Auxiliary Translation

Before introducing
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
, we first discuss the translation
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{u}:\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}\rightarrow\mathsf{s}\pi
, i.e., the translation in which the source language does not include failures.
This auxiliary translation, shown in Fig.
15
, is given for pedagogical purposes: it allows us to gradually discuss several key design decisions in
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
.

\displaystyle\llbracket x\rrbracket_{u}

\displaystyle=x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};[x\leftrightarrow u]

\displaystyle\llbracket\lambda x.M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\rrbracket_{u}

\displaystyle=u.\overline{\mathtt{some}};u(x).\llbracket M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\rrbracket_{u}

\displaystyle\llbracket M\ B\rrbracket_{u}

\displaystyle=\hskip-10.00002pt\bigoplus_{\hskip 8.19447ptB_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}\hskip-10.00002pt(\nu v)(\llbracket M\rrbracket_{v}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).(x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(B_{i})};\llbracket B_{i}\rrbracket_{x}\mid[v\leftrightarrow u]))

\displaystyle\llbracket M[x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\leftarrow x]\rrbracket_{u}

\displaystyle=x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x(x_{1}).\cdots.x(x_{k}).x.\mathtt{close};\llbracket M\rrbracket_{u}

\displaystyle\llbracket M[\leftarrow x]\rrbracket_{u}

\displaystyle=x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x.\mathtt{close};\llbracket M\rrbracket_{u}

\displaystyle\llbracket\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B\rrbracket_{x}

\displaystyle=\overline{x}(x_{1}).(x_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(B)};\llbracket M\rrbracket_{x_{1}}\ |\ \llbracket B\rrbracket_{x})

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathtt{1}\rrbracket_{x}

\displaystyle=x.\overline{\mathtt{close}}

\displaystyle\llbracket M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\rrbracket_{u}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}(\nu x)(\llbracket M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\rrbracket_{u}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(B_{i})};\llbracket B_{i}\rrbracket_{x})

\displaystyle\llbracket M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}\rrbracket_{u}

\displaystyle=(\nu x)(\llbracket M\rrbracket_{u}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(N)};\llbracket N\rrbracket_{x})

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N}\rrbracket_{u}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathbb{M}\rrbracket_{u}\oplus\llbracket\mathbb{N}\rrbracket_{u}

Figure 15.
An auxiliary translation of
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
, without failures

We describe each case of the translation
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{u}
, focusing on the rôle of non-deterministic sessions (expressed using prefixes ‘
x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}
’ and ‘
x.\mathtt{some}_{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})}
’ in
\mathsf{s}\pi
):

•

\llbracket x\rrbracket_{u}
: Because sessions are non-deterministically available, the translation first confirms that the behavior along
x
is available; subsequently, the forwarder process induces a substitution
\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$x$}\!/\mbox{\small$u$}\}
.

•

\llbracket\lambda x.M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\rrbracket_{u}
: As in the case of variables, the translation first confirms the behavior along
u
before receiving a name, which will be used in the translation of
M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
, discussed next.

•

\llbracket M\ B\rrbracket_{u}
: This process models the application of
M
to bag
B
as a non-deterministic choice in the order in which the elements of
B
are substituted into
M
. Substituting each
B_{i}
involves a protocol in which the translation of a term
\lambda x.M^{\prime}[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
within
M
confirms its own availability, before and after the exchange of the name
x
, on which the translation of
B_{i}
is spawned. This protocol uses the fact that
M\ B
does not reduce to failure, i.e., there is no lack or excess of resources in
B
.

•

\llbracket M[x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\leftarrow x]\rrbracket_{u}
: The translation first confirms the availability of the behavior along
x
.
Then, it receives along
x
a name for each
x_{i}
: these received names will be used to synchronize with the translation of bags (see below). Subsequently, the protocol on
x
safely terminates and the translation of
M
is executed.

•

\llbracket M[\leftarrow x]\rrbracket_{u}
: When there are no variables to be shared with
x
, the translation simply confirms the behavior on
x
, close the protocol immediately after, and executes the translation of
M
.

•

\llbracket\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B\rrbracket_{x}
: The translation of a non-empty bag essentially makes each element available in its corresponding order.
This way, for the first element
M
a name
x_{1}
is sent over
x
; the translation of
{M[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\leftarrow x]}
, discussed above, must send a confirmation on
x_{1}
before the translation of
M
is executed. After these exchanges, the translation of the rest of the bag is spawned.

•

\llbracket\mathtt{1}\rrbracket_{x}
: In line with the previous item, the translation of the empty bag simply closes the name
x
; this signals that there are no (further) elements in the bag and that all synchronizations are complete.

•

\llbracket M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\rrbracket_{u}
: In this case, the translation is a sum involving the parallel composition of (i) the translation of each element
B_{i}
in the bag and (ii) the translation of
M
. Observe that a fresh name
x
is created to enable synchronization between these two processes. Also, as in previous cases, notice how the translation of
B_{i}
must first confirm its availability along
x
.

•

\llbracket M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}\rrbracket_{u}
: This translation essentially executes the translations of
M
and
N
in parallel, with a caveat: the translation of
N
depends on the availability of a behavior along
x
, to be produced within the translation of
M
.

•

\llbracket\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N}\rrbracket_{u}
: This translation homomorphically preserves the non-determinism between
M
and
N
.

{exa}

Consider the
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
-term

M_{0}=(\lambda x.M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x])\Lbag N_{1},N_{2}\Rbag
.
Writing
\mathsf{fv}(B)
to denote the free variables in
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
, the process

\llbracket M_{0}\rrbracket_{u}
is as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket M_{0}\rrbracket_{u}=

\displaystyle\llbracket(\lambda x.M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x])\Lbag N_{1},N_{2}\Rbag\rrbracket_{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nu v)(\llbracket\lambda x.M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]\rrbracket_{v}\mid\underbrace{v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).(x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(B)};\llbracket\Lbag N_{1},N_{2}\Rbag\rrbracket_{x}\mid[v\leftrightarrow u])}_{P_{1}})

\displaystyle\oplus

\displaystyle(\nu v)(\llbracket\lambda x.M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]\rrbracket_{v}\mid\underbrace{v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).(x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(B)};\llbracket\Lbag N_{2},N_{1}\Rbag\rrbracket_{x}\mid[v\leftrightarrow u])}_{P_{2}})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle(\nu v)(v.\overline{\mathtt{some}};v(x).x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x(x_{1}).x(x_{2}).x.\mathtt{close};\llbracket M\rrbracket{v}\mid P_{1})

\displaystyle\oplus

\displaystyle(\nu v)(v.\overline{\mathtt{some}};v(x).x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x(x_{1}).x(x_{2}).x.\mathtt{close};\llbracket M\rrbracket{v}\mid P_{2})

The translation immediately opens up a non-deterministic choice with two alternatives, corresponding to the bag of size 2.
Because of non-collapsing non-determinism, after some reductions, this amounts to accounting for the two different orders in which
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
can be extracted from the bag.

\displaystyle\llbracket M_{0}\rrbracket_{u}\longrightarrow^{*}

\displaystyle(\nu x)(x(x_{1}).x(x_{2}).x.\mathtt{close};\llbracket M\rrbracket_{u}\mid\llbracket\Lbag N_{1},N_{2}\Rbag\rrbracket_{x})

\displaystyle\oplus

\displaystyle(\nu x)(x(x_{1}).x(x_{2}).x.\mathtt{close};\llbracket M\rrbracket_{u}\mid\llbracket\Lbag N_{2},N_{1}\Rbag\rrbracket_{x})

We show further reductions for one of the processes, which we will denote
R
, for
R=(\nu x)(x(x_{1}).x(x_{2}).x.\mathtt{close};\llbracket M\rrbracket_{u}\mid\llbracket\Lbag N_{1},N_{2}\Rbag\rrbracket_{x})
, in the resulting sum (reductions for the other process are similar):

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\nu x)(x(x_{1}).x(x_{2}).x.\mathtt{close};\llbracket M\rrbracket_{u}\mid\llbracket\Lbag N_{1},N_{2}\Rbag\rrbracket_{x})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle(\nu x)(x(x_{1}).x(x_{2}).x.\mathtt{close};\llbracket M\rrbracket_{u}\mid\overline{x}(x_{1}).(x_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(N_{1})};\llbracket N_{1}\rrbracket_{x_{1}}\ |\

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\overline{x}(x_{2}).(x_{2}.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(N_{2})};\llbracket N_{2}\rrbracket_{x_{2}}\ |\ x.\overline{\mathtt{close}})))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{*}~

\displaystyle(\nu x_{1},x_{2})(\llbracket M\rrbracket_{u}\mid x_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(N_{1})};\llbracket N_{1}\rrbracket_{x_{1}}\ |\ x_{2}.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(N_{2})};\llbracket N_{2}\rrbracket_{x_{2}})

5.3.2.
The Translation

The translation
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{x}
leverages non-deterministic sessions in
\mathsf{s}\pi
to give a concurrent interpretation of
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
, the non-deterministic (but fail-free) sub-calculus of
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
. In a nutshell, non-deterministic sessions entail the explicit confirmation of the availability of a name’s behavior, via synchronizations of a prefix ‘
x.\mathtt{some}_{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})}
’ with a corresponding prefix ‘
x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}
’. Clearly,
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{x}
under-utilizes the expressivity of
\mathsf{s}\pi
: in processes resulting from
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{x}
, no prefix ‘
x.\mathtt{some}_{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})}
’ will ever synchronize with a prefix ‘
x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
’.
Indeed, because terms in
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
never reduce to failure,
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{x}
should not account for such failures.

We may now introduce
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
, our translation of the fail-prone calculus
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
. It builds upon the structure of
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{x}
to account for failures in expressions due to the lack or excess of resources. To this end, as we will see,
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
does exploit prefixes ‘
x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
’ to signal failures.

Translating Expressions

We introduce the translation
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
, which will be shown to be a correct encoding, according to the criteria given in
\lx@sectionsign

5.1
.

{defi}

[From
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
: Expressions]

Let
u
be a name.
The translation
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}:\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}\rightarrow\mathsf{s}\pi
is defined in Fig.
16
.

We discuss the most interesting aspects of the translation in Fig.
16
, in particular how the possibility of failure (lack or excess of resources in bags) induces differences with respect to the translation in Fig.
15
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}x{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle=x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};[x\leftrightarrow u]

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\lambda x.M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle=u.\overline{\mathtt{some}};u(x).{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M\,B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}(\nu v)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}))

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}(\nu x)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle=x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}.\overline{x}(y_{1}).\Big(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{1}.\mathtt{close}\!\mid\!x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x.\mathtt{some}_{u,(\mathsf{fv}(M)\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2}\})};x(x_{1}).

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt.x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}.\overline{x}(y_{2}).\big(y_{2}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{2}.\mathtt{close}\!\mid\!x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x.\mathtt{some}_{u,(\mathsf{fv}(M)\setminus\{x_{2}\})};x(x_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.00006pt.x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(y).(y.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(M)};y.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})~\big)\Big)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M[\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle=x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}.\overline{x}(y).(y.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(M)};y.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[t]{l}x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B)};x(y_{i}).x.\mathtt{some}_{y_{i},\mathsf{fv}(\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B)};x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(x_{i})\\[2.84526pt]
\qquad.(x_{i}.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(M)};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{i}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\mid y_{i}.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\end{array}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}

\displaystyle=x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x(y).(y.\overline{\mathtt{some}};y.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{fail}^{x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle=u.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid x_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid\cdots\mid x_{k}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle=(\nu x)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(N)};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

Figure 16.
Translating
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expressions into
\mathsf{s}\pi
processes.

Most salient differences can be explained by looking at the translation of the application
M\,B
. Indeed, the sources of failure in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
concern a mismatch between the number of variable occurrences in
M
and the number of resources present in
B
. Both
M
and
B
can fail on their own, and our translation into
\mathsf{s}\pi
must capture this mutual dependency. Let us recall the translation given in Fig.
15
:

\llbracket M\ B\rrbracket_{u}=\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}(\nu v)(\llbracket M\rrbracket_{v}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).(x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(B_{i})};\llbracket B_{i}\rrbracket_{x}\mid[v\leftrightarrow u]))

The corresponding translation in Fig.
16
is seemingly simpler:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M\,B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}(\nu v)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}))

Indeed, the main difference is the prefix ‘
x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(B_{i})}
’, which is present in process
\llbracket M\ B\rrbracket_{u}
but is not explicit in process
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M\,B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
. Intuitively, such a prefix denotes the dependency of
B
on
M
; because terms in
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
do not fail, we can be certain that a corresponding confirming prefix ‘
x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}
’ will be available to spawn every
\llbracket B_{i}\rrbracket_{x}
. When moving to
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, however, this is not the case:
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}
may fail to provide the expected number of corresponding confirmations. For this reason, the role of prefix ‘
x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(B_{i})}
’ in
\llbracket M\ B\rrbracket_{u}
is implemented within process
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}
. As a consequence, the translations for sharing terms (
M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
and
M[\leftarrow x]
) and for bags (
\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B
and
\mathtt{1}
) are more involved in the case of failure.

With this motivation for
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M\,B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
in mind, we discuss the remaining entries in Fig.
16
:

•

Translations for
x
and
\lambda x.M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
are exactly as in Fig.
15
:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}x{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};[x\leftrightarrow u]\qquad\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\lambda x.M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=u.\overline{\mathtt{some}};u(x).{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

•

Similarly as
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M\,B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
, discussed above, the translation of
M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
is more compact than the one in Fig.
15
, because confirmations for each of the elements of the bag are handled within their respective translations:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}(\nu x)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x})

•

As anticipated, the translation of
M[x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\leftarrow x]
is more involved than before. For simplicity, let us discuss the representative case when
k=2
(two shared variables):

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle=x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}.\overline{x}(y_{1}).\Big(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{1}.\mathtt{close}\!\mid\!x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x.\mathtt{some}_{u,(\mathsf{fv}(M)\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2}\})};x(x_{1}).

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003ptx.\overline{\mathtt{some}}.\overline{x}(y_{2}).\big(y_{2}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{2}.\mathtt{close}\!\mid\!x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x.\mathtt{some}_{u,(\mathsf{fv}(M)\setminus\{x_{2}\})};x(x_{2}).

\displaystyle\hskip 28.00006ptx.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(y).(y.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(M)};y.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})~\big)\Big)

This process is meant to synchronize with the translation of a bag. After confirming the presence of a behavior on name
x
, an auxiliary name
y_{i}
is sent to signal that there are elements to be substituted. This name implements a short protocol that allows us to check for lack of resources in the bag. These steps on
y_{i}
are followed by another confirmation and also a request for confirmation of behavior along
x
; this represents that the name can fail in one of two ways, capturing the mutual dependency between
M
and the bag mentioned above. Once these two steps on
x
have succeeded, it is finally safe for the process to receive a name
x_{i}
. This process is repeated for each shared variable to ensure safe communication of the elements of the bag. The last line shows the very final step: a name
y
is communicated to ensure that there are no further elements in the bag; in such a case,
y
fails and the failure is propagated to
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
. The prefix ‘
x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
’ signals the end of the shared variables, and is meant to synchronize with the translation of
\mathtt{1}
, the last element of the bag. If the bag has elements that still need to be synchronized then the failure along
x
is propagated to the remaining resources within the translation of the bag.

•

The translation of
M[\leftarrow x]
corresponds to the final step in the translation just discussed:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M[\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}.\overline{x}(y).(y.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(M)};y.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})

•

The translation of the non-empty bag
\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B
is as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}

\displaystyle=x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B)};x(y_{i}).x.\mathtt{some}_{y_{i},\mathsf{fv}(\Lbag M\Rbag\cdot B)};x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\qquad.(x_{i}.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(M)};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{i}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\mid y_{i}.\overline{\mathtt{none}})

Notice how this process operates hand in hand with the translation of
M[x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\leftarrow x]
.
The process first waits for its behavior to be confirmed; then, the auxiliary name
y_{i}
is received from the translation of
M[x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\leftarrow x]
. The name
y_{i}
fails immediately to signal that there are more resources in the bag. Name
x
then confirms its behavior and awaits its behavior to be confirmed. Subsequently, a name
x_{i}
is sent: this is the name on which the translation of
M
will be made available to the application. After that, name
x
is used in the translation of
B
, the rest of the bag.

•

The translation of
\mathtt{1}
operates aligned with the translations just discussed, exploiting the fact that in fail-free reductions the last element of the bag must be
\mathtt{1}
:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}=x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x(y).(y.\overline{\mathtt{some}};y.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})

This process relays the information that the translated empty bag is no longer able to provide resources for further substitutions. It first waits upon a correct behavior followed by the reception of a name
y
. The process then confirms its behavior along
y
: this signals that there are no further resources. Concurrently, name
x
waits for a confirmation of a behavior and ends with ‘
x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
’, thus signaling the failure of producing further behaviors.

•

The explicit failure term
\mathtt{fail}^{x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}}
is not part of
\widehat{\lambda}_{\oplus}
and so it was not covered in Fig.
15
. Its translation is straightforward:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{fail}^{x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=u.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid x_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid\cdots\mid x_{k}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}

The failure term is translated as the non-availability of a behavior along name
u
, composed with the non-availability of sessions along the names/variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
encapsulated by the source failure term.

•

The translations for
M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}
and
\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N}
are exactly as before:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=(\nu x)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(N)};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x})\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

5.3.3.
Examples

Before presenting the session types associated to our translation
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
, we present a series of examples that illustrate different possibilities in a step-by-step fashion:

•

No failure: an explicit substitution that is provided an adequate amount of resources;

•

Failure due to excess of resources in the bag;

•

Failure due to lack of resources in the bag.

We first discuss the translation of a term in which there is no failure. In that follows, we refer to a specific reduction by adding a number as in, e.g., ‘
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{3}]}
’.

{exa}

[No Failure]
Let us consider the well-formed
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term
N[x_{1}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag M\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
, where, for simplicity, we assume that
\mathsf{fv}(N)\setminus\{x_{1}\}=\mathsf{fv}(M)=\emptyset
.
As we have seen,
N[x_{1}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag M\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow N{\langle\!|}M/x{|\!\rangle}
.
We discuss reduction steps for
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N[x_{1}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag M\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
, highlighting in
blue
relevant prefixes.
First, we have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N[x_{1}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$\Lbag M\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=

\displaystyle(\nu x)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N[x_{1}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Lbag M\Rbag{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nu x)\big({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}}.\overline{x}(y_{1}).(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{1}.\mathtt{close}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x.\mathtt{some}_{u};

\displaystyle.x(x_{1}).x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(y).(y.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}};y.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})~)

\displaystyle\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset}};x(y_{1}).x.\mathtt{some}_{y_{1}};x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(x_{1})

\displaystyle.(x_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{1}}\mid y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x(y).(y.\overline{\mathtt{some}};y.\overline{\mathtt{close}}

\displaystyle\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}))\big)

A detailed description of the reduction steps follows:

•

Reduction
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{1}]}
concerns the name
x
confirming its behavior (see highlighted prefixes above), and reduction
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{2}]}
concerns the communication of name
y_{1}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N[x_{1}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$\Lbag M\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{1}]}

\displaystyle(\nu x)({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\overline{x}(y_{1})}.\big(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{1}.\mathtt{close}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x.\mathtt{some}_{u};x(x_{1}).

\displaystyle.x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(y).(y.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}};y.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})~\big)

\displaystyle\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x(y_{1})}.x.\mathtt{some}_{y_{1}};x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(x_{1}).(x_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{1}}

\displaystyle\mid y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x(y).(y.\overline{\mathtt{some}};y.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{2}]}

\displaystyle(\nu x,y_{1})(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{1}.\mathtt{close}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}};x.\mathtt{some}_{u};x(x_{1}).

\displaystyle.x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(y).(y.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}};y.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})~

\displaystyle\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x.\mathtt{some}_{y_{1}}};x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(x_{1}).(x_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{1}}

\displaystyle\mid\!y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\!\mid\!x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x(y).(y.\overline{\mathtt{some}};y.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\!\mid\!x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})))\ (:=P)

•

Reduction
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{3}]}
concerns
x
confirming its behavior, which signals that there are variables free for substitution in the translated term. In the opposite direction,
reduction
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{4}]}
signals that there are elements in the bag which are available for substitution in the translated term.

\displaystyle P\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{3}]}

\displaystyle(\nu x,y_{1})(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{1}.\mathtt{close}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{u}};x(x_{1}).

\displaystyle.x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(y).(y.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}};y.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})~

\displaystyle\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}};\overline{x}(x_{1}).(x_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{1}}\mid y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x(y).

\displaystyle(y.\overline{\mathtt{some}};y.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{4}]}

\displaystyle(\nu x,y_{1})(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{1}.\mathtt{close}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x(x_{1})}.x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(y).(y.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}};y.\mathtt{close};

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\displaystyle\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\overline{x}(x_{1})}.(x_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{1}}\mid y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}

\displaystyle\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x(y).(y.\overline{\mathtt{some}};y.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})))\hskip 28.45274pt(:=Q)

•

Given the confirmations in the previous two steps, reduction
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{5}]}
can now safely communicate a name
x_{1}
. This reduction synchronizes the shared variable
x_{1}
with the first element in the bag.

\displaystyle Q\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{5}]}

\displaystyle(\nu x,y_{1},x_{1})(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{1}.\mathtt{close}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}};\overline{x}(y).(y.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}};y.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\mid x_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{1}}\mid y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset}};x(y).(y.\overline{\mathtt{some}};y.\overline{\mathtt{close}}

\displaystyle\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}))\hskip 28.45274pt(:=R)

•

Reduction
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{6}]}
concerns
x
confirming its behavior. At this point, we could have alternatively performed a reduction on name
y_{1}
. We chose to discuss all reductions on
x
first; thanks to confluence this choice has no effect on the overall behavior. Reduction
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{7}]}
communicates name
y
along
x
.

\displaystyle R\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{6}]}

\displaystyle(\nu x,y_{1},x_{1})(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{1}.\mathtt{close}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\overline{x}(y)}.(y.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}};y.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}~

\displaystyle\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\mid x_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{1}}\mid y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x(y)}.(y.\overline{\mathtt{some}};y.\overline{\mathtt{close}}

\displaystyle\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{7}]}

\displaystyle(\nu x,y,y_{1},x_{1})(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{1}.\mathtt{close}\mid y.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}};y.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}}~

\displaystyle\mid x_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{1}}\mid y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid y.\overline{\mathtt{some}};y.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset}};x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\hskip 22.76228pt(:=S)

•

Reduction
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{8}]}
cancels the behavior along
x
, meaning that there are no more free variables to synchronize with. Subsequently, reduction
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{9}]}
cancels the behavior along
y_{1}
: at the beginning, when
y_{1}
was received, the encoded bag had the element
M
left to be synchronized; at this point, the failure on
y_{1}
signals that the bag still has elements to be synchronized with.

\displaystyle S\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{8}]}

\displaystyle(\nu y,y_{1},x_{1})({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset}};y_{1}.\mathtt{close}\mid y.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}};y.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{1}}

\displaystyle\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}}\mid y.\overline{\mathtt{some}};y.\overline{\mathtt{close}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{9}]}

\displaystyle(\nu y,x_{1})({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}y.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}}};y.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{1}}x\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}y.\overline{\mathtt{some}}};y.\overline{\mathtt{close}})\hskip 18.49988pt(:=T)

•

Finally, reductions
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{10}]}
and
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{11}]}
concern name
y
: the former signals that the bag has no more elements to be synchronized for substitution; the latter closes the session, as it has served its purpose of correctly synchronizing the translated term. The resulting process corresponds to the translation of
N{\langle\!|}M/x{|\!\rangle}
.

\displaystyle T\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{10}]}

\displaystyle(\nu y,x_{1})({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}y.\mathtt{close}};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{1}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}y.\overline{\mathtt{close}}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{11}]}

\displaystyle(\nu x_{1})({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{1}})={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\langle\!|}M/x{|\!\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

We now discuss the translation of a term that fails due to an excess of resources.

{exa}

[Excess of Resources]
Let us consider the well-formed
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term that does not share occurrences of
x
, i.e.,
N[\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag M\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
, where
M,N
are closed (i.e.
\mathsf{fv}(N)=\mathsf{fv}(M)=\emptyset
). This term’s translation is:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N[\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$\Lbag M\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle=(\nu x)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N[\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Lbag M\Rbag{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x})

\displaystyle=(\nu x)(x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}.\overline{x}(y_{1}).(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{u};y_{1}.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\mid

\displaystyle x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x(y_{1}).x.\mathtt{some}_{y_{1}};x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(x_{i}).(x_{i}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{i}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\mid y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}))

•

Reductions
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{1}]}
and
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{2}]}
follow as in Example
5.3.3
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N[\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$\Lbag M\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{1}]}(\nu x)(\overline{x}(y_{1}).(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{u};y_{1}.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\mid

\displaystyle x(y_{1}).x.\mathtt{some}_{y_{1}};x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(x_{i}).(x_{i}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{i}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\mid y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{2}]}(\nu x,y_{1})(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{u};y_{1}.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}}}\mid

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\displaystyle x.\mathtt{some}_{y_{1}};}}x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(x_{i}).(x_{i}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{i}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\mid y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}))\hskip 18.49988pt(:=P)

Notice how the translation of the term first triggers the failure: prefix
x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
(highlighted in
red
) signals that there are no (more) occurrences of
x
within the process; nevertheless, the translation of the bag is still trying to communicate the translation of
M
. This failure along
x
causes the chain reaction of the failure along
y_{1}
, which eventually triggers across the translation of
N
.

•

Reduction
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{3}]}
differs from
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{3}]}
in Example
5.3.3

x

y_{1}

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{3}]}(\nu y_{1})({{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{u};}}y_{1}.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid{\longrightarrow{y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{4}]}u.\overline{\mathtt{none}}={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

Reduction
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{4}]}
differs from that of
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{9}]}
and
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{10}]}
from Example

is guarded by the confirmation along

Finally, we illustrate how
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
acts on a term that fails due to lack of resources in a bag.

{exa}

[Lack of Resources]
Consider the well-formed
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term
N[x_{1}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
, where
N
is a closed term (i.e.
\mathsf{fv}(N)=\emptyset
). This term’s translation is:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N[x_{1}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=

\displaystyle(\nu x)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N[x_{1}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nu x)(x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}.\overline{x}(y_{1}).(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{1}.\mathtt{close}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x.\mathtt{some}_{u};

\displaystyle x(x_{1}).x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(y_{2}).(y_{2}.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}};y_{2}.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})~)\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}

\displaystyle x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x(y_{1}).(y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{some}};y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}))\hskip 18.49988pt(:=P)

Notice how the translation of the empty bag
\mathtt{1}
triggers the failure: prefix ‘
x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
’ signals that there are no (more) elements in the bag; however, the translated term aims to synchronize, as it (still) requires resources.

Reductions
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{1}]}
and
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{2}]}

\displaystyle P\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{1}]}

\displaystyle x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(y_{2}).(y_{2}.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}};y_{2}.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})~)\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{2}]}

\displaystyle(\nu x,y_{1})(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{1}.\mathtt{close}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x.\mathtt{some}_{u};x(x_{1}).

\displaystyle x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(y_{2}).(y_{2}.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}};y_{2}.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}

Reductions
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{3}]}
and
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{4}]}
follow from that of
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{9}]}
and
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{10}]}
in Example
5.3.3
; as the term contains the element
x_{1}
for synchronization, the encoding of
N
is not guarded by
y_{1}
.

\displaystyle Q\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{3}]}

\displaystyle(\nu x,y_{1})(y_{1}.\mathtt{close}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x.\mathtt{some}_{u};x(x_{1}).

\displaystyle x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(y_{2}).(y_{2}.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}};y_{2}.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}

\displaystyle y_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{4}]}

\displaystyle(\nu x)(x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x.\mathtt{some}_{u};x(x_{1}).x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(y_{2}).(y_{2}.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}};y_{2}.\mathtt{close};

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\displaystyle\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{5}]}

\displaystyle(\nu x)({{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}x.\mathtt{some}_{u}}};x(x_{1}).x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(y_{2}).(y_{2}.\mathtt{some}_{u,x_{1}};y_{2}.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{6}]}

\displaystyle u.\overline{\mathtt{none}}=~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

•

Reduction
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{5}]}
follows from reduction
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{3}]}
in Example
5.3.3
.

•

Reduction
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{6}]}
differs from that of
\longrightarrow_{[\mathsf{4}]}
from Example
5.3.3
: the bag contains no elements, and signals this by aborting along the name
x
; still, the term expects to receive an element of the bag, and prematurely aborts.

Translating Types

In describing our translation
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{-}
we have informally referred to (non-deterministic) session protocols in
\mathsf{s}\pi
that implement (non-deterministic) expressions in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
. We are actually able to make these intuitions precise and give a translation of intersection types (for
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, cf. Def.
2.3.1
) into session types (for
\mathsf{s}\pi
, cf. Def.
4.3
). This provides the protocol-oriented interpretation of intersections mentioned earlier. Intuitively speaking, given an intersection type
\pi
, we will have a corresponding session type
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\pi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}
that determines a protocol tied to the evaluation of a (fail-prone, non-deterministic) expression with type
\pi
.

{defi}

[From
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
: Types]

The translation
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}
on types is defined
in Fig.
17
.
Let

\Gamma=x_{1}:\sigma_{1},\cdots,x_{m}:\sigma_{k},v_{1}:\pi_{1},\cdots,v_{n}:\pi_{n}

be as in Def.
3.3
.

For some strict types
\tau_{1},\cdots,\tau_{n}
and
i_{1},\cdots,i_{n}\geq 0
we define:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}=x_{1}:{\binampersand}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}},\cdots,x_{k}:{\binampersand}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma_{k}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}},v_{1}:{\binampersand}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\pi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\tau_{1},i_{1})}},\cdots,v_{n}:{\binampersand}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\pi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\tau_{n},i_{n})}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbf{unit}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}

\displaystyle={\binampersand}\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\pi\rightarrow\tau{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}

\displaystyle={\binampersand}((\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\pi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\sigma,i)}})\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\tau{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}})\quad\text{(for some strict type $\sigma$, with $i\geq 0$)}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma\wedge\pi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\tau,i)}

\displaystyle=\overline{{\binampersand}((\oplus\bot)\otimes({\binampersand}\oplus(({\binampersand}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}})\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\pi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\tau,i)}}))))}

\displaystyle=\oplus(({\binampersand}\mathbf{1})\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(\oplus{\binampersand}((\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}})\otimes({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\pi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\tau,i)}))))

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\omega{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\sigma,i)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\overline{{\binampersand}((\oplus\bot)\otimes({\binampersand}\oplus\bot)))}&\text{if $i=0$}\\
\overline{{\binampersand}((\oplus\bot)\otimes({\binampersand}\oplus(({\binampersand}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}})\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\omega{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\sigma,i-1)}}))))}&\text{if $i>0$}\end{cases}

Figure 17.
Translating intersection types as session types.

As we will see, given a well-formedness judgement
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\tau
, with the translations on types and assignments defined above, we will have
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}},u:{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\tau{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}
; this is the content of the
type preservation
property (Theorem
31
).

The translation of types in Fig.
17
leverages non-deterministic session protocols (typed with ‘
{\binampersand}
’) to represent non-deterministic fetching and fail-prone evaluation in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.
Notice that the translation of the multiset type
\pi
depends on two arguments (a strict type
\tau
and a number
i\geq 0
) which are left unspecified above, but are appropriately specified in Proposition
30
.
This is crucial to represent mismatches in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
(i.e., sources of failures) as typable processes in
\mathsf{s}\pi
.
For instance, in Fig.
7
, Rule
\mathtt{[FS{:}app]}
admits a mismatch between

\sigma^{j}\rightarrow\tau
and
\sigma^{k}
, for it allows
j\neq k
.
In our proof of type preservation, these two arguments are instantiated appropriately, enabling typability as session-typed processes.

We are now ready to consider correctness for
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
, in the sense of Def.
5.1
.
First, the compositionality property follows directly from Fig.
16
.
In the following sections, we state the remaining properties in Def.
5.1
: type preservation, operational correspondence, and success sensitiveness.

5.3.4.
Type Preservation

We prove that our translation from
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to
\mathsf{s}\pi
maps well-formed
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expressions to session-typed processes in
\mathsf{s}\pi
. First, we show that translated multiset types can be “lengthened” by setting appropriate parameters to the encoding.

Proposition 30
.

Suppose
\sigma^{j}
and
\sigma^{k}
are arbitrary strict types (Def.
2.3.1
), for some
j,k\geq 0
.
Following Fig.
17
, consider their encoding into session types
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{j}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\tau_{1},m)}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{k}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\tau_{2},n)}
, respectively, where
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
are strict types and
n,m\geq 0
.

We have

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{j}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\tau_{1},m)}={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{k}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\tau_{2},n)}

under the following conditions:

(1)

If
j>k
then we take
\tau_{1}
to be an arbitrary strict type and
m=0
; also, we take
\tau_{2}
to be
\sigma
and
n=j-k
.

(2)

If
j<k
then we take
\tau_{1}
to be
\sigma
and
m=k-j
; also, we take
\tau_{2}
to be an arbitrary strict type and
n=0
.

(3)

Otherwise, if
j=k
then we take
m=n=0
. Also,
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
are arbitrary strict types.

Proof 5.9
.

Immediate by unfolding the translation. The full analysis can be found in App.
D.1
.

Given Proposition
30
we now show that the translation preserves types:

Theorem 31

(Type Preservation for
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
)
.

Let
B
and
\mathbb{M}
be a bag and an expression in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, respectively.

(1)

If
\Gamma^{\dagger}\models B:\pi

then

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Gamma^{\dagger}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}},u:{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\pi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\sigma,i)}
, for some strict type
\sigma
and index
i\geq 0
.

(2)

If
\Gamma^{\dagger}\models\mathbb{M}:\tau

then

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Gamma^{\dagger}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}},u:{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\tau{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}
.

Proof 5.10
.

By mutual induction on the typing derivation of
B
and
\mathbb{M}
, with an analysis of the last rule applied in
\Gamma\models B:\pi
and in
\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\tau
. One key aspect of this proof is the application of Proposition
30
to ensure duality of types. Intuitively, the conditions given by Proposition
30
are used to instantiate the parameters in the encoding of intersection types, so as to ensure that when intersection types have different types the smaller type can be correctly “padded” to match the size of the larger type—Example
5.3.4
, given below, illustrates this padding. The full proof can be found in App.
D.1
.

{exa}

[Parameters in the encoding of types]

We give the dual types when encoding intersection types, namely the case of
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma\wedge\pi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\sigma,i)}
, to express the encoding of intersection typed behavior into session typed behavior.
The application of dual types is most evident in the application of a bag into an abstraction: the bag providing the intersection type and the abstraction consuming it. In session types the interaction between these is expressed by dual session types where one channel provides a behavior and and the dual channel provides the dual session type behavior via the cut rule.
Let us consider the term
(\lambda x.M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x])B
typed with the well-formedness rules by:

\Gamma\models\lambda x.M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]:(\sigma\wedge\sigma)\rightarrow\tau\quad\Delta\models B:\sigma^{k}

\mathtt{[FS{:}app]}

\Gamma,\Delta\models(\lambda x.M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x])B:\tau

When applying the translation of Fig.
16
to the term we obtain:

\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}(\nu v)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\lambda x.M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}))

By appealing to Type Preservation (Theorem
31
) we obtain both
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\lambda x.M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}},v:{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}(\sigma\wedge\sigma)\rightarrow\tau{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Delta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}},x:{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{k}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{2},i_{2})}
.
We give the typing for one non-deterministic branch where we take an arbitrary permutation of
B
is as follows by applying the rules of Fig.

and that
\Pi_{1}

\mathtt{[Tid]}

[v\leftrightarrow u]\vdash v:\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\tau{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}},u:{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\tau{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Delta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}},x:{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{k}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{2},i_{2})}

\mathtt{[T\otimes]}

\mathtt{[T\oplus^{x}_{\widetilde{w}}]}

Hence we obtain the derivation:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\lambda x.M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}},v:{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}(\sigma\wedge\sigma)\rightarrow\tau{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}

\Pi_{1}

\mathtt{[Tcut]}

(\nu v)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\lambda x.M[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}\!\mid\!v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\!\mid\!{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x})\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Delta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}},u:{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\tau{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}

Now we shall focus on the typing of the channel
v
and
x
in this process as these channel describes the behavior of the encoded intersection type which we are trying to match via duality. By the translation on types from Fig.
