we have that

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}(\sigma\wedge\sigma)\rightarrow\tau{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}={\binampersand}((\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}(\sigma\wedge\sigma){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{1},i_{1})}})\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\tau{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}})

•

When
B=\mathtt{1}
we have derivation:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\models{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Delta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}},x:{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\omega{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{2},i_{2})}

To obtain duality from Rule
\mathtt{[Tcut]}
we must have that
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{1},i_{1})}={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\omega{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{2},i_{2})}
. By Proposition
30
we can take
\delta_{1}
to be an arbitrary strict type,
i_{1}=0
,
i_{2}=2
,
\delta_{2}=\sigma
. We have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\omega{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\sigma,2)}

\displaystyle=\overline{{\binampersand}((\oplus\bot)\otimes({\binampersand}\oplus(({\binampersand}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}})\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\omega{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\sigma,1)}}))))}

\displaystyle=\overline{{\binampersand}((\oplus\bot)\otimes({\binampersand}\oplus(({\binampersand}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}})\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}({\binampersand}((\oplus\bot)\otimes({\binampersand}\oplus(({\binampersand}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}})\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\omega{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\sigma,0)}}))))))))}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{1},i_{1})}

•

When
B=\Lbag N_{1},N_{2}\Rbag
we have derivation:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Lbag N_{1},N_{2}\Rbag{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\models{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Delta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}},x:{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{2},i_{2})}

To obtain duality from Rule
\mathtt{[Tcut]}
we must have that
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{1},i_{1})}={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{2},i_{2})}
. By Proposition
30
we can take
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
to be an arbitrary strict type and
i_{1}=i_{2}=0
. We then obtain
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{1},0)}={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{2},0)}
, as
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\omega{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{1},0)}={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\omega{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{2},0)}
for any two strict types
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
.

•

When
B=\Lbag N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}\Rbag
we have derivation:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Lbag N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}\Rbag{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\models{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Delta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}},x:{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{3}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{2},i_{2})}

To obtain duality from Rule
\mathtt{[Tcut]}
we must have that
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{1},i_{1})}={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sigma^{3}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{(\delta_{2},i_{2})}
. By Proposition
30
we can take
\delta_{2}
to be an arbitrary strict type,
i_{2}=0
,
i_{1}=2
,
\delta_{1}=\sigma
. Then the case proceeds similarly to when
B=\mathtt{1}
.

5.3.5.
Operational Correspondence: Completeness and Soundness

We now state our operational correspondence results (completeness and soundness, cf. Fig.
19
).

A Congruence

We will identify some
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-terms such as
M[\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
and
M
. The identification is natural, as the former is a term
M
with no occurrences of
x
in which
x
is going to be replaced with
\mathtt{1}
, which clearly describes a substitution that “does nothing”, and would result in
M
itself. With this intuition, other terms are identified via a
congruence
(denoted
\equiv_{\lambda}
) on terms and expressions that is formally defined in Fig.
18
.

\begin{array}[]{rll}M[\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle&\equiv_{\lambda}M&\\
MB{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}&\equiv_{\lambda}(M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle})B&\hskip-30.00005pt\text{with }x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(B)\\
M{\langle\!|}N_{2}/y{|\!\rangle}{\langle\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\rangle}&\equiv_{\lambda}M{\langle\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\rangle}{\langle\!|}N_{2}/y{|\!\rangle}&\hskip-30.00005pt\text{with }x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(N_{2}),y\notin\mathsf{fv}(N_{1})\\
MA[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle&\equiv_{\lambda}(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)A&\hskip-30.00005pt\text{with }x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}\Rightarrow x_{i}\not\in\mathsf{fv}(A)\\
M[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$A$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle&\equiv_{\lambda}(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$A$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle&\text{with }x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}\Rightarrow x_{i}\not\in\mathsf{fv}(A)\\
C[M]&\equiv_{\lambda}C[M^{\prime}]&\hskip-30.00005pt\text{with }M\equiv_{\lambda}M^{\prime}\\
D[\mathbb{M}]&\equiv_{\lambda}D[\mathbb{M}^{\prime}]&\hskip-30.00005pt\text{with }\mathbb{M}\equiv_{\lambda}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}\end{array}

Figure 18.
Congruence in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

{exa}

[Cont. Example
3.3.2
]
We illustrate the congruence in case of failure:

\displaystyle(\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])\Lbag\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\Rbag

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[RS{:}Beta]}}x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[RS{:}Ex\text{-}Sub]}}x_{1}{\langle\!|}\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle/x_{1}{|\!\rangle}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[RS{:}Lin\text{-}Fetch]}}\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}[\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv_{\lambda}\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}

In the last step, Rule
\mathtt{[RS{:}Cons_{2}]}
cannot be applied:

y
is sharing with no shared variables and the explicit substitution involves the bag
\mathtt{1}
.

Theorem 32

(Consistency Stability Under
\equiv
)
.

Let
{\mathbb{M}}
be a consistent
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-expression. If
\mathbb{M}\equiv\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
then
{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}
is consistent.

Proof 5.11
.

By induction on the structure of
\mathbb{M}
; see
Section

D.2
for details.

Operational Completeness
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
:

\mathsf{s}\pi
:

Figure 19.
Operational Correspondence for
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}

{defi}

[Partially Open Terms]
We say that a
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term
M
is
partially open
if
\forall x\in\mathsf{fv}(M)
(cf. Def.
3.2
) implies that
x
is not a sharing variable.

Notice that the class of open terms (no conditions on free variables) subsumes the class of partially open terms, which in turn subsumes the class of closed terms. Consider the following example.

{exa}

[Partially Open Terms]
We give three examples of well-formed
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-terms:

\displaystyle M_{1}

\displaystyle=\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x]

\displaystyle\quad M_{2}

\displaystyle=\lambda x.(x_{1}\Lbag y\Rbag)[x_{1}\leftarrow x]

\displaystyle\quad M_{3}

\displaystyle=(x_{1}\Lbag y\Rbag)[x_{1}\leftarrow x]

Here the only closed term is
M_{1}
as
M_{2}
has one free variable (i.e.,
y
) and
M_{3}
has two free variables (
y
and
x
).
While
M_{2}
is partially open,
M_{3}
is not because
x
is a sharing variable.

The following proposition will be used in the proof of operational completeness (Theorem
34
) and operational soundness (Theorem
35
). The proposition relies on well-formed partially open terms; however, in the proof of operational correspondence we only consider closed terms rather then partially open terms.

Proposition 33
.

Suppose
N
is a well-formed, partially open
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term with
\mathsf{head}(N)=x
.
Then, there exist an index set
I
, names
\widetilde{y}
and
n
, and processes
P_{i}
such that the following four conditions hold:

(1)

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\longrightarrow^{*}\bigoplus_{i\in I}(\nu\widetilde{y})({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}x{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{n}\mid P_{i})

(2)

There exists a
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term
N^{\prime}
such that
N\equiv_{\lambda}N^{\prime}
and:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=\bigoplus_{i\in I}(\nu\widetilde{y})({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}x{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{n}\mid P_{i})

(3)

For any well-formed and partially open
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term
M
:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\{\!|}M/x{|\!\}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\longrightarrow^{*}\bigoplus_{i\in I}(\nu\widetilde{y})({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{n}\mid P_{i})

(4)

There exists a
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term
M^{\prime}
such that
M^{\prime}\equiv_{\lambda}N{\{\!|}M/x{|\!\}}
and:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=\bigoplus_{i\in I}(\nu\widetilde{y})({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{n}\mid P_{i})

Proof 5.12
.

By induction on the structure of
N
. We briefly sketch the strategy for proving it case below, but
the complete proof can be found in App.
D.2
.

(1)

The interesting cases are for
N=M{\langle\!|}N^{\prime}/x{|\!\rangle}
and
N=M[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle
, when
\mathsf{size}(B)=\mathsf{size}(\widetilde{y})=0
and
\mathsf{head}(M)=x
. Notice that
N=M[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y]
is not a case, because of the definition of partially open term:
y
is a sharing variable in
N
and
y\in\mathsf{fv}(N)
.
The other cases follow easily by the induction hypothesis.

(2)

Reductions are only introduced by explicit weakening, which can be eliminated via the precongruence.

(3)

Follows from (1) and the fact that linear head substitution can be placed deeper within the term until it reaches the head variable.

(4)

Follows from (2) and (3).

Because of the diamond property (Proposition
9
), it suffices to consider a completeness result based on a single reduction step in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
:

Theorem 34

(Operational Completeness)
.

Let
\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbb{M}
be well-formed, partially open
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expressions. If
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}
then there exist
Q
and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}

such that
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}\equiv_{\lambda}\mathbb{M}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\longrightarrow^{*}Q={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
.

Proof 5.13
.

By induction on the reduction rule applied to infer
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}
. The case in which

\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[RS:Lin\text{-}~Fetch]}}\mathbb{M}
happens for
\mathbb{N}=M{\langle\!|}N^{\prime}/x{|\!\rangle}
with
\mathsf{head}(M)=x
, and
\mathbb{M}=M{\{\!|}N^{\prime}/x{|\!\}}
. The translation of
\mathbb{N}
is of the form (omitting details):

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle=(\nu x)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(N^{\prime})};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{*}(\nu x)(\bigoplus_{i\in I}(\nu\widetilde{y})({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}x{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{n}\mid P_{i})\mid x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{fv}(N^{\prime})};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}),~\text{by Proposition~\ref{prop:NEEDTONAME}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{*}\bigoplus_{i\in I}(\nu\widetilde{y})(P_{i}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{n})={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

The other cases follow by analyzing reductions from the translation of
\mathbb{N}
. The full proof can be found in App.
D.2
.

Notice how Proposition
33
requires a term to be partially open; however, we prove operational correspondence for closed terms. The reason for this is that we start from a source closed term in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, which is translated by
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
into a closed
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term.

{exa}

[Cont. Example
5.3.3
]

Recall that
M
and
N
are well-formed with
\mathsf{fv}(N)=\mathsf{fv}(M)=\emptyset
, we can verify that
N[\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag M\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
and
\mathtt{fail}^{\mathsf{fv}(N)\cup\mathsf{fv}(M)}
are also well-formed. We have

N[\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag M\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[RS{:}Fail]}}\mathtt{fail}^{\mathsf{fv}(N)\cup\mathsf{fv}(M)}

In
\mathsf{s}\pi
, this reduction is mimicked as

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N[\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\Lbag M\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\longrightarrow^{*}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{fail}^{\mathsf{fv}(N)\cup\mathsf{fv}(M)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}.

In fact,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N[\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$\Lbag M\Rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=

\displaystyle(\nu x)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N[\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\Lbag M\Rbag{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nu x)(x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}.\overline{x}(y_{i}).(y_{i}.\mathtt{some}_{u};y_{i}.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\mid

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x(y_{i}).x.\mathtt{some}_{y_{i}};x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(x_{i}).(x_{i}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{i}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\mid y_{i}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle(\nu x)(\overline{x}(y_{i}).(y_{i}.\mathtt{some}_{u};y_{i}.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\mid

\displaystyle x(y_{i}).x.\mathtt{some}_{y_{i}};x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(x_{i}).(x_{i}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{i}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\mid y_{i}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle(\nu x)(y_{i}.\mathtt{some}_{u};y_{i}.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid

\displaystyle x.\mathtt{some}_{y_{i}};x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x}(x_{i}).(x_{i}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x_{i}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\mid y_{i}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle(\nu x)(y_{i}.\mathtt{some}_{u};y_{i}.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\mid y_{i}.\overline{\mathtt{none}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle u.\overline{\mathtt{none}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\displaystyle\llbracket}\mathtt{fail}^{\mathsf{fv}(N)\cup\mathsf{fv}(M)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

To state soundness we rely on the congruence relation
\equiv_{\lambda}
, given in Fig.
18
.

{nota}

Recall the congruence
\equiv_{\lambda}
for
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, given in Figure
18
.
We write
N\longrightarrow_{\equiv_{\lambda}}N^{\prime}
iff
N\equiv_{\lambda}N_{1}\longrightarrow N_{2}\equiv_{\lambda}N^{\prime}
, for some
N_{1},N_{2}
. Then,
\longrightarrow_{\equiv_{\lambda}}^{*}
is the reflexive, transitive closure of
\longrightarrow_{\equiv_{\lambda}}
.
We use the notation
M\longrightarrow_{\equiv_{\lambda}}^{i}N
to state that
M
performs
i
steps of
\longrightarrow_{\equiv_{\lambda}}
to
N
in
i\geq 0
steps. When
i=0
it refers to no reduction taking place.

Theorem 35

(Operational Soundness)
.

Let
\mathbb{N}
be a
well-formed, partially open
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expression.
If
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\longrightarrow^{*}Q

then there exist
Q^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{N}^{\prime}
such that

Q\longrightarrow^{*}Q^{\prime}
,
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow^{*}_{\equiv_{\lambda}}\mathbb{N}^{\prime}

and

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=Q^{\prime}
.

Proof 5.14

(Proof (Sketch))
.

By induction on the structure of
\mathbb{N}
with sub-induction on the number of reduction steps in
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\longrightarrow^{*}Q
. The cases in which
\mathbb{N}=x
, or
\mathbb{N}=\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
, or
\mathbb{N}=\lambda x.M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
, are easy since there are no reductions starting from
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\longrightarrow^{0}Q
which implies
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{N^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=Q=Q^{\prime}
and the result follows trivially. The analysis for some cases are exhaustive, for instance, when
\mathbb{N}=(M\ B)
or
\mathbb{N}=M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
, there are several sub-cases to be considered: (i)
B
being equal to
\mathtt{1}
or not; (ii)
\mathsf{size}(B)
matching the number of occurrences of the variable in
M
or not; (iii)
M
being a failure term or not.

We now discuss one of these cases to illustrate the recurring idea used in the proof: let
\mathbb{N}=(M\ B)
and suppose that we are able to perform
k>1
steps to a process
Q
, i.e.,

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}(M\ B){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}(\nu v)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}))\longrightarrow^{k}Q

(2)

Then there exist an
\mathsf{s}\pi
process
R
and integers
n,m
such that
k=m+n
and

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{m}\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}(\nu v)(R\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\mid[v\leftrightarrow u]))\longrightarrow^{n}Q

where the first
m\geq 0
reduction steps are internal to
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}
; type preservation in
\mathsf{s}\pi
ensures that, if they occur, these reductions do not discard the possibility of synchronizing with
v.\mathtt{some}
. Then, the first of the
n\geq 0
reduction steps towards
Q
is a synchronization between
R
and
v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)}
.

We will consider the case when
m=0
and
n\geq 1
. Then
R={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\longrightarrow^{0}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
and there are two possibilities of having an unguarded
v.\overline{\mathtt{some}}
or
v.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
without internal reductions:

(1)

M=(\lambda x.M^{\prime}[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]){\langle\!|}N_{1}/y_{1}{|\!\rangle}\cdots{\langle\!|}N_{p}/y_{p}{|\!\rangle}\qquad(p\geq 0)

(2)

M=\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{z}}

Firstly we use case (i) to express the need for the reduction
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow^{*}_{\equiv_{\lambda}}\mathbb{N}^{\prime}
. In this case
\mathbb{N}=((\lambda x.M^{\prime}[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]){\langle\!|}N_{1}/y_{1}{|\!\rangle}\cdots{\langle\!|}N_{p}/y_{p}{|\!\rangle}\ B)
and
\llbracket\mathbb{N}\rrbracket_{u}
may perform synchronizations where both
\llbracket\lambda x.M^{\prime}\rrbracket_{v}
and
\llbracket B\rrbracket_{x}
synchronize across their shared channel. Here we use the congruence relation as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle=((\lambda x.M^{\prime}[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]){\langle\!|}N_{1}/y_{1}{|\!\rangle}\cdots{\langle\!|}N_{p}/y_{p}{|\!\rangle}\ B)

\displaystyle\equiv_{\lambda}((\lambda x.M^{\prime}[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\ B){\langle\!|}N_{1}/y_{1}{|\!\rangle}\cdots{\langle\!|}N_{p}/y_{p}{|\!\rangle}

This enables the abstraction
\lambda x.M^{\prime}
to synchronize with the bag
B
.

Now we will develop case (ii):

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{z}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{z}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}=v.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid\widetilde{z}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}

With this shape for
M
, the translation and reductions from (
2
) become

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}(\nu v)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\mid[v\leftrightarrow u]))

(3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}(\nu v)(v.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid\widetilde{z}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}\mid[v\leftrightarrow u]))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}u.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid\widetilde{z}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid\mathsf{fv}(B).\overline{\mathtt{none}}

We also have that

\mathbb{N}=\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{z}}\ B\longrightarrow\sum_{\mathsf{PER}(B)}\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{z}\cup\mathsf{fv}(B)}=\mathbb{M}
.
Furthermore, we have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\sum_{\mathsf{PER}(B)}\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{z}\cup\mathsf{fv}(B)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

(4)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\mathsf{PER}(B)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{z}\cup\mathsf{fv}(B)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\mathsf{PER}(B)}u.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid\widetilde{z}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mid\mathsf{fv}(B).\overline{\mathtt{none}}

From reductions in (
3
) and (
4
) one has
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
, and the result follows with
n=1
and
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=Q=Q^{\prime}
. The full proof can be found in App.
D.2
.

5.3.6.
Success Sensitiveness

Finally, we consider success sensitiveness. This requires extending
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and
\mathsf{s}\pi
with success predicates.

{defi}

We extend the syntax of
\mathsf{s}\pi
processes (Definition
4.1
)
with the
\checkmark
construct, which we assume well typed.
Also, we extend Definition
5.3.2
by
defining
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\checkmark{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=\checkmark

{defi}

We say that a process occurs
guarded
when it occurs behind a prefix (input, output, closing of channels and non-deterministic session behavior). That is,
P
is guarded if
\alpha.P
or
\alpha;P
, where
\alpha=\overline{x}(y),x(y),x.\overline{\mathtt{close}},x.\mathtt{close},

x.\overline{\mathtt{some}},x.\mathtt{some}_{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})}
.
We say it occurs
unguarded
if it is not guarded for any prefix.

{defi}

[Success in
\mathsf{s}\pi
]

We extend the syntax of
\mathsf{s}\pi
processes
with the
\checkmark
construct, which we assume well-typed.
We define

P\Downarrow_{\checkmark}
to hold whenever there exists a
P^{\prime}

such that

P\longrightarrow^{*}P^{\prime}

and
P^{\prime}
contains an unguarded occurrence of
\checkmark
.

Proposition 36

(Preservation of Success)
.

The
\checkmark
at the head of a partially open term is preserved to an unguarded occurrence of
\checkmark
when applying the translation
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
up to reductions and vice-versa. That is to say:

(1)

\forall M\in\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}:\quad\mathsf{head}(M)=\checkmark\implies{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\longrightarrow^{*}(P\mid\checkmark)\oplus Q

(2)

\forall M\in\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}:\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=(P\mid\checkmark)\oplus Q\implies\mathsf{head}(M)=\checkmark

Proof 5.15

(Proof (Sketch))
.

By induction on the structure of
M
.
For item (1), consider the case
M=(N\ B)
and
\mathsf{head}(N\ B)=\mathsf{head}(N)=\checkmark
. This term’s translation is

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N\ B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}(\nu v)({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x})).

By the induction hypothesis,
\checkmark
is unguarded in
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
after a sequence of reductions, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\longrightarrow^{*}(\checkmark\mid P^{\prime})\oplus Q^{\prime}
, for some
\mathsf{s}\pi
processes
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
. Thus,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}N\ B{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{*}\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(B)}(\nu v)((\checkmark\mid P^{\prime})\oplus Q^{\prime}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}))

\displaystyle\equiv\checkmark\mid(\nu v)(P^{\prime}\oplus Q^{\prime}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{j}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}))

\displaystyle\oplus\Big(\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in(\mathsf{PER}(B)\setminus\!\!\setminus B_{j})}\checkmark\mid(\nu v)(P^{\prime}\oplus Q^{\prime}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}))\Big)

\displaystyle\equiv(\checkmark\mid P)\oplus Q

and the result follows by taking
P=(\nu v)(P^{\prime}\oplus Q^{\prime}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{j}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}))
and

Q=\bigoplus_{B_{i}\in({\mathsf{PER}(B)}\setminus\!\!\setminus B_{j})}\checkmark\mid(\nu v)(P^{\prime}\oplus Q^{\prime}\mid v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(B)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{x}))
.
The analysis for the other cases are similar; see App.
D.3
for details.

The translation
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}:\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}\rightarrow\mathsf{s}\pi
is success sensitive on well-formed closed expressions.

Theorem 37

(Success Sensitivity)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a closed well-formed
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-expression.
Then,

\mathbb{M}\Downarrow_{\checkmark}\iff{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\Downarrow_{\checkmark}.

Proof 5.16

(Proof (Sketch))
.

Suppose
\mathbb{M}\Downarrow_{\checkmark}
.
By Definition
5.2.3
there exists
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=M_{1}+\cdots+M_{k}
such that
\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow^{*}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
and

\mathsf{head}(M_{j})=\checkmark
, for some
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
and
M_{j}
. By operational completeness (Theorem
34
), there exists
Q
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\longrightarrow^{*}Q={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}
.
Due to compositionality of
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}
and the homomorphic preservation of non-determinism, we have:

•

Q={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M_{k}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}

•

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M_{j}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}=C[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\checkmark{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{v}]=C[\checkmark]

By Proposition
36
, item (1), since
\mathsf{head}(M_{j})=\checkmark
it follows that
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}M_{j}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}_{u}\longrightarrow^{*}P\mid\checkmark\oplus Q^{\prime}
. Hence
Q
reduces to a process that has an unguarded occurrence of
\checkmark
. The proof of the converse is similar and can be found in App.
D.3
.

As main result of this sub-section, we have the corollary below, which follows from the previously stated Theorems
31
,

34
,

35
, and

37
:

Corollary 38
.

Our translation
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rrbracket}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\lightning}}
is a correct encoding, in the sense of Def.
5.1
.

Together, Corollary
29
and Corollary
38
ensure that
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
can be correctly translated into
\mathsf{s}\pi
, using
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
as a stepping stone.

6.
Related Work

Closely related works have been already discussed in the introduction and throughout the paper; here we mention other related literature.

Intersection Types

The first works on intersection types date back to the late 70s (see, e.g.,
[
CD78
,
Pot80
]
) and consider intersections with the
idempotence
property (i.e.,
\sigma\land\sigma=\sigma
).
This formulation enables the analysis of
qualitative
properties of
\lambda
-calculi, such as (strong) normalization and solvability.
By dropping idempotence, intersection types can characterize
quantitative
properties, such as, e.g., bounds on the number of steps needed to reach a normal form.
Early works on non-idempotent intersection types include
[
Gar94
,
Kfo00
,
KW04
]
.
The paper
[
BD20
]
overviews the origins, development, and applications of intersection types.

Our work formally connects non-idempotent intersection types and classical linear logic extended with the modalities
{\binampersand}
and
\oplus
, interpreted in
[
CP17
]
as session types for non-deterministically available protocols.
To the best of our knowledge, this is an unexplored angle. Prior connections between (non-idempotent) intersection types and linear logic arise in very different settings (see
[
MPV18
]
and references therein). They include
[
NM04
]
, which presents a connection based on a correspondence between normalization and type inference;
the work
[
dC09
,
dC18
]
, which shows a correspondence between the
relational model
of linear logic and an non-idempotent intersection type system;
and
[
Ehr20
]
, which concerns
indexed
linear logic (cf.
[
BE00
,
BE01
]
).

The work
[
LdVMY19
]
develops a type system for the
\pi
-calculus based on non-idempotent intersections. The type system ensures that processes are “well-behaved”—they never produce run-time errors, and can always reduce to an idle process. Remarkably, they show that their type system is
complete
: every well-behaved process is typable. Although their type system does not consider session types, it is related to our work for it builds upon Mazza et al.’s correspondence between linear logic and intersection types, given in terms of
polyadic approximations

[
MPV18
]
.

Other Resource
\lambda
-calculi

A fine-grained treatment of duplication and erasing—similar to our design for
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
—is present in Kesner and Lengrand’s
\lambda
lxr
-calculus
[
KL07
]
, a
simply-typed, deterministic
\lambda
-calculus that is in correspondence with proof nets.
The
\lambda
lxr
-calculus includes operators called weakening
\mathcal{W}_{\_}(\_)
and contraction
\mathcal{C}_{\_}^{\_|\_}(\_)
to deal with empty and non-empty sharing, respectively.
In this approach, our terms

\lambda x.x\Lbag x\Rbag
and
\lambda x.y\Lbag z\Rbag

would be expressed as
\mathcal{C}_{x}^{x_{1}|x_{2}}(\lambda x.x_{1}\Lbag x_{2}\Rbag)
and
\mathcal{W}_{x}(\lambda x.y\Lbag z\Rbag)
, respectively.

Our approach is convenient when expressing the sharing of more than two occurrences of a variable in a term; as in, e.g., the
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-term
\lambda x.(x\Lbag x,x\Rbag)
which would correspond to
\lambda x.(x_{1}\Lbag x_{2},x_{3}\Rbag)[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\leftarrow x]
in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.
In the
\lambda
lxr
-calculus, contractions are binary, and so representing
\lambda x.(x\Lbag x,x\Rbag)
requires the composition of two binary contractions.

More substantial differences appear at the level of types.
As we have seen, in
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
we use intersection types to define well-typed and well-formed expressions (see Fig.
6
and Fig.
7
, respectively). In particular, recall the well-formedness rule for the sharing construct:

\Gamma,x_{1}:\sigma,\ldots,x_{k}:\sigma\models M:\tau\quad x\notin\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)\quad k\neq 0

\mathtt{[FS:share]}

\Gamma,x:\sigma^{k}\models M[x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\leftarrow x]:\tau

where, as mentioned above,
\sigma^{k}
denotes the intersection type
\sigma\wedge\ldots\wedge\sigma
.
Differently, the typing rule for contraction in the
\lambda
lxr
-calculus involves an arbitrary (simple) type
A
:

\Gamma,y:A,z:A\vdash M:B

(Cont)

\Gamma,x:A\vdash\mathcal{C}_{x}^{y|z}(M):B

Our weakening rule
\mathtt{[FS:weak]}
types the empty sharing term
M[\leftarrow x]
as follows:

\Gamma\models M:\tau

\mathtt{[FS:weak]}

\Gamma,x:\omega\models M[\leftarrow x]:\tau

Hence, the context
\Gamma
is weakened with a variable assignment
x:\omega
, where
\omega
denotes the empty type. In contrast, weakening in the
\lambda
lxr
-calculus involves a (simple) type
A
:

\Gamma\vdash M:A

(Weak)

\Gamma,x:B\vdash\mathcal{W}_{x}(M):A

Hence, the context can be weakened with an assignment
x:B
, where
B
is a simple type.

Inspired by the multiplicative exponential fragment of linear logic, Kesner and Renaud
[
KR11
]
define the so-called
prismoid of resources
, a parametric framework of simply-typed
\lambda
-calculi in which each language incorporates different choices for contraction, weakening, and substitution operations.
The prismoid defines a uniform and general setting for establishing key properties of typed terms,
including simulation of
\beta
-reduction, confluence, and strong normalization.
One of the languages included in the prismoid is a minor variant of the
\lambda
lxr
-calculus, which we have just mentioned.

There are some similarities between
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and the differential
\lambda
-calculus, introduced in
[
ER03
]
. Both express non-deterministic choice via sums and use linear head reduction for evaluation. In particular, our fetch rule, which consumes non-deterministically elements from a bag, is related to the derivation (which has similarities with substitution) of a differential term. However, the focus of
[
ER03
]
is not on typability nor encodings to process calculi; instead they relate the Taylor series of analysis to the linear head reduction of
\lambda
-calculus.

Functions as Processes

A source of inspiration for our developments is the work by Boudol and Laneve
[
BL00
]
. As far as we know, this
is the only prior study that connects
\lambda
and
\pi
from a resource-oriented perspective, via an encoding of a
\lambda
-calculus with multiplicities into a
\pi
-calculus without sums.
The goal of
[
BL00
]
is different from ours, as they study the discriminating power of semantics for
\lambda
as induced by encodings into
\pi
. In contrast, we study how typability delineates the encodability of resource-awareness across sequential and concurrent realms.
The source and target calculi in
[
BL00
]
are untyped, whereas we consider typed calculi and our encodings preserve typability.
As a result, the encoding in
[
BL00
]
is conceptually different from ours; remarkably, our encoding respects linearity and homomorphically translates sums.

Prior works have studied encodings of typed
\lambda
-calculi into typed
\pi
-calculi; see, e.g.,
[
San99
,
BL00
,
SW01
,
BHY03
,
TCP12
,
HYB14
,
TY18
]
. None of these works consider non-determinism and failures; the one exception is the encoding in
[
CP17
]
, which involves a
\lambda
-calculus with exceptions and failures (but without non-determinism due to bags, as in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
) for which no reduction semantics is given. As a result, the encoding in
[
CP17
]
is different from ours, and is only shown to preserve typability: properties such as operational completeness, operational soundness, and success sensitivity—important in our developments—are not considered.

7.
Concluding Remarks

Summary

We developed a correct encoding of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, a new resource
\lambda
-calculus in which expressions feature non-determinism and explicit failure, into
\mathsf{s}\pi
, a session-typed
\pi
-calculus in which behavior is non-deterministically available: session protocols may perform as stipulated but also fail.
Our encodability result is obtained by appealing to
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, an intermediate language with a
sharing construct
that simplifies the treatment of variables in expressions.
To our knowledge, we are the first to relate typed
\lambda
-calculi and typed
\pi
-calculi encompassing non-determinism and failures, while connecting intersection types and session types, two different mechanisms for resource-awareness in sequential and concurrent settings, respectively.

Design of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
(and
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)

The design of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
has been influenced by the logically justified treatment of non-determinism and explicit failure in
\mathsf{s}\pi
. Our correct encoding of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
makes this influence precise by connecting terms and processes but also their associated intersection types and linear logic propositions.
We have also adopted features from previous resource
\lambda
-calculi, in particular those in
[
Bou93
,
BL00
,
PR10
]
.
Major similarities between
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and these calculi include:
as in
[
BL00
]
, our semantics performs lazy evaluation and linear substitution on the head variable;
as in
[
PR10
]
, our reductions lead to non-deterministic sums.
A distinctive feature of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is its lazy treatment of failures via the term
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
. In contrast, in
[
Bou93
,
BL00
]
there is no dedicated term to represent failure.
The non-collapsing semantics for non-determinism is another distinctive feature of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

Our design for
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
has been informed by the atomic
\lambda
-calculus introduced in
[
GHP13
]
.
Also, our translation from
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
(Def.
3.4
) borrows insights from translations given in
[
GHP13
]
.
The calculus
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is also loosely related to the
\lambda
-calculus with sharing in
[
GILL11
]
, which considers (idempotent) intersection types.
Notice that the calculi in
[
GHP13
,
GILL11
]
do not consider explicit failure nor non-determinism.
We distinguish between
well-typed
and
well-formed
expressions: this allows us to make fail-prone evaluation in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
explicit. It is interesting that explicit failures can be elegantly encoded as protocols in
\mathsf{s}\pi
—this way, we make the most out of
\mathsf{s}\pi
’s expressivity.

Bags in
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
have
linear
resources, which are used exactly once.
In recent work, we have defined an extension of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
in which bags contain both linear and
unrestricted
resources, as in
[
PR10
]
, and
established that our approach to encodability into
\mathsf{s}\pi
extends to such an enriched language
[
PNP21b
]
. This development requires the full typed process framework in
[
CP17
]
, with replicated processes and labeled choices (not needed to encode
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
).

Future Work

The approach and results developed here enable us to tackle open questions that go beyond the scope of this work. We comment on some of them:

•

It would be useful to investigate the
relative expressiveness
of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
with respect to other resource calculi, such as those in
[
BL00
,
PR10
]
. Derived
encodability (and non-encodability) results could potentially unlock transfer of reasoning techniques between different calculi.

•

Besides transfer of techniques, one application of encodings between sequential and concurrent calculi is in the design of functional concurrent languages with advanced features. In this respect, it should be feasible to develop a variant of Wadler’s GV
[
Wad12
]
with non-determinism, resources, explicit failure, and session communication by exploiting our correct encodings from
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to
\mathsf{s}\pi
.

•

It would be relevant to investigate
decidability properties
of the intersection type systems for
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and
\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
. Our translation is proven correct under the assumption that we consider only well-formed
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-terms. The type assignment problem for intersection type systems is, in general, undecidable
[
Lei83
]
; it would be interesting to consider decidable fragments of intersection type systems via, for instance, ranking restrictions
[
vB95
]
.

•

It would be insightful to establish
full abstraction
for our translation of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
.
We choose not to consider it because, as argued in
[
GN16
]
, full abstraction is not an informative criterion when it comes to an encoding’s quality. Establishing full abstraction requires developing the behavioral theory of
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and
\mathsf{s}\pi
, which is relevant and challenging in itself.

Appendix to

Proof A.1
.

By induction on the structure of
M
:

(1)

When
M=x
then we have
x{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}=N
and may derive the derivation of
\Gamma\vdash N:\tau
with
x\not\in\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)
. By taking
\Gamma_{1}=\emptyset
and
\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma
as
\Gamma=\emptyset\wedge\Gamma
the case follows as
\Gamma\vdash N:\tau
and

\mathtt{[T:var]}

x:\sigma\vdash x:\sigma

(2)

When
M=(M\ B)
then we have that
(M\ B){\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}=(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})\ B
. Let us consider two cases:

(I)

When
x\in\mathsf{fv}(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})

\Gamma^{\prime},x:\sigma^{k-1}\vdash M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}:\pi\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}

\Delta\vdash B:\pi

\mathtt{[T:app]}

(\Gamma^{\prime},x:\sigma^{k-1})\wedge\Delta\vdash(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})\ B:\tau^{\prime}

By the IH we have that
\Gamma^{\prime},x:\sigma^{k-1}\vdash M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}:\pi\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}
implies that
\exists\ \Gamma_{1}^{\prime},\Gamma_{2}
such that
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},x:\sigma^{k}\vdash M:\tau
, and
\Gamma_{2}\vdash N:\sigma
with
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\Gamma_{2}
.

\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},x:\sigma^{k}\vdash M:\pi\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}

\Delta\vdash B:\pi

\mathtt{[T:app]}

(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},x:\sigma^{k})\wedge\Delta\vdash M\ B:\tau^{\prime}

(II)

When
x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}:\pi\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}

\Delta\vdash B:\pi

\mathtt{[T:app]}

\Gamma^{\prime}\wedge\Delta\vdash(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})\ B:\tau^{\prime}

By the IH we have that
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}:\pi\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}
implies that
\exists\ \Gamma_{1}^{\prime},\Gamma_{2}
such that
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau
, and
\Gamma_{2}\vdash N:\sigma
with
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\Gamma_{2}
.

\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},x:\sigma\vdash M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}:\pi\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}

\Delta\vdash B:\pi

\mathtt{[T:app]}

(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},x:\sigma)\wedge\Delta\vdash M\ B:\tau^{\prime}

(3)

When
M=M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle
then we have that
(M\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle){\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}=(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle
where
x\not=y

(I)

When
x\in\mathsf{fv}(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})

\Gamma^{\prime},x:\sigma^{k-1},{y}:\delta^{j}\vdash(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}):\tau

\Delta\vdash B:\delta^{j}

\mathtt{[T:ex\text{-}sub]}

\Gamma^{\prime},{y}:\delta^{j}\wedge\Delta\vdash(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

By the IH we have that
\Gamma^{\prime},x:\sigma^{k-1},{y}:\delta^{j}\vdash(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}):\tau
implies that
\exists\ \Gamma_{1}^{\prime},\Gamma_{2}
such that
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},x:\sigma^{k},{y}:\delta^{j}\vdash M:\tau
, and
\Gamma_{2}\vdash N:\sigma
with
\Gamma^{\prime},{y}:\delta^{j}=(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},{y}:\delta^{j})\wedge\Gamma_{2}
.

\Gamma^{\prime}_{1},x:\sigma^{k},{y}:\delta^{j}\vdash M:\tau

\Delta\vdash B:\delta^{j}

\mathtt{[T:ex\text{-}sub]}

\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\Delta\vdash M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

(II)

When
x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})

\Gamma^{\prime},{y}:\delta^{k}\vdash(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}):\tau

\Delta\vdash B:\delta^{k}

\mathtt{[T:ex\text{-}sub]}

\Gamma^{\prime}\wedge\Delta\vdash(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

By the IH we have that
\Gamma^{\prime},{y}:\delta^{k}\vdash(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}):\tau
implies that
\exists\ \Gamma_{1}^{\prime},\Gamma_{2}
such that
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau
, and
\Gamma_{2}\vdash N:\sigma
with
\Gamma^{\prime},{y}:\delta^{k}=(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},{y}:\delta^{k})\wedge\Gamma_{2}
.

\Gamma^{\prime}_{1},x:\sigma,{y}:\delta^{k}\vdash M:\tau

\Delta\vdash B:\delta^{k}

\mathtt{[T:ex\text{-}sub]}

\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\Delta\vdash M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

(4)

When
M=\lambda y.M
then linear head substitution is undefined on this term as
\mathsf{head}(M)\not=x
.

(5)

When
M=\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
then
M
is not well typed.

Proof A.2
.

By induction on the reduction rule applied.
There are four possible cases.

(1)

When
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is reduced to via the Rule
\mathtt{[R:Beta]}

\mathtt{[R:Beta]}

(\lambda x.M)B\longrightarrow M\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

Then
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=M\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
can be type as follows:

{\Gamma,{x}:\sigma^{k}\vdash M:\tau}

\Delta\vdash B:\sigma^{k}

\mathtt{[T:ex\text{-}sub]}

{\Gamma\wedge\Delta\vdash M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau}

From the typing of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
we can deduce that
\mathbb{M}=(\lambda x.M)B
may be typed by:

{\Gamma,{x}:\sigma^{k}\vdash M:\tau}

\mathtt{[T:abs]}

\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.M:\sigma^{k}\rightarrow\tau

\Delta\vdash B:\pi

\mathtt{[T:app]}

{\Gamma\wedge\Delta\vdash(\lambda x.M)B:\tau}

(2)

When
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is reduced to via the Rule
\mathtt{[R:Fetch]}

\mathsf{head}(M)=x

B=\Lbag N_{1},\dots,N_{k}\Rbag\ ,\ k\geq 1

\#(x,M)=k

\mathtt{[R:Fetch]}

M\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow M{\{\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$(B\setminus\!\!\setminus N_{1})$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle+\cdots+M{\{\!|}N_{k}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$(B\setminus\!\!\setminus N_{k})$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

Let us consider two cases:

(I)

The bag
B
has
k
elements where
k>1
, then we type
M{\{\!|}N_{i}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$(B\setminus\!\!\setminus N_{i})$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
with the derivation
\Pi_{i}
to be:

\Gamma,{x}:\sigma^{k-1}\vdash M{\{\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\}}:\tau

\Delta\vdash(B\setminus\!\!\setminus N_{1}):\sigma^{k-1}

\mathtt{[T:ex\text{-}sub]}

\Gamma\wedge\Delta\vdash M{\{\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$(B\setminus\!\!\setminus N_{1})$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

We can type the sum with each derivation
\Pi_{i}
to be

\Pi_{1}

\Gamma\wedge\Delta\vdash M{\{\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$(B\setminus\!\!\setminus N_{1})$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

\Pi_{k}

\Gamma\wedge\Delta\vdash M{\{\!|}N_{k}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$(B\setminus\!\!\setminus N_{k})$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

\vdots

\Gamma\wedge\Delta\vdash M{\{\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$(B\setminus\!\!\setminus N_{1})$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle+\cdots+M{\{\!|}N_{k}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$(B\setminus\!\!\setminus N_{k})$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

By the anti-substitution lemma (Lemma
4
) we have that
\exists\ \Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
such that
\Gamma_{1},x:\sigma^{k}\vdash M:\tau
, and
\Gamma_{2}\vdash N_{i}:\sigma
with
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\wedge\Gamma_{2}
and finally we have:

\Gamma_{1},{x}:\sigma^{k}\vdash M:\tau

\Delta\wedge\Gamma_{2}\vdash B:\sigma^{k}

\mathtt{[T:ex\text{-}sub]}

\Gamma\wedge\Delta\vdash M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

notice that we make use that
\Gamma_{2}\vdash N_{i}:\sigma
to ensure that the bag
B
is well typed.

(II)

The bag
B
has one element, then we type
M{\{\!|}N_{i}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
with the derivation
\Pi
to be:

\Gamma\vdash M{\{\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\}}:\tau

\Delta\vdash\mathtt{1}:\omega

\mathtt{[T:ex\text{-}sub]}

\Gamma\wedge\Delta\vdash M{\{\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

By the anti-substitution lemma (Lemma
4
) we have that
\exists\ \Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
such that
\Gamma_{1},x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau
, and
\Gamma_{2}\vdash N_{1}:\sigma
with
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\wedge\Gamma_{2}
and finally we have:

\Gamma_{1},{x}:\sigma\vdash M:\tau

\Delta\wedge\Gamma_{2}\vdash\Lbag N_{1}\Rbag:\sigma

\mathtt{[T:ex\text{-}sub]}

\Gamma\wedge\Delta\vdash M{\{\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$N$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

(3)

When
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is reduced to via the Rule
\mathtt{[R:TCont]}

M\longrightarrow M^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+M^{\prime}_{k}

\mathtt{[R:TCont]}

C[M]\longrightarrow C[M^{\prime}_{1}]+\cdots+C[M^{\prime}_{k}]

Hence the proof follows by the IH on
M
.

(4)

When
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is reduced to via the Rule
\mathtt{[R:ECont]}

\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}^{\prime}

\mathtt{[R:ECont]}

D[\mathbb{M}]\longrightarrow D[\mathbb{M}^{\prime}]

Hence the proof follows by the IH on
M
.
